Human Ecology

Vol. 55, No. 3 · 2027-06 · 10 篇 · Springer · Ecological / environmental · Double-blind (verified)
  1. Assessment of Drinking Water Security and Waterborne Disease Prevalence in Imphal East and West Districts of Manipur, India
    Wazir Alam, Konthoujam Khelchandra Singh, Abhinandan Saikia, Kulendra Chandra Das · online 2026-10-19
  2. Breaking Down Barriers to Conservation Conflict Resolution in Sub-Saharan Africa: A Systematic Review
    Salma Khatibu, Joseph Kahimba · online 2026-10-22
    摘要

    The global commitment to expand protected areas (PAs) to cover 30% of Earth’s land and oceans by 2030 has sparked significant debate. Such expansions often displace local communities, particularly smallholder farmers and pastoralists in Sub-Saharan Africa (SSA), leading to lost livelihoods, food insecurity, and social conflicts. This systematic review, conducted via Google Scholar, JSTOR, and Dimensions, analyses 26 peer-reviewed studies from 2010–2024, guided by the PRISMA framework, to explore why conservation conflict resolution mechanisms in SSA are often ineffective. The findings reveal that while community-based conservation initiatives show promise in improving livelihoods, they frequently impose restrictions that exacerbate food insecurity and economic instability. The intensity of these impacts varies across SSA countries due to historical land tenure disputes, resource scarcity, non-participatory governance, and inadequate compensation for wildlife-related damage. The study concludes that effective conservation requires participatory governance, equitable benefit sharing, and the integration of local socioeconomic and cultural needs to balance ecological sustainability with social justice.

  3. Bridging Conservation and Human-Wildlife Conflict for Coexistence
    Dinesh Neupane, Bhagawan Raj Dahal, Prakash Chandra Aryal, Sunjeep Pun, Maheshwor Basnet, Abarta Pandey et al. · online 2026-10-17
    摘要

    Human-wildlife conflict (HWC) poses a significant threat to conservation, particularly in the region, where increasing wildlife populations intersect with dense human communities. This study examines the HWC scenario in one of the protected areas (Parsa National Park) of Terai Arc Landscape, where a growing population of large mammals shares the resources in highly populated buffer zone. Analyzing official relief claims from the past five years (2018/19–2022/23) as indicators of damage intensity, the study found that elephants were involved in 45% of conflicts, followed by leopards at 29% and tigers at 13.5%. Trend analysis revealed the increasing trend in herbivore-related damage while decreasing trend of carnivore-related conflict, despite the increase in tiger population within the study area. Human settlements displayed species-specific hotspots with no overlap, suggesting that species tend to avoid areas occupied by competitors and predators. Financial claims totaled NPR 9.47 million (~ USD 80,275; with an average of ~ NPR 2 million per year), predominantly for elephant-related incidents (55%). However, there was significant difference in relief claim amount among trophic groups (F 2, 448 = 4.308, p = 0.014). Statistical analysis showed that conflict intensity, expressed as relief claims, increased with greater distance from buffer zone forests (β = 0.0008, t = 2.6, p < 0.01) and significantly associated with tiger (β = 0.52, t = 3.8, p < 0.01). Additionally, distance from water sources has non-linear relationship with conflict, being low in the distance of 1.5km. These findings underscore the need for habitat management interventions, such as enhancing water availability within protected areas and maintaining corridors to connect the park's source populations with sink populations in degraded buffer zone and national forests. Implementing a field-based assessment process for wildlife-related damage could support human-wildlife coexistence in the region.

  4. From Herders’ Knowledge to Ecological Insight: Reindeer, Voles, and Food Caches in the Arctic
    Alexandra Terekhina, Alexander Volkovitskiy, Natalia Sokolova, Pavel Orekhov, Ivan Fufachev, Olga Krasheninnikova et al. · online 2026-10-17
  5. Greener Urban Environments, Greater Climate Anxiety? Pro-Environmental Behavior and Well-Being Across Levels of Green-Space Provision
    Chien-Wen Lo · online 2026-10-19
  6. How Changes in Elephant-Handling Practices Shape Human Perceptions of Their Wild Conspecifics
    Gilles Maurer, Olivier Gimenez, Baptiste Mulot, Nicolas Lescureux · online 2026-10-22
    摘要

    Opportunities for direct experience with wildlife shape human perceptions and behaviors, influencing human-environment relationships. These perceptions vary across communities according to lived experiences and the assessed costs and benefits of living with wildlife. In a changing global context, a “perception-practice loop” emerges, where shifts in local practices directly affect attitudes toward wildlife. This study uses a mixed-methods approach - combining qualitative and statistical analysis of semi-structured interviews - to examine how evolving elephant handling practices in Laos influence direct interactions with and perceptions of wild elephants. Our findings indicate that owners adapt their strategies to diverse socio-ecological contexts driven by recent global pressures. These practices, often grounded in reciprocity, shape perceptions and levels of tolerance toward wild individuals. The results indicate that tolerance depends less on aggressive elephant behavior and more on the perceived balance of costs and benefits. While wild elephants impose psychological or delayed burdens, they also provide tangible and symbolic benefits, such as mating opportunities and conservation value. We conclude that fostering reciprocal relationships and integrating traditional knowledge can enhance human-wildlife coexistence, supporting both biodiversity and local well-being.

  7. How Indigenous Socio-Cultural Systems Resist, Accept, or Direct Ecological Transformation: A Research Agenda
    Tony W. Ciocco, Gregor W. Schuurman, Brian W. Miller, Stefan Tangen · online 2026-10-22
    摘要

    Anthropogenic ecological transformations are challenging conventional environmental management paradigms, as maintaining historical ecological structures and functions becomes increasingly untenable. The resist–accept–direct (RAD) framework offers a strategic tool to navigate these shifts. However, as a product of the dominant socio-cultural system, the RAD framework risks perpetuating the same patterns of thinking that have contributed to unsustainable social-ecological dynamics. Indigenous socio-cultural systems, considered here through the lens of long-term coevolution with their environments, offer valuable empirical models for adaptive responses to environmental change. Such Indigenous socio-cultural responses, grounded in narratives, governance institutions, value systems, and other cultural elements collectively referred to as Indigenous Knowledge (IK), hold relevance for addressing today’s intertwined environmental and social challenges. We provide a targeted review of existing literature at the intersection of IK and the RAD framework, along with a broader conceptual overview of relevant insights across related fields. These reveal contrasting assumptions about Indigenous responses to environmental change, such as consistently resisting to preserve cultural fidelity or uniformly accepting, reflecting deeper cultural, temporal, and ecological complexity. We propose a structured research agenda to investigate Indigenous socio-cultural responses to environmental change through empirical research, theoretical synthesis, hypothesis development, and practical application. This agenda is grounded in comprehensive collaboration, emphasizing shared power and integrating both emic and etic perspectives throughout all stages of research and implementation. By bridging the adaptive dynamics of coevolved social-ecological systems and mainstream management strategies, an IK–RAD framework could enhance the efficacy of responses to global environmental change.

  9. Hydrological Vulnerability and Degradation of Mountain Springs in Tehri, Uttarakhand Himalaya: Systematic Review of Drivers, Impacts, and Adaptation Pathways (2015–2025)
    Amit Bora, Binita Giri, Pallavi Upreti · online 2026-10-27
  10. River Crossings at the Yangtze’s Headwaters: Mapping Hydrosocial Mobility Infrastructures across the Sino-Tibetan Borderlands
    Monia Chies · online 2026-10-22
    摘要

    This article investigates river crossing spaces and practices as key features of past and present human-river relationships along the fluvial landscapes of today’s Three-River-Source (TRS or sanjiangyuan ) region of the Qinghai Tibetan Plateau (QTP), which includes the sources of the Yellow, Yangtze, and Mekong Rivers. Through an ethnographically informed analysis of Western travel literature about the Sino-Tibetan borderlands (1850–1950), this study contributes to the environmental history of the QTP by mapping the main pre-1950s river crossings within the Yangtze’s headwaters region and examining how they inform contemporary state-led infrastructural and heritagization processes. This essay draws on critical infrastructure and hydrosocial theory to present river crossings as “hydrosocial mobility infrastructures” (HMIs) enabling connectivity within the wider system of historical trade routes linking China and Tibet in the headwaters region. The HMI framework highlights the ecological and relational character of pre-1950s river crossings, which are shaped by human and non-human interactions, local ecological knowledge, overlapping authorities, and frontier geopolitics, as well as by hydrological, geomorphic, and seasonal changes.

Culture, Medicine and Psychiatry

Vol. 50, No. 4 · 2026-12 · 14 篇 · Springer · Medical / psychological · Double-blind (verified)
  1. A Fire and Altadena: Biographical Diffraction and Climate Disaster Loss
    Elizabeth McKibben · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    This paper develops a conceptualization of biographical diffraction via a creative autoethnography of climate disaster grief. In January 2025, the Eaton Fire devastated Altadena, California killing 19 people and destroying over 9,000 structures. Among them was my family home. Having lived abroad in Aotearoa New Zealand for 5 years, I return to Altadena after the fire, navigating loss from sites of distance and proximity. Drawing upon Barad’s concept of diffraction and the void, I grapple with the asymmetries of grief, privilege, belonging, and self that emerge with the loss of “home.” To do so, I craft a narrative with audio recordings of my footsteps walking around the debris of the Eaton Fire burn scar. By weaving together sensory vignettes, evocative writing, images, and reflections I consider how climate disaster grief is an embodied and emplaced phenomenon both historically entangled and unequally distributed. I then draw upon cross-cultural learnings to explore how Indigenous knowledge can, and cannot, inform healing of place and self from positions of settler-colonialism. Biographical diffraction is thus presented as a tool for attending to the layered emotional, material, and political dimensions of climate disaster, particularly when “home” becomes an unrecognizable site of trauma, memory, and transformation.

    Wolfgang Mastnak · online 2026-09-03
  3. Flexible Interview for ICD-11 (FLII-11): Cultural Adaptation and Translation for India
    T. S. Jaisoorya, Girish N. Rao, Geoffrey M. Reed, Lakshmi Jogi, Karen T. Maré, Ajit B. Dahale et al. · online 2026-09-03
  4. From Moral Ambiguity to Moral Injury in Nursing Academia: An Autoethnographic Account of Moral Distress and Institutional Life
    Patricia Camillo · online 2026-09-03
  5. From ‘Sensory’ to ‘Anxiety’: Embodied States, Relational Care, and Ethical Transformation in Parental Care for U.S. Neurodivergent Childhood
    Elsa Davidson · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    This paper draws on an ethnography of care for social–emotional differences in middle-class North American childhood to explore the relation between care practices employed by parents of neurodivergent, or neuroatypical, children, and emergent ethics of care. Focusing on a single case of asymmetrical care, that between a mother and a neurodivergent child, I draw on video-recorded interviews as well other ethnographic material to highlight how practices of care emphasizing forms of bodily and emotional connection, and labors of attunement to embodied states and forms of expression, enable shifts in the caretaker’s perception of, and affective orientation toward, embodied states. In doing so, I show how moments of partially shared feeling across bodies (interembodiment) and intercorporeal connections can engender the parent–caretaker’s perception of enhanced intersubjective understanding, and authentic insight into the child’s embodied experience, particularly with regard to anxious and excited states. As such, I describe the caretaker’s perceptual shift from an individualizing evaluation of her child’s “sensory issues” to a relational understanding of “anxiety” as a collaboratively engendered embodied emotional state that can be altered, felt, shared, and alleviated through non-verbal tactile communication. In turn, I argue that it can be seen as a “gift” of ethical self-transformation that has implications for the care of neurodivergent childhood. In doing so, I suggest that such insight engenders care for a neurodivergent “other” that may come closer to preserving the other’s singularity. Moreover, I argue that such insight rejects a stereotyped understanding of embodied neurodivergent selfhood as bounded, brain-based, and impermeable.

  6. Healing Minds Through Education: Cultural Pathways to Medical and Health Humanities in Post-conflict Iraq
    Bushra Juhi Jani, Luma K. Mohammed, Nawal Juhi Jani · online 2026-09-03
  7. Liberation Medicine in Authoritarian Contexts: The Brutality of U.S. Mass Incarceration
    Philippe Bourgois · online 2026-09-26
    摘要

    This article engages Führer and Vorhölter’s (2025) reinvigoration of Nancy Scheper–Hughes’s utopian call for liberation medicine in their special issue of Culture, Medicine, and Psychiatry . They offer a practical reconceptualization of liberation medicine as “concrete utopias” in the “medical undercommons”. Inspired by Scheper–Hughes’s humble call for “good-enough” ethnographic engagement in her documentation of a successful solidarity movement of single-mother agricultural laborers in Northeastern Brazil, I highlight ethnographically a (fragile) concrete utopian initiative: a peer-support program for inmates with psychosis spectrum conditions who are swept into the Los Angeles County Jail system’s overcrowded mental health units on a daily basis by routinized law enforcement sweeps of homeless individuals. Developed by former gang members (without mental illness) facing capital punishment charges, this remarkable initiative of solidarity, redemption, and anti-racism through radical caregiving in a claustrophobic carceral environment is carving out a temporarily safe space free from abuse and violence. The larger facility remains gang-controlled and is overwhelmed by what colleagues and I have been calling “predatory accumulation” and “necrogovernance” processes (Hart et al., 2024, 2025). The inmates are powerless, caught within the “carceral mesh” of U.S. political-economic corporate and opportunistic special interests. Furthermore, the discretionary power of “hyperpunitive” U.S. judges allows them to routinely impose Life Without Parole (LWOP) sentences on redeemed inmates incarcerated as adolescents. Inspired by Scheper–Hughes’s practical political optimism coupled with her love for ethnography and political activism, I end with eight practical suggestions for specific state bureaucratic reforms to foment public transparency about the useless cruelty of mass incarceration and the global rise of authoritarianisms in our perilous moment in global history.

    Seth M. Holmes · online 2026-09-03

    Armaan A. Rowther, Rakhshanda Liaqat, Hadia Maryam, Faareha Siddiqui, Abid Malik, Najia Atif et al. · online 2026-09-03
  11. Witnessing the Theatre of Self-Harm Among South Asian Women: Expression, Performance, and Hot Tea
    Anamika Shrimali · online 2026-09-03
  12. Working in Pairs: The Dialogic Distribution of Clinical Responsibility
    Lauren Cubellis · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    At an innovative site of psychiatric crisis care work, dialogically trained clinicians in Berlin, Germany transformed the skills learned from the Open Dialogue approach into a means for building solidarity in the face of precarious health structures. Learning to sit with unknowing and tolerate uncertainty, at first developed as techniques to support clients in crisis, became a way by which these clinicians navigated a shifting and unsteady health insurance landscape. In the face of budgetary restrictions and increased oversight by health insurance companies, which challenged the terms of their work and ethical commitments, they supported each other by reconfiguring the terms of clinical responsibility: they dispersed the authoritative gaze most often cultivated in institutional psychiatry and risk management approaches, and developed a means for attending to the uncertainty of crisis by sharing the burden of unknowing with each other. They did this through their unwavering commitment to working in pairs.

  13. “I Really Feel Heard by AI”: A Cultural Case Study of AI-Mediated Listening Across China and the UK
    Xin Zhan · online 2026-09-02
    摘要

    The increasing use of generative AI for emotional support has prompted growing debate about the future of mental health care. This article suggests that such reliance is better understood not simply as a technological shift, but as symptomatic of a wider crisis in the politics of listening. This cultural case study examines the experiences of Lily, a 24-year-old Chinese migrant woman navigating emotional distress across uneven care infrastructures in China and the UK. Drawing on person-centred ethnography, it shows how AI chatbots come to function as provisional sites of care under conditions of precarity, gendered obligation, and moralised endurance. The analysis conceptualises these chatbot interactions as a care-patch : a temporary form of digital holding that emerges where human listening is scarce, delayed, or experienced as burdensome. Rather than treating AI use as a matter of technological adoption, the case situates AI-mediated listening within culturally specific moral relations of obligation and responsibility. In doing so, it reframes therapeutic automation as an index of the erosion of listening as a shared social good and redirects attention to the political and ethical challenge of rebuilding infrastructures of human listening capable of absorbing distress without extracting it, outsourcing it, or returning it as blame.

  14. …And Breathe! Wellness, the Nation, and Holistic Reproductive Practitioners in India
    Adhvik Shetty · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    Garbh Sanskar (GS) is an Indian holistic reproductive programme that claims roots in ancient Ayurvedic and yogic traditions while drawing upon contemporary wellness practices and international health policy. GS centres offer breathwork, guided meditation, and counselling to expectant and hopeful couples. This article draws upon interviews with GS practitioners across nine Indian cities to examine how these practitioners use wellness discourses to articulate strategies of individual and national improvement. I argue that GS practitioners seek to interweave soteriological and wellness logics to produce enriched reproductive subjects for a therapeutic vision of nation-building. This project uses wellness to assuage the anxieties of modernity, suggest civilisational exceptionalism, and articulate aspirations for the nation. Together, GS illustrates the emergence of a bio-spiritual therapeutic politics where wellness is part of reproductive governance.

    Shamo Thar · online 2026-09-21
    摘要

    How do languages move with their speakers across changing social landscapes?In Amdo Lullaby, linguistic anthropologist Shannon Ward meticulously examines language shift and language socialization among Tibetan children in Amdo, a region within the broader Tibetosphere.Rich in excerpts from everyday conversational interactions, the book invites readers to vividly experience the linguistic practices of children who speak the farmers' variety of Amdo Tibetan (yul skad, originally meaning "local speech") in both rural and urban communities of Qinghai Province.Across five sequenced chapters, Ward situates her study in Tsachen, a small village in Amdo Tibet, and Xining, the provincial capital and an urban center of Tibetan cultural production and religious education.Ward skillfully weaves together ethnographic and linguistic analyses to demonstrate the role and agency of Tibetan children in sustaining cultural and linguistic continuity through multilingual practices.As families in Amdo increasingly migrate to urban centers, many Tibetan children experience a shift away from their mother tongue as they navigate Mandarin-dominant educational environments.Rather than simply framing the phenomenon as language loss, Ward compellingly conceptualizes it as language shift, illustrating the diversity and vitality of Amdo Tibetan yul skad (place-based language).Through children's multilingual practices, yul skad is shown to be deeply associated with land, place, kinship, and even emotions such as love and compassion.The author also urges readers to reconsider standard language ideologies that elevate literary Tibetan as the "correct" form and discourage mixing of Tibetan and Mandarin.These language ideologies reinforce children's linguistic shift toward the dominant language by creating social and educational pressures that devalue bilingual or mixed language practices.In Chapter 1, Lhamo and Drolma, ages three and five, engage in a traveling game in which they navigate language choices and varieties, particularly between Farmer Talk (Tib.rong skad) and Nomad Talk (Tib.'drog skad).Despite the dominant language ideologies of adults like Akha Norbu, the children's uncle, who uphold Nomad Talk as an ideal, pure standard language (Tib.pha skad gtsang ma) untouched by language contact, rural children use language to shape their identities and reproduce meaningful social distinctions by code-switching between different Amdo Tibetan yul skad.Chapter 2 examines how Tibetan children in rural Tsachen use   3. Coming of Age as Syrian Jewish Mexicans: The Pragmatics of Diaspora By Evelyn MaríaDean‐Olmsted, Leiden: Brill. 2026. 257 pages
    Sarah Leiter · online 2026-10-20
    摘要

    Amidst a contemporary global political climate in which "Arab" and "Jew" are so often assumed to be antonyms, how do some people identify as both simultaneously?And when their identification practices encompass yet more socially perceived contradictions, like being both "Jewish" and "Mexican," how do they assert the validity of claiming all of these identities together and at once?How do these identification practices occur through and become apparent in their language use?These are the questions that Evelyn María Dean-Olmsted sets out to answer in her linguistic ethnography Coming of Age as Syrian Jewish Mexicans: The Pragmatics of Diaspora.With its focus on the multi-chronotopic discourse of Arab Jewish Mexicans, the book is poised to contribute to linguistic anthropological scholarship that examines how transnational social identification takes shape in and through everyday interaction.Specifically, it situates individuals' linguistic choices within the sociohistorical and political contexts that render them indexically meaningful, showing readers how speakers draw on those contexts to position themselves within and in relation to them.Through this focus on language use, the author demonstrates the creative agency involved not only in communicative practices but also in modern religiosity.Contrary to popular notions of "religious" individuals blindly following directives, those profiled in the book are engaged in a constant process of conscious negotiation in relation to their religiosity.Attending to the linguistic resources they draw on illuminates that process and helps to reorient popular ideas about contemporary religiosity.Because the book sits at the intersection between linguistic anthropology and the anthropology of religion, it is relevant to scholars in each area.It is particularly relevant to students, as the author intentionally presents the theoretical concepts she uses-and makes the case for the usefulness of linguistic anthropology-with a nonspecialist audience in mind.Though some familiarity with disciplinary concepts is probably necessary for full comprehension of the text, the book is appropriate for readers who want to learn how identities rooted in multiple places and social collectivities at once emerge through ordinary interactions.Dean-Olmsted's interlocutors are young adults in Mexico City who belong to the locally predominant Halebi (Aleppan descendant) or Shami (Damascene/Beiruti descendant) Jewish communities.For those involved in the study, all three of the identification categories the author   4. Embodied semiotics of risk and protection: Ritual silence, onomatopoeia, and role switching in the Kannarekō lightning rite of northern Kagoshima
    Masayuki Owatari · online 2026-09-07
    摘要

    This paper analyzes the Kannarekō (雷講, “lightning rite”) of northern Kagoshima, a minimal speech ritual built from silence, ideophonic vocalization, and indexical role assignment rather than propositional language. A rotating thunder‐caller vocalizes goro‐goro‐goro / pikka / dōn as two gohei circulate the circle in opposite directions; at dōn , both holders drink sake , but only the fire‐ gohei holder becomes the next thunder‐caller. I propose ideophonic enactment as a third, performative mode of ideophones, in which sound‐iconic form constitutes rather than depicts its referent, and argue that this minimal speech structure confers transmission resilience amid demographic contraction.

  5. From Small Talk to Microaggression: A History of Scale By MichaelLempert, Chicago: University of Chicago Press. 2024. 300 pages
    Brendan H. O'Connor · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    Like Michael Lempert, I was trained to revere the small, to regard Ray Birdwhistell's Microcultural Incidents in Ten Zoos as a sacred text, to transcribe conversational pauses to the nearest tenth of a second and document each laughter pulse faithfully.Lempert and I weren't trained by the same people, but we have many common ancestors in our family tree.(I remember a certain graduate school professor's delight at learning that I'd been made to read Edward T. Hall as an undergraduate.)Lempert's book, which promises a history of scale, is more properly described as a history of the micro, as the most contentious and seductive pole of the scalar continuum.It is, importantly, not just a history of what was once called "microanalysis," though that figures into it.Rather, from an anthropological perspective, Lempert attempts to account for what ideas about the "micro" have done culturally in a range of social fields.To take the obvious example from his title: the point of talking about microaggressions, for feminists or critical race scholars, is that they aren't "micro" at all; their seeming smallness conceals their widereaching social effects (or so the argument goes).From Small Talk to Microaggression presents a historical perspective on the ways that perceived smallness has been taken up as a problem (or opposed to the "bigger" interpretive pretensions of the social sciences) as well as a panacea, or a means of grounding these "larger" interpretive claims empirically.It's timely, but Lempert asserts that it's always timely; these scalar tensions are inescapable and recur in ways that are sometimes surprising and sometimes predictable.In light of the contemporary crisis of replicability in the quantitative social sciences, for example, Lempert would invite us to see early attempts to "ontologize" the social (through microscopy, or principled attention to "small" details) as an articulation of similar anxieties about where to locate social life.Closer to home, if philosophers and their allies accuse anthropologists of pursuing "political acceptability" at the expense of "disinterested inquiry" (Vanderbilt Report, 2026), might not anthropologists be tempted to "go micro" and ground our findings in the ever more "microscopic" details of culture, per Geertz?Recursivity aside, the book's signal contribution is Lempert's impressive genealogical work of showing that what linguists and anthropologists might have thought "belonged" to us-the consistent tension between macro and micro in our methods and emphases-is, in fact, a historical development that profoundly shaped other disciplines and political programs in the twentieth century.Moreover, interaction analysts have often been in contact

  6. Herder in the lab: Maximizing “cultural density” in multilingual training data for an open‐science AI project
    Joseph Wilson · online 2026-09-06
    摘要

    This paper provides ethnographic analysis of interactions between volunteers in an open‐science AI project entrusted with preparing multilingual text for use in fine‐tuning a pretrained LLM. Volunteers were encouraged to entextualize translated text into structured corpora and to write and edit prompt‐completion pairings in over 100 languages. This drive to capture linguistic diversity often conflicted with the need to organize data sets into standardized categories to effectively fine‐tune the LLM. Volunteers purified text annotations by judging what constituted normatively correct syntax, punctuation, spelling, and what kinds of content best represented the “essence” of their culture. Certain forms of language were described as “culturally dense,” suggesting an underlying linguistic ideology aligning with Herder's Romantic notions of cultural relativism.

  7. Moral Enregisterment: Dialect, naming, and affirmative satire in Saudi television comedy
    Yousef Al‐Rojaie, Albatool Alyahya · online 2026-10-16
    摘要

    This article introduces moral enregisterment: how dialects acquire moral value, not merely social indexicality, through mediated performance. Analyzing Tash Ma Tash (1993–2011), Saudi Arabia's landmark sketch series, we show how Urban Najdi indexes bureaucratic rationality, Urban Hijazi indices affective civility, and Southern varieties are enregistered as authentic yet institutionally marginal. Naming initiates this process: character names prime moral expectations before speech begins, one mechanism through which moral enregisterment operates. Through affirmative satire, the series grants marginalized voices emotional visibility while withholding institutional authority. This study extends enregisterment theory from social typification to the production of evaluative stance.

  8. Mother Tongue Prestige: The Sociolinguistics of Privilege in Urban Middle‐Class Education in India By Jessica SujataChandras, London and New York: Routledge. 2023. 216 pages
    Deepika Kumari Meena · online 2026-10-06
    摘要

    Jessica Sujata Chandras's Mother Tongue Prestige offers a theoretically rich and ethnographically grounded account of how language becomes a site through which caste, class, gender, and educational aspiration are organized.The central argument is that language cannot be treated as a neutral medium of communication.It is a form of social and cultural power that shapes educational mobility, reproduces privilege, and creates new forms of distinction.Through the case of Marathi, a regional language spoken in Pune, Maharashtra, especially debates around the mother tongue and the idea of shuddh, or "pure," Marathi, Chandras shows how linguistic value is produced through language history, institutional practice, and everyday language aspiration.The study is structured through a grammatical metaphor, with two sections titled "Nouns" and "Verbs."The first section establishes the conceptual and historical foundation by examining the political economy of language, language ideology, aspirational mother-tongue prestige, and the cultural compensation attached to linguistic identity.It demonstrates that English education in India did not emerge as a neutral educational choice.Rather, it became closely linked to caste and class location, particularly among groups that already possessed social and cultural capital.English therefore added another layer to existing hierarchies while presenting itself as a language of merit, mobility, and modernity.A key strength of the analysis lies in its ability to connect students, teachers, and parents within a single field of linguistic aspiration.This enables Chandras to show that language ideology is not produced in one location alone.It circulates across schools, homes, peer groups, and intergenerational language expectations.The framework of "aspirational prestige" is especially useful because it explains how desires for mobility are expressed through language choices, accents, medium of instruction, and claims over linguistic purity.The study therefore contributes to debates on how elite groups secure access to Englishmedium education while also claiming cultural authority over the mother tongue.The historical transformation of the mother tongue is central to this argument.The mother tongue debate gradually became tied to prestige for those groups that already had access to linguistic capital.As   9. Reclaiming a Basque youth voice in Navarre: The Iruinkokoa carnival as an agentive chronotope
    Agurtzane Elordui, Iñaki Sagardoi‐Leuza, Samara Velte · online 2026-10-09
    摘要

    This article analyzes Iruinkokoa , a contemporary Basque carnival in Pamplona‐Iruñea, as an example of emerging forms of minority‐language activism grounded in carnivalesque critique and performative intervention. Based on ethnographic fieldwork conducted between 2023 and 2025, the study examines how parody, satire, and collective performance allow young Basque speakers to contest the sociolinguistic marginalization of Basque in Navarre. Moving beyond approaches that treat context as a passive backdrop, the article adopts Blommaert's reinterpretation of Bakhtin's concept of the chronotope to conceptualize context as historically sedimented, socially active, and productive of meaning and action. From this perspective, the article proposes the notion of the “agentive chronotope” to explain how the carnivalesque framework of Iruinkokoa creates the conditions for collective agency. The study argues that carnival temporarily reconfigures dominant indexical meanings surrounding the Basque language and identity while enabling alternative forms of political voice and collective belonging.

  10. Resisting language shift: Scaling and rescaling Circassian and Russian across urban and rural Adygea
    Valeriya Minakova · online 2026-10-27
    摘要

    This article employs scalar analysis to explore the language practices of Circassians in Adygea, a republic in Russia situated on the traditional lands of the Indigenous Circassian people. Paying attention to language ideological assemblages in urban and rural areas, I examine how Circassians navigate and attempt to reconfigure linguistic hierarchies across the urban–rural continuum in response to the ongoing language shift to Russian. The paper questions the analytical usefulness of the urban–rural dichotomy, which may be diminishing as communities increasingly engage with digital media and online platforms, where content in the ancestral language remains limited. It also suggests possibilities for rescaling a minoritized language away from its association with bounded rural settings and toward its value as a resource for connecting with transnational diaspora communities.

  11. The enregisterment and social meaning of fresas on the Texas border
    Natalie Rangel · online 2026-10-12
    摘要

    The fresa characterological figure is widely recognizable in Mexico and has been considered to be performable through a variety of semiotic markers. By viewing fresas as a meta‐sign, their performance consists of three main behavioral practices: linguistic behavior, physical appearance, and inhabited space. The present study explores the presence of fresas in the Texas borderlands and investigates their social meaning and their relationship to certain geographical and social spaces. The present study supports how the construction of the fresa persona not only depends on the co‐occurrence of linguistic behaviors, physical appearance, and inhabited spaces but it also depends on how Laredoans move through, inhabit, and position themselves within different socially meaningful places. These findings demonstrate that geographical and social places should not be understood merely as one indexical cue among many but rather place organizes the various semiotic resources through which the fresa persona becomes recognizable and performable. Thus, in the context of the Texas–Mexico border, this study shows how the circulation of the fresa persona across these national contexts depends on their local reconfiguration through meaningful places rather than their straightforward reproduction.

  12. The keys to the garden: Capital, class, and the repurposing of Persian mystical poetry in Los Angeles
    Zia Khoshsirat · online 2026-09-28
    摘要

    This article traces the social life of medieval Persian poetry as it is repurposed within Los Angeles's Iranian diaspora. Based on 5 years of immersive ethnography in private erfān classes and mystical assemblies, I argue these gatherings function as contemporary diasporic courts, mobilizing core texts within a spiritual marketplace structured by economic exclusion. While historical scholarship has shown how Persian poetry negotiated status in medieval courts, and anthropological work has grounded these traditions in social life, this study offers the first analysis of how diasporic Iranians monetize this heritage to redraw ethnolinguistic and class boundaries from within. Synthesizing Bourdieu's model of capital conversion with theories of diasporic temporality and the semiotic politics of language, I trace a dual process. Externally, participants leverage poetic mastery to convert economic capital into compound capital of refined civility, positioning the community within Los Angeles's racialized landscape. Internally, through ritualized performances of interpretation and an ideology of pre‐Islamic purity, they forge class boundaries and authenticate a distinct diasporic identity. Ultimately, it reveals how spiritual tradition enables the interconversion of capital through the performance of a purified past.

  13. The return of the Bee Sultan: Vibrant encounters of environmentalism and language reclamation in Turkey's Hemshin lands
    Neşe Kaya‐Özkan · online 2026-09-06
    摘要

    This article examines how language and environment become constitutive through the emergent environmentalism of Hemshin youth returning to Turkey's Hemshin lands from major cities. As development transforms the landscape, young Hemshins engage in “reading” their lands, encountering linguistic and material traces like stories, place names, and beehive platforms that orient them toward past lifeways shaping their environmentalism. These “vibrant encounters” provide resources for environmental activism while reanimating connections to Hemshin language, knowledges, and identity long disrupted by Turkish nationalism, outmigration, and economic changes.   14. Transposition as activism: Toward a public‐facing linguistic anthropology
    Mike Mena, Britta Ingebretson · online 2026-09-08
    摘要

    In 2023, the Demystifying Language Project (DLP) held a 3‐day writing workshop that brought together 12 scholars, paired with 11 undergraduates and 15 high schoolers, to collaboratively recompose their own peer‐reviewed articles into a genre legible to those without specialized training in language‐related disciplines. This was done through transposition as activism , or the process by which participants undermine, transcend, or defect from highly regimented boundaries of academic writing genres and their entailed participant roles. In particular, transposition as activism seeks to dismantle the hierarchical arrangement of conventionalized “expert/non‐expert” academic positionalities (i.e., author/reader, professor/student, instructor/learner, etc.) and their assumed reading/writing practices (i.e., the consumption of academic/non‐academic genres, the use of academic/everyday language). In this article, we offer transposition as an activist approach to confront the raciolinguistic hierarchies endemic to knowledge production. As of 2026, there are 12 transposed academic publications on the Demystifying Language Project website.

  2. The politics of (post-)truth: Anthropological knowledge-making and new normative challenges
    Heike Drotbohm, Olaf Zenker · pp. 622-631
    摘要

    The introduction to this special section explores the contemporary politics of (post-)truth as both an object of anthropological inquiry and a challenge to anthropological knowledge-making itself. Rather than treating post-truth as a simple erosion of facts, we examine how contemporary conflicts increasingly concern the conditions under which truth claims are validated, contested, and rendered authoritative. Drawing on debates in anthropology, science and technology studies, and post-positivist scholarship, we revisit long-standing concerns with epistemic plurality, incommensurability, collaboration, and the social production of knowledge. We argue that the current conjuncture is marked less by the existence of competing truths than by intensified struggles over the infrastructures, procedures, and normative frameworks that make truth claims intelligible and actionable. Introducing the contributions to this special section, we propose the concepts of “good-enough truths” and “minimal commensurability” as analytical tools for navigating tensions between epistemic openness, normative accountability, and democratic contestation under increasingly polarized conditions.

  3. Awkward alignments: Towards empathetic skepticism and agonistic confrontation in anthropological knowledge-making
    Mario Krämer, Olaf Zenker · pp. 632-646
    摘要

    Anthropological knowledge is increasingly contested in our postliberal world, both from within and outside the discipline. Against this backdrop, this article addresses two interrelated questions: first, how should we methodologically address this challenge of contested anthropological knowledge-making and, second, what should be anthropology’s political approach in the (post-)truth era? We argue that anthropology best handles these conundrums through a research orientation we describe as aiming for “awkward alignments.” In awkward alignments, anthropologists engage their interlocutors with an ethico-onto-epistemological attitude of “empathetic skepticism,” attempting to understand how people think and act on the basis of their own experiences with similar situations while at the same time systematically questioning all knowledge claims. While suggesting that anthropologists should practice empathetic skepticism across the full spectrum of their research encounters, they should simultaneously not shy away, politically, from an “agonistic confrontation” when engaging “normative others” during and beyond their research.

  4. Walking the tightrope: Public ethnography, the far right, and the dilemma of sustaining ideologically opposed relationships
    Rosana Pinheiro-Machado · pp. 647-662
    摘要

    This article examines the ethical and methodological challenges and possibilities of conducting public ethnography and studying far-right voters in precarious settings. Drawing on twenty-six years of longitudinal research in Brazil, I reflect on how long-standing relationships with interlocutors—many of whom have adopted authoritarian views over time—can be sustained amid deep political disagreement and contested truths. As a public scholar, I examine how visibility and transparency have reshaped both the ethnographic encounter and ethnographic authority, making politics an unavoidable dimension of fieldwork. The analysis foregrounds the emotional and political tightrope walked by ethnographers who must balance empathy, nuance, and critique. The paper shows how mutual interests, long-term trust, and agonistic intimacy make it possible to build understanding without endorsement. Ultimately, I argue that public ethnography and studying ideological opponents produce an irresolvable contradiction best met through transparency, reflexive limits, and an acceptance of coeval change and contested truths.

  5. On researching “pseudolaw” in sovereignist movements
    Anna Löbbert · pp. 663-678
    摘要

    The so-called “conspiratorial sovereignism” milieu in Germany is made up of groups that contest the authority of the state through ritualized interactions inspired by conspiratorial legal claims. Drawing on one year of ethnographic fieldwork and online observation, I analyze how these groups enact alternative sovereignties and legalities. Analytically separating the different ways in which states can be understood as fantasies through the dimensions of the imagined, imaginary, and un/acceptable, I suggest that anthropological frameworks that view the state as a social fantasy provide important insights into these performances, but risk normalizing and romanticizing the movement’s deeply hierarchical and exclusionary ideologies. The article calls for critical reflection on implicit normative biases in the scholarship, emphasizing the need to understand sovereignism’s political dangers beyond hegemonic/marginal divides.

  7. Red-pilled: Memetic truthiness, sticky stories, and anthropology’s unruly heritage of epistemic contingency
    Konstanze N’Guessan · pp. 693-707
    摘要

    This article contributes to anthropological scholarship on the politics of (post-)truth by linking anthropology’s legacy of epistemic contingency to the memetic truth practices of far-right digital culture. Drawing on fourteen months of digital ethnography among German far-right activists on Discord, 4chan, Twitch, and X (2021–2023), I analyze “red-pill stories” as performances of truthiness—affective narratives of awakening that consolidate community through stickiness rather than factual verification. Reading these stories as “deep stories” in Hochschild’s sense, I show that red-pilling is a form of secondary enculturation. In the second part of the article, I use my field encounters with a would-be red-piller as a lens to examine how far-right actors exploit anthropological concepts—epistemic contingency, partial truths, ontological difference—to demand legitimacy, thereby forcing anthropologists to confront the unruly heritage of their own epistemology.

  8. Obstinate truths: Eigensinn , epistemic contrarianism and the outer edges of anthropological knowledge-making
    Annika Lems · pp. 708-722
    摘要

    This article examines a central epistemological dilemma of the post-truth moment: how anthropology can remain committed to grounding knowledge in vernacular meaning-making when everyday understandings of the world increasingly draw on exclusionary ideas. Reflecting on unsettling theories about climate change and the environment encountered in my home village in the southern Austrian Alps, an area affected by rapid ecological change, I explore what happens when ethnographic attention reaches the outer edges of anthropological knowledge production. Drawing on Alf Lüdtke and Antonio Gramsci—two theorists who probed such edges from within their own historical conjunctures—I argue that taking seriously eigensinnige (obstinate) modes of everyday sense-making about environmental change offers a crucial analytical opening. Attending to these antagonistic truth claims not only illuminates the fraught relationship between vernacular and normative forms of knowledge-making but also compels us to rethink the nature of the everyday itself.

  9. A new paradigm in the American Anthropological Association: Neo-Racial Anthropology
    David Stoll · pp. 723-737
    摘要

    Is the most critical issue facing anthropology departments their whiteness? In an address to the American Anthropological Association, then-president Akhil Gupta announced a “racial reckoning” and “decolonizing project.” Whiteness, white space, or white-norming is how liberal antiracist anthropology continues to harbor racism, Gupta and other critical theorists hold. They further argue that the racial and political identity of anthropologists—either as voices of marginalized groups or allies of such groups—should determine the future of our field. This is a new paradigm, I argue, and my term for it is Neo-Racial Anthropology. Neo-racialism grows out of increasing skepticism about the value of crossing sociocultural divides to do ethnographic research. Ethnographic research also has been undermined by the presumed moral authority of the oppressed. Once neo-racialists add the assumption that white hegemony is the key underlying issue in most situations, an anthropologist’s racial identity becomes a key determinant of what they judge to be the best new research. This is the logic that turns neo-racialists into racial reductionists. If other anthropologists acquiesce to this kind of thinking, an ethnography-based interpretive social science could ossify into an academic priesthood.

  10. When is hoarding?: Temporal regimes, material time, and the domestic life of accumulations in Japan
    Fabio Gygi · pp. 738-753
    摘要

    This article reconsiders hoarding not as a psychological disorder, but as a phenomenon embedded in the temporality of social life. Drawing on ethnographic research in Japan, it explores local understandings of accumulation and disorder in three contexts: the biographical trajectories of participants, the everyday rhythms of consumption and disposal, and the material temporalities that emerge when objects are taken out of circulation. Rather than describing hoarding disorder as an enduring mental health condition, the article shows how domestic accumulations become salient at moments of biographical transition, specifically marriage and/or cohabitation. The contrasting experiences of women and men reveal how domestic accumulations become problematic when they disrupt culturally normative trajectories of household formation and family life. Interviews with participants also reveal systemic barriers to disposal, such as rigid waste collection schedules that privilege middle-class norms, making accumulation a social, not merely personal, deviance. Finally, accumulated objects acquire significance through their temporal suspension outside ordinary circulation, becoming indeterminate things whose meanings are resolved only through later acts of sorting and disposal. Taken together, these findings suggest an alternative understanding of hoarding that traces a path through the thicket of the social field rather than the internal landscape of the psyche.

  12. How did borders get so bad?: Meaning and function between Honduras and El Salvador
    Daniel R. Reichman · pp. 769-783
    摘要

    Recent anthropology theorizes borders primarily as technologies of exclusion that perpetuate structural inequality and racialized violence. This perspective downplays the varied cultural meanings of boundaries that shape identities within and amongst countries of the global south, ultimately resting on a neo-functionalist model of borders and boundary-making. Using the shifting politics of boundaries between Honduras and El Salvador as an example, this article shows how historical boundary-making practices affect contemporary Central American migratory patterns, with a specific focus on the position of Salvadoran migrants in Honduras.   13. Surprises in a minor key: Doing ethnology and anthropology in post/socialist modernities
    Čarna Brković · pp. 784-799
    摘要

    What are the epistemological strategies of anthropology as a globally decentered discipline? How do anthropologists work beyond the colonially inflected geopolitics of surprise, in which knowledge supposedly emerges from a serendipitous encounter between the modern Western liberal subject and the non-modern Other?   14. When silence speaks: Negotiating memory and inter-generational relationships in leftist families in Turkey
    Lorenzo D’Orsi · pp. 800-815
    摘要

  16. Concerning the boundaries of serious anthropology, or: Can witches fly?
    Hans Peter Duerr · pp. 837-845
    摘要

    In a commentary on German anthropology in the 1970s and 1980s, the literary critic Peter Braun has remarked that Duerr’s essay “Can witches fly?” (together with Hubert Fichte’s “Heretical remarks for a new human sciences”) represented nothing less than a charter “for the revolutionary renewal of anthropology against the orthodox forces of the discipline” (Braun 2002: 223). As the literary critic Ian Robinson once observed (1975: 124), “‘In reality’ is the assertion that one set of rules should judge the rest, especially if they are the rules of physics.” Duerr’s compact and sometimes cryptic argument is essentially an exploration of how such judgments about reality are made with respect to claims of sorcerous flight, from the Renaissance scholar Della Porta to the ethnographer Carlos Castaneda. How is an anthropologist to get to grips with such experiences? It cannot be, suggests Duerr, by positing that alien forms of life are radically incomparable, nor yet by assuming that they can be neatly folded into our own frameworks. Instead, we ought to keep ourselves open to the onto-epistemic surprises occasioned by boundary crossings and borderline cases. For if—as Duerr suggests elsewhere (1985a: 131)—“shocks are the precondition for insight” then this is only so long as we allow ourselves the possibility of being affected.

  2. Home at the Margins: Police Violence, Evictions, and the Resistance to Belonging in São Paulo's ‘Cracolândia’
    Flávio Eiró, Giordano Magri · online 2026-09-29
    摘要

    In São Paulo's Cracolândia—one of the world's largest open‐air drug scenes—urban transformation operates not through direct expulsion but through the systematic erosion of territorial bonds. Drawing on twenty months of ethnographic research with unhoused crack users, NGO workers, and state officials, this article examines how interactions with the state—entangled with commercial and legal pressures—dismantle the survival infrastructures that enable marginalised populations to remain: work opportunities, social networks, housing access, and community ties. Home, we demonstrate, is not merely space but a political claim to stability, community, and the right to remain—a claim constantly under erasure yet persistently reinvented at the margins. Through two extended ethnographic vignettes, we trace how deterritorialisation unfolds in individual lives: one trajectory ending in departure after years of cyclical displacement; another sustained only through alliances with neighbours and outsiders as the condition for conditional belonging. We argue that anthropological attention to these trajectories reveals mechanisms invisible to urban studies focused on physical removal or state action alone. Deterritorialisation operates through making people's identities with territory untenable—severing the relationships, routines, and survival infrastructures that constitute home—even as physical presence remains technically possible.

  3. Ka'aguy Mba'apo: Indigenous Illicit Work and Strategic Opacity in the Interstices of the Paraguay–Brazil Border
    Paul Fabié · online 2026-10-08
    摘要

    This article examines how Indigenous Paĩ Tavyterã/Kaiowa communities along the Paraguay–Brazil border navigate illicit economies that have proliferated amid the expansion of soybean agribusiness and the withdrawal of the state. Drawing on long‐term ethnographic fieldwork in several communities on both sides of the border, it explores how participation in drug‐related work—locally known as ka'aguy mba'apo (“work in the woods”)—is entangled with strategies of subsistence and territorial permanence. The article traces how the “law of the border,” an unwritten and violent normative system, shapes everyday life and constrains the possibilities of visibility and speech. By analyzing the experiences of Indigenous leaders and families involved in, resisting, or negotiating with drug trafficking networks, the article highlights how violence, illegality, and precarity intersect in the interstices of the plantation. It argues that the interstitial geography of the frontier—between legality and illegality, visibility and opacity—produces specific forms of political and ethical navigation. These practices reveal a “strategic opacity” that enables the Paĩ Tavyterã to move between multiple normative regimes while sustaining a fragile presence on their land.

  5. Moral Bureaucracies: Objects, Values and Affects in a UK Asylum Office
    Ana Aliverti · online 2026-08-27
    摘要

    British asylum bureaucracy carries a paradoxical mandate: To allocate humanitarian protection while operating inside a policy regime explicitly designed to deter. Drawing on ethnographic fieldwork in a Home Office decision‐making office, this article examines how that contradiction is materialized in mundane bureaucratic objects—a whiteboard, a letter and a plastic folder—and how it shapes the moral and emotional work of the staff who handle asylum claims. Each object simultaneously depersonalizes the asylum applicant and serves as a surface on which caseworkers perform a residual ethics of personalization. Against accounts that locate bureaucratic power in emotional detachment, moral distance, or indifference, I argue that the power of asylum bureaucracy lies in its capacity to sustain a promise of care against an institutional architecture of fear and suspicion. The article contributes to anthropological work on asylum paperwork, materiality, and the affective life of the state.

  8. The Disposability Industry: Waste and Responsibility in Circular Economy Policymaking in the United States
    Christine Hegel, Elizabeth Balkan · online 2026-09-30
    摘要

    Increasing amounts of disposable packaging and products, which litter city streets and reveal the limits of landfilling, have prompted calls to hold corporations responsible for the waste they produce. Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR) policies were initially designed to regulate industry in ways that would force them to package consumer products more sustainably. But in recent years corporations have positioned themselves as the authors of such policies and a new legal formation, the Producer Responsibility Organization, has emerged as a mechanism for managing EPR systems. This paper examines this shift in the United States through an analysis of public campaigns by corporate stakeholders to center their role in the regulation of their industries. This has been achieved in part by altering the meanings of “responsibility” in EPR. In so doing, EPR has emerged as a new form of enclosure and resource extraction (of recyclable waste), enabling endless growth in the manufacture of disposable goods and packaging. As the same time, examples from the United States and around the globe reveal multivalent understandings of responsibility that are vital to decentralized projects of circularity.

  9. The Turn to Process: American Legal, Political, and Economic Thought, 1870–1970: By Kunal M. Parker. Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 2024. 325 pp.
    Marianne Constable · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    Kunal Parker'sThe Turn to Process offers an elegant and insightful account of what Parker identifies as the modernist "turn to process" in the disciplines of law, political science, and economics from 1870 to 1970 in the United States.The book charts how pre-20 th -century truths that had been thought of as foundational, such as the common law, the group, money/man/labor and market, became "methods, techniques, process."Modernism for Parker is "an entire orientation toward the world" (9) which focuses on how things are done (as in the work of Jackson Pollock, who adorns the cover) rather than on what things are.If for Pollock, a painting becomes an impression of how one paints that emphasizes how painting happens, rather than what a painting is of, then Parker suggests that similar transformations hold in many other fields, from sociology to music.Law for instance, Parker shows, becomes how one does law; knowing law involves learning methods as substance.So too, politics and economics become matters of process or method.

  1. An Archaeology of Redress: Freedom as Impossible Praxis
    Justin Dunnavant, Ayana Omilade Flewellen · pp. 719-739
    摘要

    Black studies critical theorizations have much to offer the field of archaeology both in theory and in practice. For Saidiya Hartman, redress entails confronting formations of epistemic violence that undergird the archival record; it is a praxis that is always incomplete. This article maps Hartman’s theory of redress onto the practice of community archaeology to explore the opportunities and challenges that arise when archaeologists center youth engagement, capacity building, and cultural and natural heritage stewardship. Using the Estate Little Princess Archaeology Project as a case study, we explore one way archaeologists attempt to offer restitution to the issues their work reveals (i.e., legacies of environmental racism, truncated avenues of economic sustainability, racialized disenfranchisement) through an “archaeology of redress.”

  2. “One of Us or One of Them”: Implications of Forensic Anthropological “Ancestry” Knowledge Production in Global Human Rights
    Jaymelee Kim, Lucia Elgerud · pp. 740-765
    摘要

    Anthropologists have long interrogated and produced knowledge of biological differences between populations. Biological bases, real or perceived, of human variation have also informed US forensic anthropology, with analyses of “ancestry” being routinely conducted to aid in identifying human remains. Methodological bases of such work have been criticized; however, less attention has been paid to how such practices may influence knowledge production in other parts of the world, in part due to assumptions of forensic analyses as objective science. Here, we present ethnographic data from Uganda, Tanzania, and Somaliland, three East African countries without fully developed domestic forensic anthropological capabilities, and the ways in which medicolegal practitioners and survivors of conflict have requested assistance with determining group affiliations of human remains. We demonstrate that such requests raise numerous questions related to best practices for forensic anthropologists working in postconflict contexts, given entrenchments of ethnic tensions, risks of renewed conflicts, effects on policy change, and colonial paternalism. Ultimately, we argue that ancestry knowledge production has spread beyond the United States, which requires investigation because methods intended for the United States—with legacies of scientific racism—inform forensic anthropological work in Eastern Africa.

  3. The Judeo-Arabic Debates: Ideology, Ecology, Evolution
    Jonathan Glasser · pp. 766-790
    摘要

    For its critics, the notion that Jewish usages of Arabic are sufficiently distinct to merit a separate name (Judeo-Arabic) smacks of ideologically motivated linguistic differentiation. But if we accept that language ideology is ubiquitous in human experience, the grounds for questioning or affirming a linguistic boundary become shaky. This article seeks to make room for context-sensitive notions of Judeo-Arabic while remaining attuned to the conditions that can make such notions fraught and elusive. Unlike critics who prioritize modern histories of nationalism and colonialism, I draw attention to the importance of the diglossia characteristic of both the longue-durée Arabic field and the traditional Jewish language practices for making sense of the Judeo-Arabic debates. Through a critical reading of the scholarly literature as well as consideration of turn-of-the-century talk about language in Algeria under French rule, I propose an interpretation that takes seriously the implications of the language ideology framework while situating it within a broader vision of ecological conditions and evolutionary process.

  4. The Diaspora of Pre-Columbian Objects from Atacama into a Global Network of Goods, Collectors, and Museums between 1850 and 1950
    Benjamín Ballester · pp. 791-819
    摘要

    Pre-Columbian objects from Atacama not only held value and articulated social relations in pre-European times. To this day, and from at least the nineteenth century onward, they continue to play this role by being exhibited in national and international museum showcases, reproduced in catalogs, or owned by private collectors. It is clear that their past value was changed in these new contexts, but what did not change was their amazing ability to bring individuals and institutions together across time and space, even across continents and generations. The bonds forged in the acquisition, circulation, and possession of these ancient objects and collections formed a global network of relationships between humans and things. Such a web was not woven randomly or whimsically but rather warped within the structure of the world system where the Atacama Desert has participated since the mid-nineteenth century, orchestrated principally by the saltpeter, copper, industrial, and mercantile economies. The circulation and biographies of pre-Columbian objects from Atacama is an extraordinary source for understanding the world system in which they were extracted, exchanged, accumulated, assembled, inherited, and exhibited between 1850 and 1950. This dual reflection also opens up a fantastic possibility to better comprehend the role of the cultural works of the past in the making of each social reality at different moments in time.

  5. Interracial Publics: Negotiating Race, Intimacy, and Belonging in Postapartheid Public Culture
    Sebastian Jackson · pp. 820-830
    摘要

    This article examines the formation of “interracial publics” in postapartheid South Africa, showing how race, intimacy, and belonging are negotiated through mediated encounters. Drawing on ethnographic fieldwork, interviews, and media analysis, it traces how relationships between people historically classified as “white” and “nonwhite” become publicly visible and collectively interpreted across print, broadcast, and digital platforms. These publics are ambivalent and contested: they can normalize formerly forbidden intimacies and foster cross-racial connection, yet they also reinscribe racialized hierarchies through gendered and sexualized discourse. Situating these dynamics within South Africa’s longer histories of settler colonialism and apartheid, the article argues that the regulation of intimacy has been central to projects of racial governance and white supremacy and continues to animate their afterlives in contemporary struggles over visibility, belonging, and desire. Interracial intimacy functions as both a measure and a mediator of contemporary racial formations, contributing to comparative anthropological debates on race, media, affect, and the public sphere in postcolonial societies.

  6. Queer Muslim Subjectivity: LGBTQIA+ Identity in Malaysia between Transnational Self-Awareness, Pilgrimage Politics, and Islamic Repression
    Viola Thimm · pp. 831-849
    摘要

    Identities of nonnormative genders and sexualities in Malaysia have recently transformed in tune with global developments. The particularity of a queer identity (i.e., an identity by people who do not feel that they belong to the cisheteronormative majority society in terms of their sexual or gendered orientation or identity) evolved in the Malaysian context only around 10–15 years ago. This can be traced by the growing visibility of local queer Malay Muslims in Malaysia, foremost through claiming a queer subject position in public space and on pilgrimage, given the importance of this spiritual journey for every believing Muslim. Yet the claiming of this position is in a reciprocal relationship with repression: state Islamic institutions aim to marginalize queer people in Malaysia. One main approach to reach their goal is to ambitiously pursue the idea of “rehabilitating” queer people through offering Islamic education programs that include pilgrimage packages. This article deals with recent developments of “queerness” as a subject position that is situated in a context of transnational self-awareness of LGBTQIA+, embedded in broader reconfigurations of subjectivation and selfhood in Malaysia. It thereby contributes to scholarship on queer identities in Islam.

  7. Blurring Islam: Sensorial Negotiations of Secularity among Malabaris in Malaysia
    Waseem Naser · pp. 850-870
    摘要

    This article centers on a sensory ethnography of the Malabari community in the state of Johor in Malaysia. In a country where Islam is legally and bureaucratically tied to the Malay “race,” the collective mobilization of the Malabari community was observed to be primarily through the cultivation of a sensibility that is discernibly Islamic but distinct from that of the majority Malay Muslims. The ethnography demonstrates this through the capacity of particular sensations to become sedimented in the body through collective practices. Having explored how the senses are collectively privileged by them, the article then moves on to the question of why. I argue that the cultural attention granted to the senses arises from the particular context of Malaysian secularity, wherein Islam is actively invoked for mobilizing and regulating a segment of the population. Sensory ethnography of Malabaris thereby discloses distinct features of secularity in Malaysia while concurrently stimulating a distinct means of conceptualizing it through the notion of sensibility.

  8. Negotiating Gender and Justice: Alternative Judicial Bodies and Dispute Resolution in Kurdish Turkey
    Ruşen Bingül · pp. 871-885
    摘要

    Long neglected and marginalized by the state, inhabitants of Turkey’s southeast region, particularly its Kurdish communities, look not to the official courts but to renowned local figures, religious leaders, and civil organizations to resolve disputes. This paper explores three types of alternative judicial bodies (AJBs): (1) traditional Heqî AJBs, which prioritize religion and patriarchy in their decision-making; (2) self-identified progressive AJBs run by local civil society organizations; and (3) newly emerging AJBs aligned with state institutions, reflecting the state’s new frontiers in the region. Although these judicial bodies, shaped by diverse adjudicative tendencies and deeply embedded in the region’s religious, political, and social fabric, differ in their impact in terms of whom and what they include or exclude, they collectively provide alternative pathways for dispute resolution. This paper examines the role of AJBs in resolving disputes in the Kurdish southeast, focusing on gender, religious discourses, the involvement of feminist activists and organizations, and the positionality of these bodies within regional politics and sovereignty formation. Based on ethnographic fieldwork in Mardin, it examines interactions between Kurdish AJBs and women’s agency and mobility, while situating AJBs within broader debates on legal pluralism and gender justice in feminist legal scholarship.

  9. Moving to the Center of the Earth: The Anthropology of Kinetic Rituals
    Nurit Stadler · pp. 886-902
    摘要

    This article inquires how specific bodily movements, such as kneeling, crawling, bowing, and other forms of approach-oriented, low-posture locomotion, produce religious experiences and spatial public manifestations of the sacred. To address this question, I present and theorize kinetic rituals: the directional and counted sequences of embodied movement elicited by site-specific architectural conditions. The analysis focuses on microchoreographies generated through scripted pilgrimages and devotional approaches to sacred centers. My formulation departs from Mauss’s “techniques of the body” (learned motor schemes), Bourdieu’s habitus (durable dispositions), Csordas’s embodiment (the body as existential ground or modality of attention), and Coleman’s accounts of pilgrimage mobility (circulation across networks). Instead, in what I term “kinetic rituals,” I make an analytic distinction between potentiality (i.e., the socially and materially charged capacities to move) and the kinetic emplacement (i.e., their actualization in mimetic, repetitive, directional motion that reactualizes sacred times in public centers). On the basis of a multisite ethnography at Catholic shrines, I show how architectural fittings and surfaces (carpeted knee paths, enumerations of steps, low portals, narrow stairs) render kinetics empirically legible and ritually efficacious. I describe how repetitive locomotion generates devotional attention, temporal blending, and spatial claims that link the sacred and profane registers in public spaces while simultaneously having political consequences, including the inscription of territorial claims. By identifying the kinetic ritual as a distinct mechanism, this article extends embodiment and mobility approaches and puts forward a movement-centered framework for analyzing how built form and disciplined motion together make the sacred experientially present in already secularized spaces.
