  1. Restorative Storytelling for Migrants’ and Refugees’ Mental Wellbeing and Community Empowerment in Canada: A Scoping Review
    Joseph Adu, Rade Zinaic, Priscilla N. Boakye, Yemisi Onilude, Raliat A. Owolabi, Esther Olukayode et al. · pp. 399-417 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    Storytelling is a widely used method in community-based research. We conducted a scoping review to explore restorative storytelling and community theatre as a tool to support mental health literacy and wellness as well as equity among immigrant communities. Our review followed the methodological framework espoused by Arksey and O’Malley and modified by Levac et al . All 13 peer-reviewed studies that met the inclusion criteria were conducted in Canada. Twelve of the included studies employed qualitative methods, with only one using a quantitative approach. The included studies involved diverse immigrants living in communities across Canada. We identified five themes that spoke to the objective of the review: (1) stories of social marginalization and barriers; (2) increased awareness and understanding through stories; (3) storytelling as a space for transformation and healing; (4) connectedness and sharing through stories; and (5) storytelling promoting empowerment and resilience. Language barriers alongside race were identified as major hurdles encountered by some immigrants. Digital storytelling was seen by some immigrants as a process that provided the opportunity to reflect and reminisce on life, which fostered understanding and (re)connection with self and, in so doing, improved emotional wellbeing. Given the increasing mental health disparities in immigrant and racialized communities in Canada, efforts toward improving the mental wellbeing of immigrants through restorative storytelling and community theatre warrant further investigations.

  2. “I Don't Have Answers, What Must I Say?”: Maternal Communication in Forcibly Displaced Families Who Are Seeking International Protection in Ireland
    Elizabeth Nixon, Natalie Flanagan, Nina Rottmann, Frédérique Vallières, Maj Hansen, Rory Halpin et al. · pp. 418-430 · online 2026-06-23
    摘要

    As forced displacement and migration reach unprecedented levels globally, the ways in which parents communicate stories of trauma, displacement, and post-migration adversities to their children offer unique insight into how these experiences affect forcibly displaced families. The present study aimed to explore how mothers communicate with their children about their experiences of trauma, displacement, and mental health whilst seeking International Protection in Ireland. Semi-structured qualitative interviews were conducted with 19 mothers who had children aged between four and 17 years. All mothers were applicants for, or recipients of, International Protection in Ireland. Data were analyzed using thematic analysis. Two superordinate themes were developed from the analysis. ‘Guarding communication’ occurred when mothers used strategies including avoiding certain topics of conversation, silencing their children, and withholding information from their children. ‘Grappling with communication’ occurred when mothers sought to be child centred yet doubted themselves and were inconsistent in how they communicated with their children. Mothers do not adopt one strategy for communicating with their children. Rather, communication was fluid and dynamic as mothers oscillated between silence and narration. The findings show how difficult it can be for mothers to tell their narratives to their children, and have implications for the provision of therapeutic support for parents and families. What matters most may be to support mothers’ agency in choosing the manner, timing, and content of their communication with their children.

  3. Prevalence of Mental Disorders and Healthcare Seeking Among Mozambican Adolescents in School
    Aisha King, Salma Adam, Carolina Bila, Maria Eduarda Fernandes, Teresa Rodrigues, Palmira Fortunato dos Santos et al. · pp. 431-442 · online 2026-06-12
    摘要

    The objective of this study was to describe the prevalence and demographic correlates of mental disorders and healthcare-seeking behavior of adolescents in Mozambique. Trained assessors administered sociodemographic questionnaires and the Portuguese Mini International Neuropsychiatric Interview for Children and Adolescents (MINI-KID) to adolescents in two schools in Maputo City, Mozambique to determine mental disorder diagnoses. Associations between sociodemographic factors and mental disorders were assessed with logistic regressions. Among 488 Mozambican students (ages 12 to 19; 66.4% female), the prevalence of mental disorders was 23.0%. Anxiety (17.8%) and depressive disorders (8.6%) were most common. Half of adolescents who were diagnosed with a mental disorder had two or more diagnoses. Females were more likely than males to have a mental disorder (odds ratio (OR) 1.60, 95% confidence intervals (CI) 1.00–2.57), and the likelihood of mental disorder diagnosis increased with age (OR 1.28, 95% CI 1.12–1.47) and grade (OR 1.24, 95% CI 1.07–1.45). Only 2.7% of adolescents with a mental disorder had sought mental health care in the previous year. To our knowledge, this is the among the first studies to use a structured diagnostic interview (MINI-KID) to report the prevalence of mental disorders among Mozambican adolescents. High rates of untreated mental disorders underscore urgent need for increased mental health services. High comorbidity of mental disorders suggests that transdiagnostic interventions may be an effective approach to care. Primary healthcare settings where adolescents routinely receive care may be appropriate for mental health treatment integration.

  4. Multilingualism in Psychotherapists in Training in Germany: Results of a Nationwide Cross-Sectional Study
    Paula Bachmann, Vera Mohwinkel, Mike Mösko · pp. 443-454 · online 2026-06-23
    摘要

    Patients who speak languages other than those spoken by mental health professionals are significantly disadvantaged in mental health care settings. To bridge the communication gap, multilingual health professionals and professional interpreters are target-orientated solutions. This study was conducted to assess the multilingual resources, attitudes and needs of psychotherapist trainees in Germany. A cross-sectional nationwide online survey was conducted on psychotherapists-in-training. The 48-item survey consisted of a self-developed instrument to assess ‘Attitudes towards working with an Interpreter in Health Care Services (AIHC-20)’ with partly reasonable psychometric properties. Psychotherapists-in-training were reached via 238 institutes for psychotherapy training in Germany. Some 803 psychotherapists-in-training completed the survey, of whom 27% had a migration background and 76% would treat patients in a language other than German. According to the participants, on average, 7.5% of their treated patients had limited German skills. The likeability of treating patients with limited German skills and of working with professional interpreters was positively influenced by a personal migration background, cross-cultural encounters in everyday life and previous work experience. Some 52% of all trainees had experience in working with an interpreter and 72% of them stated a training on ‘psychotherapy with an interpreter’ to be useful. The linguistic diversity among future psychotherapists is not able to fully cover the patients’ actual multilingual needs. Therefore, qualified interpreters need to be integrated into mental health care services. In addition to adequate funding and minimal qualification standards for interpreters, psychotherapists need to be trained to overcome cultural barriers.

  5. De-Occupation as a Determinant of Health: A Genealogy of Public Health Sovereignty
    William Rezentes, Izaak L. Williams · pp. 455-470 · online 2026-09-14
    摘要

    Within the context of the U.S. occupation of Hawai‘i, State-sponsored and Hawaiian-led health initiatives cannot be reduced to a binary of “colonial/unhealthy” versus “Hawaiian/healthy.” Instead, they operate within overlapping and often conflicting paradigms structured by what we term the Ideology of Occupation. This ideology obscures a central reality: kaumaha—the deep, collective malaise affecting the Hawaiian psyche ( Rezentes, 1996 )— is the result of a disruption in the kū‘auhau (genealogy) of Hawaiian public health, in which sovereign systems of care were replaced by a bureaucratic apparatus detached from Hawaiian life. Although culturally grounded interventions provide meaningful therapeutic value, the prevailing culture-as-health paradigm remains embedded within the status quo of occupation. As such, it functions as a co-opted form of palliative care that manages the symptoms of a political source of suffering. Drawing on Laenui's phases of Decolonization and grounded in Sai's legal framework of Belligerent Occupation, we reframe healing through the conceptual paradigm of health sovereignty, defined as the authority of a people to determine the knowledge systems, institutions, and political conditions that shape health and well-being. We argue that the health of the Hawaiian nation cannot be realized within an occupied system of care. Meaningful healing requires the restoration of the Hawaiian Kingdom's lawful authority and the reestablishment of self-determined health systems rooted in Hawaiian knowledge, institutions, and jurisdiction.

  6. Navigating Faith-based Practices in Therapy: Psychotherapists’ Experiences in India
    Ananda Krishnan, Amrita Deb · pp. 471-482 · online 2026-08-14
    摘要

    Addressing clients’ faith-based practices (FBPs) is indispensable in psychotherapy, especially in a religiously diverse country like India. This can be particularly challenging in contexts where psychotherapy is highly stigmatized and FBPs are culturally accepted. A review of the literature highlighted a gap in research on how psychotherapists negotiate FBPs in the Indian context. This pilot qualitative study aims to address this gap by exploring the experiences of Indian psychotherapists in navigating FBPs as mental health service providers. Semi-structured interviews were conducted with 10 Indian psychotherapists. Thematic analysis of the transcribed data generated three major themes and eight subthemes. The findings indicate that FBPs play a nuanced role in clients’ mental health care, especially when clinical services like psychotherapy are stigma-laden and difficult to access. However, it is often challenging for psychotherapists to discern the adaptive and maladaptive aspects of FBPs for the client's welfare. Additionally, systemic factors shape clinician interactions with FBPs in psychotherapy practice. This study highlights the dual role of FBPs in supporting and hindering psychotherapy and provides insights into the interplay of culture, faith, and mental health. However, all participants revealed that religious and spiritual competence training programs were unavailable in India. Although psychotherapists learn these skills through experience, their ability to effectively engage these competencies is limited by social barriers like stigma. The findings of this study underscore the importance of these competencies and provide insights into contextualizing such training programs.

  7. Developing Cultural Self-Awareness for Intervention in Diversity Contexts: A Scoping Review
    Nolwenn Gonzalez, Maeva Traverso, Yvan Leanza · pp. 483-502 · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    In a diversity intervention context, the practitioner may have biases and stereotypes related to their own cultural background that will influence their approach. Developing cultural self-awareness could help reduce the manifestation of these biases and stereotypes. However, it is likely to be one of the least researched aspects of intercultural competence. This scoping study was conducted to provide an overview of how cultural self-awareness is addressed across disciplines related to interventions in diverse contexts. The methodology used followed that of the Joanna Briggs Institute. The different steps of the scoping review are the following: keyword selection and translation in the different selected databases; a first sorting of the selected studies according to the defined criteria, based only on the title and the abstract; a second sorting by reading the entire study; data extraction and finally, analysis of the results. In total, 25 studies were included. The results showed that it is indeed possible to develop cultural self-awareness through workshops that promote interaction among participants, whether they are students or professionals already in the workforce. However, this focus of the research is relatively new and underexplored in the literature.

  8. Understanding Generational Differences in Muslim American Mental Health Help-Seeking
    Leena Badran, Niveen Rizkalla, Steven P. Segal · pp. 503-513 · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    Attitudes toward mental health help-seeking are shaped by a complex interplay of environmental, sociocultural, religious, and generational factors. Within Muslim communities, these attitudes are further informed by religious teachings, cultural norms, and intergenerational dynamics. This study explores the disparities in mental health-seeking behavior between first- and second-generation Muslim Americans. The sample consisted of 37 participants, including 16 first-generation immigrants (foreign-born) and 21 second-generation individuals born in the USA to at least one immigrant parent. The study used a combined deductive and inductive thematic approach to the analysis of semi-structured interviews. Interview content codes and themes were developed using a six-phase method for qualitative thematic analysis. Four major themes emerged encompassing distinct subthemes. First, acculturation effects including: (a) mental health awareness, normalization, and knowledge; and (b) the influence of acculturation on attitudes. Second, intentions including: (a) first-generation participants often sought private help-seeking; and (b) openness to public help-seeking and a health-conscious orientation. Third, perceived behavioral control. Fourth, pathways to seeking mental health care including: (a) preference for informal support systems; (b) incorporation of religious aspects; and (c) a preference for Muslim therapists. The first three themes highlighted notable intergenerational differences; the fourth theme revealed shared pathways to seeking care between the two groups. Culturally adapted practices to mental health service provision should integrate both traditional and non-traditional cultural awareness while addressing intergenerational gaps to enhance mental health support within these communities.

  9. The Culture of Silence and Inuit Mental Health in Post-Pandemic Greenland: Reconfiguring Silence and Voice Beyond the Talking Cure
    Daria Morgounova Schwalbe · pp. 514-530 · online 2026-08-11
    摘要

    Over the past three decades, psychological trauma has become a central framework in Indigenous mental-health research, shaping how silence, disclosure, and healing are interpreted. Within this framework, Inuit silence has been framed as a collective pathology—a “culture of silence”—and a risk factor contributing to suicide in the Arctic. In this article, I draw on pandemic-era public communication and professional narratives collected at a large health event in Greenland to critically reflect on cultures of silence and confession. The article demonstrates how crises such as the pandemic bring to the surface tacit or neglected dynamics, and highlights how hegemonic knowledge frameworks, which prioritize disclosure-centred models of care, shape contemporary mental health governance in Greenland. The case study identifies three recurring patterns: (a) disclosure is framed as both a cure and a civic duty; (b) local practices of quiet that sustain dignity and relational safety are overlooked; and (c) institutional logic of disclosure redirects blame and responsibility from structural conditions to individual and collective failures. Drawing on conceptual metaphor theory, anthropological and historical scholarship on silence and violence, and decolonial approaches to knowledge production, the article highlights the risks of disclosure-centred frameworks. It argues that such models may misrecognize culturally grounded practices of quiet as absence or pathology, rather than as meaningful relational forms of coping and care. It suggests the need for approaches that attend to multiple forms of communication and care, and avoid equating well-being with verbal disclosure and silence with deficiency. By reframing silence as a relational and culturally situated practice, the article contributes to ongoing debates in transcultural psychiatry about the limits of universalizing therapeutic models. It also contributes to scholarship on structural violence, Indigenous mental health, and the potential mistranslation of illness and wellness.

  10. Intoxicated with Madness: Exploring the Psychology of Intoxication in Uncovering the Experimental Roots of the Kraepelinian Dichotomy
    Matthew Perkins-McVey · pp. 531-544 · online 2026-08-11
    摘要

    Scholarship surrounding the Kraepelinian dichotomy of dementia praecox and manic-depressive insanity has overwhelmingly approached Kraepelin's nosology as the product of Kraepelin's clinical method, use of diagnostic cards, and pre-existing disease concepts, rather than his work in experimental psychology. Revisiting the significance of Kraepelin's 1881–1895 concept of intoxication as model psychosis, this article establishes that Kraepelin's earlier psychological experiments with intoxicants were a potential source of latent, synthesizing concepts, informing the organization of clinical data around two discrete forms of psychosis. Tracking Kraepelin's nosological system over the course of the first six editions of his textbook, it examines Kraepelin's classificatory descriptions relative to the content of his experimental studies on the psychometrics of intoxication, in particular the dualities Kraepelin finds in exogenous and endogenous psychoses. More specifically, it addresses the alignment of inhibitory and excitatory effects at the level of sensation and perception found within both Kraepelin's experiments and psychiatric nosology, proposing that manic-depression and dementia praecox conform with excitatory and inhibitory modes of exogenous psychosis.

  1. The Outsiders: Principled Withdrawal, Whiteness, and Power in the Los Angeles Food Justice Movement
    Hanna Garth · pp. 445-455 · online 2026-04-27
    摘要

    This article draws on understandings of whiteness and the misconstrual of South Central Los Angeles to analyze the power dynamics between “outsider” activists and residents of South Central as they worked toward a more equitable food system. As concerns over the “obesity epidemic” and the push for “real food” in the 2000s coincided with a surge in GIS data on “food deserts,” a new cadre of activists, often white and middle‐class, began intervening in South Central Los Angeles as part of the food justice movement.   2. Containing Histories Past and Present: Making Samples in the “Huntington Collection” (1893–1921)
    Alanna L. Warner‐Smith · pp. 456-468 · online 2026-05-25
    摘要

    The Huntington Anatomical Collection (1893–1921) includes the skeletal remains of immigrants, migrants, and lifelong New York City residents. The collection's formation was coeval with the formalization of physical anthropology, and the collection was made to serve research aims centered on race and origin. This early focus on race has materially shaped the collection, and, in turn, researchers’ ongoing engagements with it. When delineating research questions and forming samples, researchers (even those critical of race science and the collection's history) tend to do so along race‐ and place‐based categories prioritized by early curators and physical anthropologists. Treating these categories as the primary axis of difference flattens the complex life histories, movements, and social relations of the people in the collection, thus occluding a view of life courses as lived. Drawing upon archival evidence, I argue that such separation practices have profound implications for how we view belonging, social relations, and kinship in the past. These concerns extend into present questions regarding descendant engagement, as practitioners and institutions define communities of care and navigate future consultation and return.

  3. Critical Research Spaces as Scholarship: an Ethnography Lab as an Apparatus for the Experimental, the Imaginary, and the Relational
    Fiona P. McDonald · pp. 469-479 · online 2026-05-25
    摘要

    The creation of critical research spaces, such as ethnography labs, studios, and other collaborative research environments, requires attention and attunement in anthropology to focus on the kinds of imaginative and generative spaces where creative ethnographic research can unfold as scholarship . This article reflects on the recent design, construction, and implementation process of the Collaborative + Experimental Ethnography Lab at the University of British Columbia, Okanagan (UBCO), serving as an argument that an ethnography lab is a scholarly apparatus for the experimental, the imaginary, and the relational. While this critical research space situates sensory ethnography as its methodological orientation, as a lab, it also foregrounds how digital and analog tools feature in collaborations with infrastructure and field research through processes of experimentation and prototyping with the lab as an apparatus while attending to policy, people, and the imaginary. This article argues that creating a research lab in anthropology is scholarship.

  4. Making Mining Licit: Gold, Commodification, and the Everyday Performance of Law in Colombia
    Jesse Jonkman · pp. 480-493 · online 2026-05-27
    摘要

    Ethnographies of resource‐making have shown that the extraction of resource value from objects is premised on obviating the emplaced lifeworlds that surrounded objects before they traveled to consumer markets. Much of this literature looks at such supply‐chain disentanglement from the viewpoint of corporate and formal regulatory practices. Lead firms, legal regimes, and downstream industries do the obscuring; disadvantaged people in production sites are being obscured. This article broaches the question of commodification somewhat differently, as it focuses on a gold frontier where variously informalized peoples are playing their own part in enacting politically loaded erasures.   5. It Takes Two to Tango: A Pluralist Account for Building Comprehensive Explanations in Human Evolution
    Lumila Paula Menéndez, Sophie Veigl · pp. 494-510 · online 2026-05-29
    摘要

    The evolutionary study of human dispersal is a key topic in biological anthropology. However, recent research has revealed inconsistencies between molecular and anatomical data across different timescales and geographic regions. Despite increased interdisciplinary dialogue, these discordances are rarely analyzed in depth or interpreted for their biological significance. We present two case studies: human diversification in Southeast Asia and the Americas, which highlight persistent conflicts between morphological and genetic interpretations. Drawing on recent calls for extending the conceptual tools of evolutionary theory, we argue for a pluralist explanatory framework that can account for developmental plasticity, environmental responsiveness, and multiple trajectories of inheritance. We show how treating different datasets as epistemically equal—rather than subordinating anatomical data to molecular “controls”—allows for more comprehensive and nuanced explanations of evolutionary phenomena. This approach not only offers new insights into past human variation but also addresses long‐standing issues of disciplinary fragmentation.

  6. The New Jewish Shepherd: Land Grabbing and Redemption in the Occupied West Bank
    Irus Braverman · pp. 511-527 · online 2026-05-31
    摘要

    Drawing on ethnographic fieldwork, this article explores the recent deployment of sheep and grazing practices by Jewish settlers in the occupied West Bank. The wave of herding outposts that has swept through the West Bank recently involves the use of Jewish‐owned sheep to undermine and dispossess Palestinians and to re‐indigenize the settlers in their place. The sheep have thus become potent yet clandestine agents of control as well as animated technologies for producing, instilling, and normalizing biblical and spiritual imaginaries of the landscape and of the Jewish people as authentically belonging to it. Backed by the state, the settlers’ mundane shepherding activities have already enabled the largest land grab in the occupied West Bank since 1967. Ultimately, I argue that studying Jewish shepherds in the West Bank expands our understanding of settler colonialism and illuminates how sheepwashing practices that position settlers as ecopastoralists are used to justify violence in the eyes of the occupying regime, exacerbating the problematic settler ecologies practiced here.

  7. Bonds, Bounds, and Borders: Crafting Hospitality with Unauthorized Migrants in Southern France
    Céline Eschenbrenner · pp. 528-536 · online 2026-06-02
    摘要

    This article analyzes the everyday politics of migrant hospitality in rural Southern France. Drawing on four years of fieldwork alongside benevolent residents hosting unauthorized migrants at their home or volunteering in migrant shelters, I consider how residents attempted to make up for the state's abandonment of migrant lives, the ethical dilemmas which they faced in so doing, and the political solutions they explored. Benevolent residents built infrastructures of solidarity and turned homes and shelters into sanctuaries where unauthorized migrants could stay without risking deportation. As unwilling hosts, they attempted to carve pockets of hospitality in the margins of border regimes.

  8. Anthropology and Evocative Significance: Art, Sport, and Aesthetics
    Joshua D. Rubin · pp. 537-547 · online 2026-06-22
    摘要

    “Evocations” are frequently mentioned in anthropological accounts of art and artistic production, but the particular efficacy of the term for anthropological inquiry has not been considered in significant depth. This article argues that a theory of “evocations” has the potential to enrich not just anthropological considerations of art, but examinations of aesthetics and sensory perception as well. It offers a conception of evocations as contextually specific resonances between the sensuous texture of things and the socially produced sense‐memories that are felt by those things’ perceivers. Such a conception allows for encultured understandings of aesthetic perception that extend beyond those most prevalent in “Western” thought while still offering a framework for the generalized analysis, through close ethnographic investigation, of such resonances and their political significances. This article highlights the efficacy of both evocations and their close examination by documenting some of the many ways that rugby performances in South Africa have evoked the country's political history. By focusing on rugby in this way, this article demonstrates how “evocation” invites a recognition of the aesthetic politics entailed in domains of social life—like sports—that are richly evocative but are not conventionally analyzed in conjunction with conceptions of art.

  9. Perfecting Exit: The Politics of Quitting Among Migrant Care Workers in the United States
    Cati Coe · pp. 548-557 · online 2026-06-22
    摘要

    Quitting tends to be overlooked in studies of resistance and labor because of its individual and private character, its ineffectiveness in changing conditions of labor, and the difficulty of studying it, in favor of more organized and public protests and strikes. This paper examines why quitting is so popular among African immigrant home care workers in the United States, arguing that exit was attuned to the diffuse power relations of fissured workplaces in which home care workers care for individual clients through the mediation of brokering agencies. Quitting was not entirely individualized and private, as it became a collective strategy nurtured through circulating stories and a widely shared worldview of anti‐Black racism and anti‐immigrant sentiment. In exploring the complexity, possibilities, and limitations of quitting, I point to the continuum of modes and degrees of resistance, rather than compartmentalizing some as private and some as public. The variability in the degree of privacy and individuation makes these forms of maneuver in relation to diffuse power adaptive to different degrees of scaling up and organizing. As anthropologists attend to the dynamism of resistance in relation to power, they should not overlook the phenomenon of quitting.

  10. Bodies, Stereotypes, and Reproduction: Black Migrants Experiencing Obstetric Violence and Racism in Portugal
    Catarina Barata, Chiara Pussetti · pp. 558-571 · online 2026-06-24
    摘要

    This article explores how the mistreatment of women in health care facilities in Portugal takes on particular forms for Black migrant women, shaped by essentialist ideas about race and miscegenation. In line with academic production on processes of racialization and discrimination against minorities and the medicalization of reproductive processes and biocontrol, this paper focuses on how these phenomena are reflected in the gynecological and obstetric care of Black women of Brazilian and African origin in Portugal.   11. Campaneando : Surveilling With the Dead in Bogotá’s Drug Markets
    Andrés Romero · pp. 572-580 · online 2026-07-03
    摘要

    This article offers a literary ethnography of one of Bogotá’s ollas or drug markets where centuries of violence ripple across the ambiance.   12. The Mutable Original: How Chinese Counterfeits Become Nigerian Originals in African Markets
    Jing Jing Liu · pp. 581-594 · online 2026-07-04
    摘要

    Affordable Chinese copies of Western brands are ubiquitous in African markets. Despite democratizing consumer access, these goods appear to cement hegemonic value hierarchies that rank Chinese or local products as inferior to Western goods. To unsettle this implicit geography of fakes and dominant politics of authentication, scholars have proposed a continuum of hybrid identities—from genuine fakes to copied originals—seeking to collapse a simplistic dichotomy between originals and counterfeits. This article argues the opposite: Upholding this dichotomy is crucial for Nigerian entrepreneurs operating in counterfeit economies to stake original claims by means other than being “first” or “from the West,” often impossible production conditions to satisfy. Following a well‐known Spanish brand of soaps and lotions, Idole, and other Western cosmetics brands in Nigerian markets and on e‐commerce platforms, this ethnographic study shows how improving quality, concentrating quantity, and expanding the variety of fakes offer new epistemological possibilities for what makes an original original . Counterfeits become originals when performance distorts primacy and where popularity inverts provenance. Informed by Chinese philosophies on copying, the “mutable original” captures sellers’ and buyers’ paradoxical indifference to copying and investment in originals, along with the commercial and cultural contexts that nurture these seemingly counterintuitive interests.

  13. Water Beings and Capitalist Relations in India's Sundarbans Delta
    Calynn Dowler · pp. 595-606 · online 2026-07-13
    摘要

    This article explores entanglements between water cosmologies and capitalist transformation in the Sundarbans delta of West Bengal, India. It traces how “awakened” tidal creeks have been iteratively enclosed as private fisheries from the colonial period to the present, with particular focus on the expansion of commercial aquaculture over the past 30 years. I show that the consolidation of capitalist relations has produced striking cosmological divergences. Today, private fishery owners often engage water beings through ritual worship and sacrificial offerings, recruiting them into profit‐oriented production. In contrast, their dispossessed neighbors claim that water beings have abandoned local waterways. Considering such divergences, I argue that capitalist transformation has proceeded through the delta's water cosmologies. Rather than displacing or erasing emplaced cosmologies, these processes have reorganized relationships with water beings and produced new and uneven geographies of enchantment.

  14. Living the End of Beauty: Threatened Landscapes and the Politics of Aesthetic Emotions
    Sébastien Roux, Jean Chamel · pp. 607-617 · online 2026-07-13
    摘要

    This article demonstrates how aesthetic emotions, such as wonder, awe, grief, and enchantment, transform into practical judgments that guide care and politics in threatened landscapes. Drawing on multiyear ethnographic research conducted at two sites that we place “in dialogue” rather than in strict comparison—the rapidly warming Alps (France, Italy, and Switzerland) and a conservation complex at Empire Ranch in Arizona—we demonstrate how invocations of beauty and ugliness render ecological change tangible and morally significant. In the Alps, mountaineers, guides, and glaciologists observe the “darkening” and retreat of ice, reworking their attachments as they learn to recognize—or reject—new forms of beauty. In Arizona, volunteers, scientists, and neighboring communities actively promote beauty through restoration practices, where scientific metrics and often unspoken aesthetics quietly coproduce decisions about species, histories, and futures. In both locations, aesthetic judgments are not epiphenomenal; they are situated social practices that authorize certain feelings, mute others, and establish particular orders of care. By foregrounding ordinary conversations about beauty, we contribute to anthropologies of the environment, affect, and aesthetics, showing how people experience ecological crises through sensory value, how loss is apprehended and negotiated through aesthetic judgment, and how these values become claims about what must endure.

  16. Modulating Hunting Atmospheres: Mud and Material Flows Beyond the Kill
    Juan Martin Dabezies · pp. 629-639 · online 2026-07-25
    摘要

    This article argues that hunting is not a bounded event but a materially redistributed atmosphere that extends beyond the kill and across field, truck, clothing, and home. Drawing on long‐term ethnographic fieldwork with wild boar hunters in Uruguay, I show how mud, odors, blood, and bodily residues carry the sensory, affective, and material force of tracking, waiting, and pursuit into domestic space.   21. Political Animals: To Live & Die in More‐Than‐Human Worlds
    Luísa Reis‐Castro · pp. 660-667 · online 2026-07-25
    摘要

    What does it mean to live—and therefore die—in a more‐than‐human world? This review essay explores that question through reflections on teaching Political Animals: Species, Class, Race, and Gender, an undergraduate course that introduces students to scholarship on human‐nonhuman relations. Organized around four questions—What are the politics of connections in more‐than‐human worlds? What is health or well‐being when some multispecies relations can cause harm and death? Who counts as human? And how might the human‐animal “divide” be reimagined?—the course investigated how encounters and entanglements between diverse beings, as well as the boundaries between humanity and animality, are shaped by power, hierarchy, and enduring inequalities. Tracing how these debates unfolded in the classroom, I argue that multispecies scholarship, despite critiques that it sidelines social inequalities by supposedly bypassing the human, can instead offer new ways of thinking through race, class, gender, colonialism, and the contested meanings of being human. In doing so, I show how the classroom offers a distinctive vantage point from which to examine how anthropological ideas travel beyond academic debates. Teaching more‐than‐human relations becomes a way of preparing students to grapple with the political and ethical stakes of living—and dying—in a shared world.

    摘要

    This article critically examines INDIAsize—an Indian government–funded anthropometric initiative to “decolonize” body size standards used in clothing production.   4. When Humans Need Gods and Gods Need Humans: Prophetic Authority and Ritual Negotiation in Himachal Pradesh
    Asaf Sharabi · online 2026-10-26
    摘要

    In Himachal Pradesh, India, village deities act as political figures who play an active role in communal affairs, especially during periods of social change. Bakhan , an annual divination ritual in which these deities speak through human mediums, is a public event that offers forecasts and instructions. I argue that the bakhan is a social arena in which divine authority is publicly enacted and made answerable to communal judgment. Drawing on ethnographic research, I develop the concept of “performative accountability” to show how prophetic claims gain force through a process in which they are voiced, interpreted, increasingly questioned, and measured against subsequent events. Divine authority, I argue, does not rest on revelation alone but takes shape through ongoing relations of recognition and response between gods and villagers. The bakhan reveals a form of authority sustained through public scrutiny rather than being insulated from it.

  5. Medical Archaeology and the Research Roads of Global Health in the Age of Eugenics
    Franco D. Rossi · online 2026-10-25
    摘要

    This article centers on an underexplored dimension of archaeology—the role of medicine and medical doctors in the ways archaeological excavations connected at times with early global health research and infrastructures. Archaeologists today have particular historical inheritances and responsibilities in need of understanding, and perhaps something important to contribute to the collective work of repair.

    Alpa Shah · online 2026-10-23
    摘要

    摘要

  9. Spectral Ecologies: Sintering More‐Than‐Human Pasts to Less‐Than‐Certain Futures
    Suraiya Luecke · online 2026-10-12
    摘要

    How do we mourn the loss of more‐than‐human beings, and what becomes of them when they are gone? This commentary examines two installations in Spectral Remix , an art exhibition at the 2025 American Anthropological Association Annual Meeting in New Orleans. The first is Cymene Howe and Dominic Boyer's Glacier Graveyard , which memorializes glaciers lost to anthropogenic climate change. The second is the XYZ Collective's The Atlas of Heritage Trees , which uses laser scanning, 3D printing, and digital animation to rematerialize ancient trees now threatened by climate change and saltwater intrusion. Drawing on embodied and affective encounters with each installation, I show how Glacier Graveyard extends human funerary rituals to glaciers through an immersive and visceral encounter, while The Atlas of Heritage Trees imagines uncanny technoscientific afterlives for dying trees as they are translated into digital forms. I engage the analytic of sintering —which refers to both the physical process that bonds ephemeral snowflakes into durable glacial ice and an advanced laser technique used in 3D printing—to examine uncanny resonances across these two more‐than‐human works. Taken together, these works show how installation as a medium can facilitate a deeper reckoning with more‐than‐human pasts and less‐than‐certain futures, forcing us to confront which bonds we choose to make and which bonds we choose to break in our shared worlds.

  14. Customer Support Analysts, UX Researchers, and Directors of Storytelling: A Systematic Analysis of Job Advertisements for Anthropologists
    Lauren Hayes, Tyler Bowers · online 2026-09-28
    摘要

    Anthropology graduates increasingly enter public and private practicing jobs. A decade ago, Brondo and Bennett argued that a disciplinary sea change was in motion that acknowledged applied work. Despite this recognition, the anthropological canon does not always address applied career tracks, and practicing work still encounters disciplinary stigma. Given the steep decline of academic jobs, however, we face a critical juncture, requiring a revisitation of the value of anthropological ways of knowing to careers outside the academy. To better understand how anthropological training fits into the job market, this paper presents findings from a systematic analysis of more than 300 nonacademic job advertisements directed at anthropology graduates of all levels. Results show that job ads for anthropologists include preferred technical, qualitative, and quantitative skills, yet also that epistemological approaches unique to anthropology, such as ethnographic research design and data collection and the ability to problem‐solve and translate results, are paramount. Based on these findings, we argue that anthropological training is highly valued in the practicing job market. A better understanding of nonacademic, practicing jobs for anthropologists, how to train students for these jobs, and how employers make hiring decisions deserves our attention as we consider the future relevance of anthropology.

  15. Beyond the Dichotomy of Success and Failure: Making Activism Meaningful in South Korea and France
    Guilherme Fians, Sera Yeong Seo Park · online 2026-09-26
    摘要

    First deployed in the 1970s, prefiguration has recently gained traction among ethnographers of political action as a descriptor for activist practices that foreground participatory democracy, horizontality, and direct action, as seen in social movements like Occupy Wall Street. Notably, ethnographers have come to understand these prefigurative‐inspired, present‐oriented movements as departures from “traditional” social movements, which are often institutionalized via trade unions, associations, and cooperatives, and primarily driven by preestablished, future‐oriented goals. We suggest that this dichotomy, unintentionally reinforced by anthropological scholarship, has a broader analytical implication: It prevents ethnographers from seeing both goal‐oriented and prefigurative practices as equally constitutive of any form of activism. We draw on ethnographies of two seemingly future‐oriented movements typically read as failures: activism led by families bereaved by the Sewŏl Ferry disaster in South Korea and by Esperanto speakers in France. We propose that, while these movements’ long‐term goals remain indefinitely out of reach, their activists have found additional sources of meaning that sustain their long‐term commitment. Understanding activism as a continuous meaning‐making process, rather than a means to an end, would allow ethnographers to understand why such engagements persist. This requires, we argue, venturing beyond the dichotomies of success–failure and prefigurative–traditional.

  16. Four Questions for Ethnographers Under Trumpian Authoritarianism
    Elizabeth Hanna Rubio · online 2026-09-24
    摘要

    This article examines evolving challenges and ethical dilemmas facing ethnographers working under intensifying authoritarianism in the United States. Because very little of what the United States is experiencing is new, my work here is primarily curatorial in nature, drawing upon the lessons offered by scholars across disciplines in the United States and beyond for whom ethnographic research in authoritarian contexts has always been the norm. Following my recent decision to withdraw a manuscript from publication due to potential repercussions to activist interlocutors, I outline four questions for U.S.‐based ethnographers navigating heightened surveillance, repression, and vulnerability to state violence. These questions focus on (1) how to listen differently in the field and make sense of silence amid eroding public trust, (2) how to look differently and use ethnography to refuse authoritarian chrononormalization of violence, (3) how to question one's own assumptions that exposure via research and publication will necessarily lead to change, and conversely (4) how unexamined refusal to publish may constitute acquiescence in advance. Through this work, I invite scholars to engage in a disciplined hesitation as we rethink the stakes, strategies, and limits of ethnographic work in this political moment.

  17. The Anthropocene and the End of Nuclear Disaster: Normalizing the Fukushima Nuclear Accident
    Maxime Polleri · online 2026-09-23
    摘要

    This article places the concept of disaster in critical dialogue with that of the Anthropocene, which introduces new imaginaries that negatively affect the governance of crises like nuclear accidents. I argue that tropes surrounding the Anthropocene can depict certain events as apocalyptic calamities while also portraying other events as mere “incidents” that do not warrant such labeling. I explore how specific narratives around the Anthropocene legitimize some forms of environmental harm as more important than other threats. This process creates a hierarchization of catastrophes and normalizes certain hazards as less important than others. More specifically, I claim that the very concept of a nuclear catastrophe is at risk of “disappearing” within the Anthropocene. Using ethnographic data based on the 2011 Fukushima nuclear disaster, which happened in Japan, I focus on three popular tropes associated with the Anthropocene that normalize the consequences of nuclear accidents: the figure of the global threat, the disappearance of the idea of pristine nature, and the promotion of resilience and adaptation to new realities. I conclude by reflecting on the broader implications of how Anthropocene discourse shapes which disasters matter and which are relegated to the status of distant, forgettable accidents.

  18. Burying Futures: Artisanal Mining Deaths and the Politics of Negligence in Zimbabwe
    Melusi Nkomo · online 2026-09-22
    摘要

    In the gold‐mining town of Kwekwe, central Zimbabwe, injury and death are a constant presence. Collapsing shafts, sudden explosions, gang fights, and neglected health infrastructure characterize the everyday lives of the town's impoverished residents. This article, based on ethnographic research conducted between 2019 and 2023 in Zimbabwe's artisanal and small‐scale mining regions, examines how young miners, their families, and township communities navigate a landscape where untimely deaths and near misses shape political subjectivity and reveal broader modes of state power. By examining how the state and its local enforcers respond to devastation in impoverished communities—and how these communities endure and interpret such recurring losses—the article shows that negligence is not just an absence but an active political repertoire. It contributes to anthropological debates on crisis, necropolitics, and state sovereignty in mineral‐extraction frontiers by showing how authority persists less through outright domination than through calibrated neglect: a mode of rule in which timely intervention is withheld, and the state reemerges selectively during moments of injury, death, or mourning to absorb loss as a political occasion.

  19. How Anthropology Can Harm Students During a Genocide
    Girish Daswani · online 2026-09-22
    摘要

    This essay is about the harm that is committed in the name of care. More specifically, it is about student requests for support during the ongoing genocide in Palestine and an anthropology department's use and weaponization of the language of “care” to do the exact opposite: to cause harm. I examine how the language of care for students and the exception of Palestine are weaponized by administrators—including anthropologists—to silence and shut down conversations about, and support for, Palestine.   2. Antibiotic use in Türkiye: Everyday encounters of translating policy and negotiating rationality
    Ayse Nalan Azak · online 2026-07-01
    摘要

    Antibiotic use surged during COVID‐19 despite it being a viral illness, heightening global concerns about antimicrobial resistance (AMR). In Türkiye ‐ where antibiotic consumption and AMR were already high ‐ this paradox exposed longstanding tensions between policy and practice. Türkiye has adopted World Health Organization guidelines promoting the “rational” ( akılcı ) use of antibiotics, yet everyday healthcare encounters reveal how these reforms are translated, negotiated and reinterpreted. Drawing on fieldwork in Istanbul, this article shows how local expectations of antibiotics as symbols of care and professional competence shape prescription and use.   3. Betwixt playing the waiting game and waiting in vain: Temporal governance and the thin alignment of care under universal health coverage in Kenya
    Edwin Ambani Ameso · online 2026-06-02
    摘要

    This article investigates how Kenyan citizens access healthcare within the framework of Universal Health Coverage (UHC) reforms. Based on ethnographic fieldwork, it reconceptualizes waiting as a politically structured phenomenon rather than a simple delay. The analysis shows that UHC reforms do not eliminate waiting but instead redistribute it, resulting in new bureaucratic bottlenecks, infrastructural limitations, and unpredictable care timelines. The concept of ‘thin alignment’ is introduced to characterize a temporally unstable condition in which endurance and improvisational strategies coexist as patients navigate fragmented healthcare systems.   9. Violence in harm reduction: Exploring the social, political, and emotional conditions of harm reduction work
    Guillaume Dumont, Marie Jauffret‐Roustide · online 2025-12-30
    摘要

    Harm reduction professionals strive to reduce the health, social, and legal consequences associated with drug use in contexts permeated by violence. Building on fieldwork in Paris and Barcelona, we examine how they make sense of this violence. In Paris, the discussion of violence primarily hinges on the narratives of suffering from people who use drugs and the obstacles posed by the political context. In Barcelona, the narrative emphasizes precariousness and deficiencies in organizational violence management, which intensifies perceptions of violence. Moving beyond polarized understandings of violence, we argue that violence is socially constructed as an inherent aspect of the culture of harm reduction work. This process involves mechanisms of naturalization, delegitimization, and normalization, shaping work experiences and the construction of the professional self. Although violence manifests in similar forms and manifestations across settings, experiences of that violence differ based on how it perpetuates power dynamics and inequalities within the workplace.

  10. Watching you wear out: Time as clinical labor
    Stephanie Davies · online 2026-06-15
    摘要

    Drawing on clinical fieldwork in NHS general practice in East London, I consider the relationship between time, chronic illness, and therapeutic endeavor. Over the course of three treatment narratives, I attempt to show the negative labor that I claim is implied when clinical time is used to watch and wait as somebody is “wearing out” from chronic conditions. This may be the only form of care available to clinicians in situations where medicine is limited in what it can do to intervene. It emerges in response to illnesses that must be allowed to run their course and recurring symptoms that must somehow be tolerated, tarried with, or “waited out.” The aim of the paper is to try to engage the grey area between medical intervention and non‐intervention, where time in the clinic is offered in the service of helping people to wear out on their own terms.

  11. What's in a Name? Psychiatric Concept Creep and the Moral Legibility of Student Suffering within the Canadian University Context
    Adrianna Nicole Wiley · online 2026-03-09
    摘要

    This article explores how students experiencing mental unwellness negotiate psychiatric constructs of mental health to make their suffering morally legible within the North American University context. I argue while the psychiatric construct remains pervasive, students are ambivalent toward it as a metaphor for their distress. Students engage in bottom‐up psychiatrization pragmatically to navigate relational contexts when appropriate for them, while recognizing that the impacts of top‐down psychiatrization can limit institutional moral care obligations, constraining their authority to communicate their subjective experiences authentically outside psychiatric idioms. In essence, psychiatric labeling is constraining, but students often exert agency by refusing a psychiatric master status while still recognizing their need to use psychiatric labels to allow their suffering to be seen and addressed within the current cultural‐historical moment of psychiatrization.

  1. Revisiting SAVA: Syndemic transition and the reconfiguration of disease and inequality
    Nicola Bulled, Noa Kantorovich · online 2026-10-26
    摘要

    Syndemic theory emerged within medical anthropology to challenge nosological models that fragment illness into discrete diseases divorced from social context. Revisiting the original SAVA syndemic in Hartford, Connecticut—the empirical setting from which syndemic theory first developed—we argue that syndemics are historically dynamic formations that reconfigure over time. While recent scholarship has demonstrated the historical embeddedness and continuity of vulnerability, comparatively less attention has been paid to how syndemic configurations themselves are restructured within populations over time. Drawing on historical and contemporary epidemiologic evidence, we trace a period in which HIV, substance use, and violence were mutually reinforcing, followed by HIV‐focused harm reduction and treatment efforts that reduced HIV's dominance without transforming the political–economic conditions that concentrated vulnerability. We conceptualize this patterned reordering as syndemic transition, a temporal extension of syndemic theory that foregrounds reconfiguration rather than resolution while retaining attention to the social production of disease.

  2. Convergent Reproductive Governance and the Normalization of Caesarean Birth in Northern Thailand
    Anjalee Cohen · online 2026-10-25
    摘要

    Caesarean section (C‐section) rates in Thailand have nearly doubled over the past two decades, a trend often attributed to women's preferences, convenience, or advanced maternal age. Drawing on ethnographic research in Chiang Mai, northern Thailand, this article argues that such explanations are insufficient. I show that rising C‐section rates are shaped by the convergence of biomedical discourses of risk with local understandings of bodily fragility, gendered spiritual vulnerability, and cosmological practices such as auspicious birth timing. I conceptualize this process as convergent reproductive governance, through which biomedical, familial, and spiritual authorities become aligned around a shared construction of childbirth as inherently dangerous and in need of protection. Women's reproductive decisions, often framed as matters of preference, instead emerge through negotiations across these intersecting domains of authority. Through this convergence, biomedical authority is reinforced through its alignment with, rather than displacement of, local knowledge.

  5. Transing the archive: Toward a trans‐autistic historiography of neurodivergent futures
    Jesica R Bates · online 2026-09-09
    摘要

    This article advances transing the archive as a methodological intervention into how autism's canonical history became institutionally secured through archival processes operating within clinical and bureaucratic regimes, not simply through omission.   6. Time, place, person: Field epidemiology as a political practice
    Luisa Enria · online 2026-09-09
    摘要

    Five years after the end of the Ebola epidemic, a district in Sierra Leone received a measles alert.   9. Diet and leishmaniasis in the Ecuadorian Amazon
    Veronica Cristina Vargas Roman · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    In Ecuador's Amazon, Indigenous communities affected by leishmaniasis often modify their diets as part of their health‐seeking practices. Drawing on qualitative research with sixty‐eight participants from seven Indigenous groups, this study explores how and why patients’ diets shift in pursuit of healing from leishmaniasis. Participants understand therapeutic diets holistically, including food recommendations, preparation methods, and social behaviors believed to influence disease progression. Across groups, dietary logics of therapeutic efficacy often reflect analogical reasoning, a form of relational ontology in which the qualities of foods are believed to act upon the body through correspondence rather than causality in the disease's symptoms. Therapeutic dieting operates not only as a health‐seeking practice but also politically, as a mechanism of moral and social regulation within the space of (self‐)care. This study invites medical practitioners working in Amazonian contexts to engage with non‐physiological logics of healing efficacy to enhance culturally responsive care and patient well‐being.

  1. A tour into untouched land: Enacting wilderness through relational engagements
    Eva Kotašková · pp. 707-730 · online 2024-06-10
    摘要

    Drawing upon an ethnographic study of guided tours in Svalbard, in this article I explore how is the wilderness enacted as a situational and contextual outcome of more-then-human relations, emerging from engagements with the environment. As the engagements during the guided tours contest the often expected ‘purity’ of wilderness, I further investigate in detail when and how nature-culture dichotomy is both contested and re-produced in the enactment of wilderness. I argue that the nature-culture dichotomy constituting wilderness on Svalbard shifts from the idea of human-less nature to life in harmony with nature in which some human traces are present. This dichotomy then emerges from more-than-human relations. Further, I show that the character of wilderness is also emerging from different situations and contexts, where temporality, knowledge and dependency on self or others influence the engagements and more-than-human relations.

  2. America materialis: Things and meaning in a donation warehouse
    Aditya Srinivasan · pp. 731-754 · online 2024-07-20
    摘要

    How do things shift shape - both physically and metaphorically - as they transition across spaces and social contexts? Using ethnographic data gathered over ten weeks by volunteering at a donation warehouse for one of Syracuse, New York’s largest refugee resettlement agencies, I argue that the donations in question come to represent the peculiar moral economy of donation in America.   3. Affective encounters with waste: The role of affects in trash activism
    Ella Lepistö, Olli Pyyhtinen · pp. 755-780 · online 2025-01-22
    摘要

    Previous scholarship on waste has identified lack of public knowledge as one of the greatest barriers to the success of any recycling initiative. However, informational influencing has proven insufficient, as it tends to ignore the messy, ambiguous, bodily relations that people have with waste. Waste is prone to evoke visceral affective responses, and those affects matter for how we relate to waste. In this article, we examine the role of affects in the practices of Finnish trash activists followed by means of ethnographic fieldwork both on trash walks and on social media.     Vera Lazzaretti · pp. 781-801 · online 2024-07-19

    摘要

    Toponymic struggles are about forms of civic belonging that street names enable or prevent. Drawing on a moral economy perspective, this article examines similarities and differences between struggles over street names in the Spanish city of Murcia and the South African metropolis of Johannesburg. In contrasting these cases, a paradox becomes apparent: While actors in toponymic struggles have to evoke moral goods such as inclusivity, fairness and neutrality to advocate for change or in defense of the status quo, these values are rendered suspect in the very same process. Nonetheless, in the examined cases toponymic struggles lead in part to more inclusive solutions than those originally expected by actors that face each other as adversaries in the local moral economy of toponymic struggles. The article is based on intensive field research in both areas of study, including participant observation, qualitative interviews, online ethnography, and an analysis of newspaper articles and policy documents.

  6. Constructing responsibility: Infrastructural harm, citizen oversight and the politics of publics
    Sam Rumé · pp. 822-840 · online 2024-12-16
    摘要

    While concepts such as infrastructure and assemblage can make it difficult for scholars to locate responsibility for social injustices, given the diffused nature of action and harm, this article starts from the continuous, competing and negotiated constructions of responsibility that can be explored ethnographically. The argument is based on fieldwork in Cuenca, Ecuador, where the flawed construction of a tram threatened local businesses and led to citizens’ organized attempt to attribute responsibility and be compensated. In the face of an apparently irresponsible state, these citizens thus tried to reconstruct responsibility through what I describe as the weaving of causal chains and the search for alliances of (self)responsibilization. In this analytic, the construction of responsibility proves a key component of how worlds are inhabited and made and, more specifically, how the state, the public good and citizens are configured.

  7. New forms of home blindness: Rethinking fieldwork methods in digitalized environments
    Tuva Beyer Broch, Tom Bratrud, Marianne Lien, Cecilia G Salinas · pp. 841-859 · online 2024-07-21
    摘要

    Digital Anthropology has in the past two decades emerged as a field that seeks to better grasp experiences of being human within digital technology and culture. Based on ethnographic fieldwork in Norway, one of the most digitalized countries in the world, we argue that the ubiquity of the digital re-actualizes classic debates in the discipline on ‘home blindness’ emerging from the methodological challenges of doing fieldwork in familiar surroundings.   8. The conviction of the inevitable: Collapsism and collective action in contemporary rural France
    Jérôme Tournadre · pp. 860-879 · online 2024-07-23
    摘要

    The idea of a possible collapse of thermo-industrial societies became more prominent during the 2010s, particularly in France. How does this conviction, which mixes cognitive and emotional registers, come to grasp certain individuals? And what do these people do with it? Contrary to various studies in the social sciences, the ethnographic observation of two collectives that have been affected by this conviction and have settled in the French countryside makes it possible to draw the contours of authentic collective actions.   10. Cleaning up shaʿbi: Music and class-cultural divides in cairo
    Sophie Frankford · pp. 899-920 · online 2024-11-26
    摘要

    Egyptian shaʿbi music is typically positioned as a somewhat vulgar style produced and consumed by the working-class masses.     Paul Mutsaers, Maikel Meijeren · pp. 921-940 · online 2023-02-22
    摘要

    Samuel Brookfield · pp. 1015-1034 · online 2024-04-18
    摘要

  1. Dam money and displacement: Laws and intimate rupture in the Muş Alpaslan-1 Dam Project, eastern Turkey
    Noor Boz · online 2026-10-28
    摘要

    Dams are often framed as emblems of national progress and modernisation, yet their construction produces slow, reverberating forms of violence that disrupt everyday life. This article examines the social, legal, and gendered repercussions of the Alpaslan-1 Dam, built on the Murat River in Muş province, Eastern Turkey, drawing on 10 months of fieldwork. The dam submerged villages, displaced thousands, and transformed livelihoods, land ownership, and social relations. This article shows how infrastructural projects penetrate intimate domains of life, reshaping landscapes, kinship, and lived experience.

  2. Rheostatic visibility: On neurodivergent fieldwork, impulsive adaptation, and methodological craft
    Hussein Nasser · online 2026-10-18
    摘要

    Ethnographic fieldwork assumes a researcher who can be simultaneously present and analytical, embodied and observant, legible to participants while remaining reflective about that legibility. Drawing on diary entries recorded during three years of ethnographic fieldwork in France, I examine what happens when the researcher’s cognitive particularity does not conform to that assumption. I develop the concept of rheostatic visibility : the continuous, context-sensitive calibration of self-disclosure and identity expression across multiple audiences, including the self. Like a rheostat regulating electrical current beyond the binary certainty of a switch, visibility can become continuously and agentically calibrated in ways that produce methodological consequences. I argue that neurodivergent adaptation to fieldwork conditions can exceed compensation and generate methodological innovation. The multilingual, cryptographic fieldnote system I developed emerged from coping practices that only became legible after receiving an ADHD diagnosis. I conclude by calling for critical examination of the neurotypical assumptions ethnographic methodology presumes.

  3. Deferred eating: An ethnography of hunger, waiting, and food insecurity in the floating labor markets of Dhaka, Bangladesh
    Ahsan Habib, Abu Siddiq · online 2026-10-10
    摘要

    This ethnography examines deferred eating among floating day laborers who gather at open-air morning hiring sites ( labor bazar ) across Dhaka, Bangladesh. We define deferred eating as the temporally structured postponement, thinning, or suppression of eating during the hiring window to remain visible and available for work before wages are earned. Drawing on long-term fieldwork supplemented by a background survey of 749 laborers, we show food insecurity is produced not only within households but also through the temporal and social organization of the hiring ground. Workers rely on tea stalls, informal credit, and food sharing to endure waiting, while older workers, recent migrants, less-skilled laborers, and women experience the longest and least buffered deferrals. Hunger is also morally negotiated, as workers avoid expressing need directly and insist they are workers, not beggars. Deferred eating identifies a mechanism through which precarious labor becomes embodied hunger, extending food insecurity research beyond household consumption.

  4. Who works, who sits: Embodied ethnography and the everyday organization of domestic inequality
    Ilqua Lutfi · online 2026-09-29
    摘要

    This paper examines how ordinarily taken-for-granted forms of domestic inequality become ethnographically perceptible.   5. Female religious mediation in wartime labour migration: Ukrainian Greek Catholic women in Israel
    Anna Prashizky · online 2026-09-14

  1. The magic of AI: Unveiling the sociopolitical implications of belief in artificial intelligence
    Michiel Baas, Roanne van Voorst · pp. 243-259 · online 2026-07-28
    摘要

    This introduction argues that anthropology requires a new conceptual framework for understanding the contemporary social life of artificial intelligence (AI). We propose "the magic of AI" as an analytical concept that shifts attention from AI's technical capacities to the beliefs, uncertainties, and sociotechnical imaginaries through which it acquires authority and efficacy. Revisiting anthropological theories of magic, particularly the work of Ernesto De Martino, Bruce Kapferer, and Francesco Della Costa, we argue that AI's apparent autonomy functions less as a property of the technology itself than as a collectively sustained form of belief. These beliefs naturalize AI's purported inevitability, mobilize political and economic investments, and obscure the human labour, infrastructures, and relations of power through which AI is continually produced. Rather than employing magic as a metaphor, we develop it as a theoretical lens for analysing how opacity, trust, and anticipation generate material sociopolitical effects. In doing so, this introduction offers a conceptual framework for understanding AI as a contingent sociotechnical formation whose power rests as much in collective imagination as in computation itself, while introducing the contributions that develop this argument ethnographically across diverse settings.

  2. Toward a phenomenology of mystification: The ambivalence of magic among AI technologists
    Rodrigo Ochigame, Graham M. Jones · pp. 260-277 · online 2026-07-28
    摘要

    By leveraging machine learning, computer scientists and engineers have developed generative AI models so opaque that experimenting on them is often the only means to study their inner workings. In our ethnographic engagements with such technologists, we have frequently witnessed them contemplate model outputs with rapt mystification. Although accusations that AI technologists deliberately mystify investors, regulators, and consumers are certainly valid, we argue that these critiques also obscure the mystification researchers themselves experience. Taking the anthropology of magic as a point of departure, we focus on those experiences to theorize an anthropological phenomenology of mystification. Discussing the awe experienced by AI researchers, we explore how the value of mystification may be not only a byproduct of building constitutively opaque systems, but also an emergent ambition that increasingly animates the design process. Through a contrast with systems of divination, we argue that tech industry scientists experiment on AI systems not only to overcome opacity, but also to stabilize it and make it available as a resource for future interpretative acts. Far from demystifying generative models, interpretability experiments may reproduce the very sense of enchanting metaphysical excess they nominally seek to contain.

  3. Collaborating with artificial intelligence in healthcare: How collective imaginaries of AI’s future shape its reality
    Roanne van Voorst · pp. 278-296 · online 2026-07-29
    摘要

    This article explores the role of magical beliefs in shaping the rapid integration of artificial intelligence (AI) in healthcare. Drawing on ethnographic research across hospitals in seven countries, it argues that healthcare professionals’ perceptions of AI as an autonomous, inevitable force significantly propel ongoing development and investment, despite frequent frustrations, errors, and ethical concerns. These beliefs—centered on notions of AI's self-learning, unstoppable progression, and perceived agency—function as a form of collective imagination that sustains hope and motivation, even in the face of empirical limitations. While clinicians are aware of AI's flaws and the ethical dilemmas it raises, they continue to work around its shortcomings, driven by a conviction that a flawless AI will eventually emerge through some form of “magical” development. This perspective underscores how shared magical beliefs imbue AI with agency and inevitability, shaping future trajectories beyond material and discursive forces. Recognizing these beliefs offers new insights into the socio-cultural dynamics underpinning technological adoption in healthcare and raises questions about the influence of collective imagination on policy, regulation, and ethical frameworks. Ultimately, this analysis highlights the need to critically engage with the symbolic and cultural forces that sustain the rapid rise of AI, emphasizing that understanding these shared beliefs is crucial for shaping more realistic and ethically grounded futures of healthcare technology.

  4. ‘Very small and a bit flat?' Generative AI as miracle and magic trick
    Matti Pohjonen · pp. 297-318 · online 2026-07-28
    摘要

    This article addresses the question of the ‘magic of AI’ from two interrelated perspectives: first, as a form of belief in the seemingly magical or miraculous ability of AI to replicate human-like knowledge, intelligence and creativity; and second, as a kind of magic trick used by the tech companies to make users believe in AI's extraordinary powers. By reframing AI as both a system of belief and a ritualistic marketing practice, the article argues for a shift of focus away from abstract philosophical debates about AI's existential risks towards a more ethnographically grounded analysis of how such illusions of magic – of AI's intelligence and creativity – are produced and sustained in the first place. Drawing on an ethnographic case study of how text-to-image (TTI) AI models represent controversial historical events such as the 1984 Ethiopian famine, the article shows how AI systems work by recycling a narrow set of visual tropes in their training data that have very little to do with human notions of intelligence or creativity. Underpinning the popular discourse of AI as magic we can thus find an emerging system of power in which tech companies sell the ‘miracle’ version of AI, while users receive only the ‘magic trick’.

  6. The magic in the black box: Geek wizardry and the inherent unpredictability of artificial intelligence (AI)
    Michiel Baas · pp. 342-362 · online 2026-07-28
    摘要

    Research on artificial intelligence (AI) frequently highlights the technology's inherent unpredictability – often captured through the metaphor of the ’black box’ – which makes it difficult, and perhaps even impossible, to determine how particular results are produced. These discussions typically culminate in debates about ethics, reliability and responsibility, yet they say relatively little about how those working with and developing AI themselves relate to this mystification. Drawing on extensive ethnographic fieldwork among two distinct groups in the South Indian city of Bengaluru – artists experimenting creatively with AI and data scientists developing AI-powered applications – this article examines how practitioners engage with the technology's perceived opacity. Many interlocutors acknowledged that beyond a certain threshold, they could no longer fully explain how ‘their’ AI systems arrived at particular decisions or outputs. Instead, they frequently invoked expressions such as ‘that's the magic of it’ or described their work as a form of ‘geek wizardry’. Taking these formulations seriously, the article explores how the seemingly ‘unexplainable’ aspects of AI come to be experienced as magical. Rather than dismissing such language as metaphor, it approaches these references as part of the ways practitioners make sense of technologies that appear to exceed their own knowledge and explanatory frameworks. Drawing on Ernesto De Martino's theorization of magic – particularly his emphasis on context, history and meaning – the article argues that references to ‘AI as magic’ help situate rapidly evolving technological systems within everyday lifeworlds. In a field where developments occur at extraordinary speed and even specialists struggle to remain fully up to date, the language of magic provides a way of articulating encounters with systems whose operations remain only partially intelligible. By placing practitioners’ own descriptions of AI within broader anthropological discussions of magic, the article offers a new perspective on how people live with and interpret the opacity of contemporary algorithmic systems.

  1. What about Beauvoir? Simone de Beauvoir in and for anthropology
    Eva Melstrom · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    While Simone de Beauvoir's distinct philosophical contributions have been considerably revisited over the last 30 years, anthropology has yet to fully engage the significance of her work. Drawing on The Ethics of Ambiguity and The Second Sex , this article argues that Beauvoir's philosophy is a critical recourse for anthropological inquiry, particularly for feminist and phenomenological approaches. I begin by situating the reader in her original philosophical method and theory, which are not simply deconstructions of gendered subjectivity, but rather profound considerations of the political, historical, social, and cultural moorings of lived experience, embodiment, and world-making, particularly for those who have been Othered . I bring further attention to her theory of ethics, which has been overlooked within anthropology, yet serves as an excellent implement for examining situated subjectivity and the temporality of ethics. After exploring three reasons potentially explicating her underuse, I detail her influence across feminist and phenomenological traditions within anthropology to demonstrate where she has been engaged both explicitly and, all the more so, implicitly. I close by suggesting that Beauvoir's theories are increasingly noticeable across anthropology, and reason that her work can serve as a praxis of intersectional philosophy that can expand conversations of diversity, ethical life, and the possibilities of freedom among oppression.

  2. Destabilizing citizenship: De-documentation and the politics of disenchantment
    Orlando Lara · online 2026-09-07
    摘要

    Drawing on recent critiques of liberal conceptions of legal-juridical citizenship, this article develops a model of disenchanted citizenship that moves away from treating legal-juridical citizenship as an unqualified good, normative value, or universal concept. Building on the concept of the regionally specific citizenship regime, constructions of citizenship that can be bound within one nation-state or extend across several, the article examines the inequalities, instabilities, and exclusions that remain central to contemporary regional constructions of citizenship.
