  3. Inaugurated ideology: An anthropological theory of state identity and transformation
    Stephen Fox · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    This essay introduces Inaugurated Ideology (Ix), a theoretical framework for understanding how political succession reconfigures governing rationalities through the symbolic articulation, institutional mediation, and uneven translation of state power. Drawing on political anthropology, critical theory, and the study of bureaucracy, Ix conceptualizes executive transition not as a simple change in leadership, but as a performative process through which overlapping temporal and moral orientations are publicly established and differentially enacted across institutions and policy domains. Unlike paradigms or worldviews, Ix is temporally bounded, strategically articulated, and institutionally consequential, often reshaping legal processes, bureaucratic practice, and lived experience without altering formal statutes. Rather than assigning administrations to fixed ideological types, the framework identifies configurations of temporal orientations—reactive, restorative, revolutionary, and pragmatic—together with moral orientations, inclusionary and exclusionary, that combine differently across political contexts. Through an analysis of US administrations from Ronald Reagan to Donald J. Trump's second non-consecutive administration, the essay traces how ideological succession reshapes public discourse, institutional behavior, and state practice across time. It does so through a two-level examination of US immigration governance, first as a broader field in which shifting governing rationalities become visible through executive framing, administrative priority, and uneven institutional translation, and second through the I-485 adjustment-of-status process, where these configurations become materially consequential through mechanisms such as classification, evidentiary thresholds, administrative time, and bureaucratic discretion. In doing so, the essay offers a framework for understanding political transition as a recurring site for the rearticulation of authority, belonging, and state practice.

Critique of Anthropology

Vol. 46, No. 3 · 2026-09 · 8 篇 · SAGE · Critical / political · Double-blind (verified)
  1. Introduction: The promises and perils of more-than-human resistance
    Bob Kuřík · pp. 251-269 · online 2026-09-02
    摘要

    Who resists? With whom and against whom do they do so? These questions, long thought to be exclusively human, are increasingly proving more than human in the conflicts of the Anthropocene. To keep up with such messy constellations, we suggest, in this special issue, the reintroduction of the concept of resistance by reworking it as more-than-human resistance. This captures confrontations from below, in which humans and other-than-humans forge alliances, partnerships, or otherwise collaborate to oppose dominant power. Defined as such, we argue, the concept with its orbiting analytics advances much-needed encounters between multispecies anthropology and political anthropology, to the benefit of both. This introduction conceptualizes such encounters in four steps, which the authors in this collection touch upon ethnographically in their contributions. First, recasting resistance to include more-than-humans is slowed down by careful interaction with debates from the intellectual history of the concept. Second, the concept is nuanced by juxtaposing the categories unruliness v. resistance, other-than-human v. more-than-human, or alliance v. adversity. Third, amid the contemporary rise of authoritarianism, the concept is detached from a critical anthropologist’s sympathies to encompass a range of political positions. Fourth, resistance is reconnected with power analytics over more-than-human cases to cast both in sharper relief.

  2. More-than-human infrapolitics in an Indian tech-entrepreneurial sustainability program
    Nikhit Agrawal · pp. 270-287 · online 2026-09-02
    摘要

    Alternate wetting and drying of rice fields—aimed at mitigating emissions by killing methanogenic soil bacteria—has gained traction among tech-entrepreneurs as a scalable sustainability solution in contemporary agriculture. Drawing on institutional ethnography conducted at a sustainability-promising agricultural technology (agtech) start-up in India, this article demonstrates how the company’s field staff—rural youth hired on a contractual, incentive-driven basis—turned to the affordances of rodents, snakes, digital tools, and rice plants to subtly resist the start-up’s performance demands while securing their livelihoods. Extending James Scott’s concept of “infrapolitics,” the article introduces “more-than-human infrapolitics” to describe how the subtle everyday acts of resistance emerged relationally, co-constituted by interactions among humans, crops, technologies, and ecological processes. Paradoxically, these cumulative acts did not obstruct the company’s broader goal of generating carbon credits; instead, they inadvertently enabled it, allowing the firm to present the program as a success despite its flawed implementation on farmers’ fields. In contrast to Scott’s formulation, these minor acts of defiance ultimately provided short-term reinforcement to the tech-driven sustainability agenda.

  3. Kiltra resistance: Doing autonomous politics with street dogs in post-uprising Chile
    Lucia Rončíková, Bob Kuřík · pp. 288-308 · online 2026-09-02
    摘要

    During the 2019 uprising in Chile, free-roaming dogs, so-called kiltros , confronting police were turned into symbols of resistance and gained significant attention in studying kiltro politics . While this analytical focus is stimulating for scholars, it is also troubling. It may narrow down such politics to rioting during mass protests and lead to the iconisation of kiltros. Therefore, we expand the focus by scrutinising less spectacular forms of kiltro politics in and after the uprising. Drawing on the ethnographic case of Los Pies del Cerro, an anarchist squat in Santiago, we argue that kiltra resistance emerges through cultivating autonomous collectivism in three more-than-human processes: making rules, carrying out reterritorialisations and cultivating affinities. In these instances, daily compañerismo (comradeship) of human and dog squatters is forged, allowing us to shift from Harawayan companion to compañera species and reframe the popular motto ‘Resist like kiltro!’ as ‘Resist with kiltras!’.

  4. Epistemically resistant, unruly and relational: Modelling groundwater in the Salar de Atacama (Chile)
    Sally Babidge · pp. 309-324 · online 2026-09-02
    摘要

    This article engages with the dynamics of groundwater’s epistemic resistance, its persistent resistance to being a fact, and manifold uncertainties in the human and more-than-human relations of the Salar de Atacama. How might expanded considerations of groundwater’s unruliness better inform knowledge of it and future relations with it? I draw on legal cases that illustrate entrenched problems with mining company and regulatory groundwater science, a set of peer-reviewed published studies that propose ever-better models of subsurface activity that tend to justify ongoing extraction, and the work of Indigenous-led teams of scientists engaging with unruliness and proposing new approaches to political and ecological contestation.

  5. Defending Dogs’ Hillock: The romance of ‘nature’ and administrative jujitsu as tactics of more-than-human resistance
    Mateusz Laszczkowski · pp. 325-341 · online 2026-09-02
    摘要

    Drawing on my personal involvement in defending Dogs’ Hillock – an ‘urban wasteland’ in a Warsaw neighbourhood – from transformation into an infrastructurally oversaturated city park, I examine a landscape of more-than-human resistance. First, discussing how the local residents-turned-activists were motivated by their emotional attachment to Dogs’ Hillock and concern for local fauna and flora, I suggest that ‘romance’ can be a catalyst of resistance. Next, I describe how the activists practised ‘administrative jujitsu’, using scientific expertise and administrative law to defend the Hillock against dominant power. To effectively resist the planning regime, non-human life and human affects must be articulated to forms of political agency, including demonstrations, data-gathering, and legal action. At the same time, the resistance remains more-than-human: it would be impossible without the specific processes and rhythms of life of various non-humans.

  6. Willows and farmer resistance: Withstanding change in rural Denmark
    Stine Krøijer, Inge-Merete Hougaard · pp. 342-357 · online 2026-09-02
    摘要

    This article describes willow scrubs and how, according to Danish farmers, they “sprout uncontrollably” in deindustrializing agricultural landscapes. Across Europe, the green transition of the agricultural sector has led to farmers’ protests, but in Denmark such expressions of discontent have been limited. Instead, willow scrubs have become a matter of concern after the retirement of peatland soil from production. Farmers find willows “ugly” and interpret peatland overgrowth as a sign of a lack of proper care for the future of rural landscapes. Based on fieldwork among farmers and public servants, and unlike the situations described in most multispecies literature, we show how willows lack human allies. We argue that they are to farmers what farmers are to the government, establishing an analogy between the willows’ recalcitrant nature and farmers’ assertion of autonomy from government control. In this context, withstanding becomes a weak form of resistance that involves a political use of the willow’s capacity to grow.

  7. Cats v. birds: Settler delegations and arbitrary domination in Los Angeles
    Christopher M Kelty · pp. 358-377 · online 2026-09-02
    摘要

    This article tells the story of a conflict among “trap-neuter-return” experts, environmentalists concerned about the ecological impacts of free-roaming cats, and the City of Los Angeles Department of Animal Services from 2008 to 2020. The conflict included a lawsuit, an environmental impact report, detailed models of cat population dynamics, and the question of whether sterilizing cats reduced the number of cats in the city. The story is told through a mix of fieldwork conducted around this issue in Los Angeles between 2017 and 2025, and historical and government documents. The article uses the analytics of more-than-human resistance to try to understand better how resistance, and governance, involving more-than-human Los Angeles is unfolding. I argue that the concept of arbitrary domination, and a focus on the specifically American settler-colonial relations of delegated governance can best make sense of the situation.

  2. Johannes Fabian (1937–2026) and the critique of anthropology
    Peter Pels · online 2026-09-30
    摘要

    This brief essay memorializes Johannes Fabian and his contribution to Critique of Anthropology by reviewing his contribution to the journal and his conception of critique in anthropology. It highlights not just Fabian’s role in keeping the journal going after the untimely death of Bob Scholte in 1987, but also how they collaborated in producing a epistemological revolution in anthropology. While most anthropologists today would associate this with Fabian’s contribution to the “literary turn” in anthropology by his more famous Time and the Other (1983) I argue that the build-up to this book shows a currently neglected but far more fundamental and visionary conception of critique that centered on an epistemological critique arising from fieldwork praxis.

  3. The financialization of the American academy and the decline of dissent
    Ronald Loewe · online 2026-09-01
    摘要

    This article first provides a brief overview of the development of tenure and academic freedom in the U.S. amid a series of government efforts to surveille, discipline and remove from their university posts those anthropologists who spoke out against U.S. foreign policy or war, or in favor of civil rights. Following Ellen Schrecker’s focus on key moments of academic repression, I draw on cases from World War I, the McCarthy era and the Vietnam War. The article goes on to draw attention to the systematic repression of pro-Palestinian speech, and to explore differences in the way the 20th-century university (1915–1975) and the modern corporate university have handled dissent. I argue that the contemporary university, shaped by decreased public support, a precipitous decline in tenure density, a greater dependence on the bond market and private philanthropy, and a new type of administration drawn from business and industry rather than faculty ranks, has become increasingly obedient to both government and corporate power.

  4. David Graeber’s anthropology and historical materialism
    Giulio Ongaro · online 2026-09-01
    摘要

    This article examines the one thread in David Graeber’s thought that unifies his approach to anthropological knowledge, global history, and political action: his faith in the revolutionary power of the ‘constituent imagination’. The concept refers to the human capacity to collectively imagine alternative forms of social life and bring them into being. Graeber treated it both as a key driver of history and the most viable means to overcome capitalism by way of prefigurative politics. The article illustrates how Graeber turned structuralist anthropology into a myth-busting project aimed at awakening the constituent imagination. It then weighs the charges of idealism that have been levelled at his work, arguing that, to the degree his emphasis on the imagination came at the expense of recognizing class struggle, these charges have bite. Materialist analysis points up gaps in his account of both social change and political action. The article closes with a call to ground Graeber’s startling anthropology of possibilities in the tradition of historical materialism.

Journal of Material Culture

Vol. 31, No. 3 · 2026-09 · 6 篇 · SAGE · Material culture · Double-blind (verified)
  1. Old devices, new meanings: The enduring materiality of outdated media and communication technologies
    Oya Morva · pp. 319-343 · online 2026-06-26
    摘要

    This article explores the enduring presence and shifting meanings of outdated media and communication technologies (MCTs) in everyday life. Based on 30 in-depth interviews conducted in Istanbul, it examines how individuals retain objects such as Walkmans, vinyl records, and early mobile phones – devices no longer widely used, yet still materially and emotionally significant. Rather than approaching these items as nostalgic collectibles, the study reframes retention as a materially mediated practice through which attachment, memory, and critique are enacted. Bringing material culture studies, science and technology studies, and domestication theory into conversation, the article argues that outdated MCTs act as mediators of identity and memory and enable alternative engagements with time, value, and media. Istanbul's urban ecology – marked by repair networks, second-hand circulation, and layered temporalities – further supports these practices. The article contributes to debates on materiality by showing how old devices persist as materially active participants in everyday urban life – culturally re-signified and affectively charged artefacts rather than passive remnants of past use.

  2. Multiple and ordinary: The materialities of polycystic ovary syndrome
    Júlia Karpova · pp. 344-368 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    This article focuses on the everyday materialities of polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS) (currently being renamed to polyendocrine metabolic ovarian syndrome). A PCOS is a combination of symptoms that vary from patient to patient, such as absent or irregular ovulation and menstruation, impaired glucose metabolism, excessive body hair or problems with fertility. This article adds to research on menstrual health and chronic disease, which has tended to prioritise tracking technologies and medical devices, by drawing attention to ordinary material objects. Drawing on Science and Technology Studies theorisations of disease and self-care, this article examines in-depth semi-structured interviews with people with PCOS to illuminate how materiality shapes everyday coping with this common yet diverse condition. The major finding of the analysis is that although medical professionals frame PCOS as a coherent whole, patients attend to their individual ‘packs’ of symptoms in their daily efforts to manage the condition.

  3. Civilizational gene of knotting: The craftsmanship of the Uurga on the Mongolian Plateau and the material thinking of nomadic societies
    Haiyan Bao · pp. 369-392 · online 2026-07-15
    摘要

    This study examines the Uurga, a traditional Mongolian lasso pole used for capturing and managing livestock, exploring its material, social, and symbolic significance in nomadic societies. Drawing on ethnographic fieldwork, this research traces how the Uurga has functioned not only as a practical instrument of domestication but also as a mediator of relationships between humans and animals. The study is organized into three parts. The first part clarifies the contexts of use and naming system of the Uurga. The second part provides a detailed account of Uurga-making, including pole-making, rope-making, and the intricate binding and knotting techniques. The third part explores the customs and beliefs associated with the Uurga. The study suggest that the Uurga represents a rich material culture, and as a primary technique for connecting different materials, knotting is its core method and may be foundational to the emergence and development of nomadic civilization. As such, the Uurga exemplifies the material thinking of nomadic societies, offering deep insights into the social, symbolic, and practical dimensions of life on the Mongolian Plateau.

  4. The birth of the astronomical price: An ethnographic study of value and price in the Beijing art market
    Shijun Zhang · pp. 393-421 · online 2026-07-22
    摘要

    This ethnographic study examines the genesis of the astronomical price—known as tianjia —within the Beijing art market and aims to contribute empirically to anthropological debates on the value of art. Focusing on the dynamic interplay between abstract value and monetary price through the lens of practice, it traces how astronomical prices emerge through the coordinated labor of valuation, the discursive and embodied practices of auctioneers and bidders, and the ritual intensification of the auction event itself. By examining the concrete labor involved in the construction of value and the competitive escalation of bids at auction, the study argues that modern auctions are not merely sites where an object's value is formed and its ownership is transferred, but also ritualized stages on which archaic cosmology and modern calculative knowledge are brought into convergence through interpersonal interactions on the auction floor. It is through this convergence, the article suggests, that a form of collective effervescence is generated and astronomical prices come into being.

  5. Unsettling collections, revitalizing culture: The work of art in Indigenous southeastern Australia
    Anna Weinreich, Vicki Couzens · pp. 422-444 · online 2026-08-20
    摘要

    Situated in southeastern Australia, this article examines the pivotal work of Indigenous artists in revitalizing cultural practices and knowledge that colonial violence worked to erase. Our discussion tracks the material legacy of Kaawirn Kuunawarn (c.1820–1889), an important Ancestor of the Gunditjmara Nation and the maker of two greenstone axes held in Berlin's Ethnological Museum. We reassemble Kaawirn Kuunawarn's material record—dispersed across multiple collections and a distance of over 16,000 kilometers—and chart its legacy in the work of contemporary Indigenous artists. While our analysis offers a remarkable account of Gunditjmara cultural “survivance,” it also shows how Indigenous histories were obscured, rather than preserved, by global processes of museum collecting. In light of these erasures, cultural revitalization is highlighted as transformative political work: an affective, embodied mode of presencing the past that unsettles the colonial legacies of collecting, reconstituting archival objects and images as vital conduits of Indigenous social memory and identity.

  6. Material imaginaries of the new iron curtain
    Natalia Magnani, Matthew Magnani, Anatolijs Venovcevs, Richard Fraser, Giuseppe Amatulli · pp. 445-469 · online 2026-08-08
    摘要

    The “social imaginary” has been critiqued for its overly deterministic qualities and resemblance to concepts of culture. Our article interrogates the materiality of the indeterminate at a time of heightened global uncertainty. Furthering a rapid response, material methodology in anthropology, we examine objects, animals, and infrastructures that disrupt and reinscribe border imaginaries in differential and unexpected ways in Sør-Varanger, on the Norwegian border with Russia, one month after the full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, amid emerging international sanctions and national border closures. We show how everyday engagements with various “materials"—souvenirs and monuments, surveyed and commodified species, industrial and transport infrastructures—foster a sense of uncertainty around the (im)permeability of an East-West border. The array of effects that they have on the imagination is unevenly and at times randomly shaped by their materiality. However, these differential effects are also patterned in their indeterminacy, as the scale and temporality of the materials’ movements affect their potential to transform border imaginaries. We thus rethink the material imaginary as simultaneously indeterminate and patterned in dialogue with scalar and temporal dimensions of mobility.

  1. From traps to camps: Ghost fishing, plastic materialities and carceral entrapment
    Magdalena Buchczyk · online 2026-10-29
    摘要

    This article draws on ethnographic research with weavers and fishermen in Sardinia to explore emergent modalities of entrapment. Following fish traps from a Berlin museum collection to the bottom of the lagoon, and contrasting nasse traps made from plant material with synthetic ghost traps submerged in the Gulf of Oristano, I consider how synthetic materials exercise an indiscriminate, chronic agency that extends beyond single objects and designs. Combining material ecology with a critique of fossil capitalism, I develop the concept of carceral entrapment, or a modality of world-making that produces persistent harm beyond human control, in which the durability of plastics dissolves the dramatic nexus that Alfred Gell saw as constitutive of traps. Instead, carceral entrapment operates across scales and through ecological, economic, and corporeal relations, and generates enduring, unpredictable, and pervasive forms of harm. An anthropology of traps, I argue, must attend to these complex agentic relations and their uneven distributions.

  2. Material noise at Cape Coast Castle: The costs of decolonizing heritage
    Xiao Wei · online 2026-10-28
    摘要

    Taking Cape Coast Castle as a case, this article asks how material remains sustain decolonial memory while unsettling the institutional arrangements that make it possible. Drawing on guided tours, local conversations, material observation, and institutional discussions, it shows how slavery memory is translated into controlled discomfort: powerful, recognizable, yet ordered enough to remain visitable and comfortable. The article argues that material remains are simultaneously docile and unruly: dampness, residues, corrosion, salt, microbial growth, and climatic weathering give emotional scripts sensory density, while the same traces become material noise when they demand cleaning, repair, conservation, funding, and responsibility. Digital heritage intensifies this tension as decolonial efforts to relieve material burdens risk reproducing coloniality through technological and donor dependency. Decolonization is therefore not the overcoming of pain, but a costly, unsettling, and unresolved struggle to live with material noise.

  3. Thinking with material care
    Taru Peltola, Kati Pitkänen · online 2026-10-27
    摘要

    Engaging with care theories and perspectives arising from repair studies, this article proposes material care as a conceptual possibility to think with and to interpret material relations leading to overconsumption and wasting. We bring theoretical ideas on material care into conversation with empirical material from Finland, a country in which discarding is made easy with functioning waste management infrastructures. We use the material to gain understanding of what material care means in practice for people in their everyday life. With selected insights from the material gathered through object-centred dialogues, we reflect on care ethics in action, showing how material objects themselves influence people's choices on care and how avoiding care is part of the ethical considerations. We also discuss affective and embodied relations with things and meaning-making and creativity as crucial elements of material care. Our finding is that people do care about their things even though consumerism suggests otherwise, and we propose that this is because material care is significant to people's wellbeing in ways that are rarely addressed in present policy frameworks. The value of thinking with material care lies in the alternative visions for sustainability that it allows to nurture.

  4. Geometric ornament as material knowledge: Ethnogeometric structures of spatial cognition
    Balnur Amantay, Manifa Sarkulova, İhsan Çapcıoğlu · online 2026-10-14
    摘要

    This article proposes a method for analysing recurring geometric relationships in Chalcolithic Botai material culture in northern Kazakhstan. Ornamented artefacts, dwelling architecture and settlement layout are examined without assuming a single symbolic code or historical continuity. The methodology integrates ethnogeometry, Material Engagement Theory, the enactive signification hypothesis and Peircean and material semiotics. Analysis proceeds through four levels of inference, from observable material features to culturally specific interpretations. Results show that comparable geometric relationships are materialised differently through production, object orientation, bodily interaction, movement and spatial organisation. The unit of comparison is not a motif but a geometric relationship arising within a specific material practice. Formal similarity alone cannot establish ritual or cosmological meaning. Comparative cases from Sakha culture and Çatalhöyük are used asymmetrically to distinguish evidence-based conclusions from speculation. The article therefore treats geometric ornament as a form of embodied material knowledge while defining explicit evidential limits for archaeological interpretation.

  1. Labor-power and precolonial Africa: Nyerere, Meillassoux, Marx
    Hylton White · pp. 489-504 · online 2026-02-03
    摘要

    That people are wealth is a core concept in the history and anthropology of Africa. A claim about value in precolonial social orders, it has also been mobilised to explain economic development in colonial and postcolonial settings. Here I revisit two twentieth-century efforts to link wealth-in-people to economic modernisation. One was made by Nyerere, who argued that precolonial kin-based commitments to work cooperatively were a developmental resource that could substitute for capitalist investment. The second is from Meillassoux, who argued that the persistence of precolonial kin-based production facilitated capitalist development by affording a double-exploitation of labor. The arguments reached different conclusions but relied on a common premise: precolonial wealth-in people was premised on the capacity to labor, and that was its actual or possible contribution to modern development. This premise is anachronistic. The valuation of people as vessels of labor-power is particular to the capitalist social order. It is unlike the valuation of people as persons-in-relationship.

    Dhiren Swain, Santhosh Abraham · pp. 535-553 · online 2026-02-23
    摘要

    Building on the critical scholarship in urban studies about intermediaries, this paper explores the relationship between intermediaries and urban land struggles. Intermediaries as enablers of land tenure change contest land’s bureaucratic categories. They challenge the state categories of land through a narrative mode and in an archival mode. The narrative mode comprises oral-historical knowledge of the settling in of people in the Basti, creating occupancies and translating those oral narratives of land use change into paper documents. The archival mode constitutes the incremental archives sustained by intermediaries, including old record maps, accumulated historical written artefacts, and quotidian paper traces. The paper argues that these two modes co-produce knowledge categories and histories of these forgotten places, like Bastis. The oral narrative articulation by intermediaries helps the inhabitants effectively publicize historical land tenure change. The incremental archives complement and validate those claims through old paper archival artefacts. These processes, in effect, contest local bureaucratic categories to resist eviction and reinforce the tenure changes. The paper focuses on the often-overlooked tier-II cities within southern urban theories. The study also contributes to the research on intermediaries and urban-focused public policies.

  10. Jacobinism, capitalism, and modernity in post-ottoman Turkey, Greece, and Cyprus: a review essay on the “Düzgün Debate”
    Nicos Trimikliniotis · pp. 651-660 · online 2026-04-29
    摘要

    This review essay revisits the debates generated by Eren Düzgün’s Capitalism, Jacobinism and International Relations: Revisiting Turkish Modernity and argues that their significance extends beyond Turkish studies. It shows how Düzgün’s historically specific definition of capitalism—as generalized market dependence rooted in particular social-property relations—opens space for understanding Jacobinism as a distinct, non-capitalist route to modernity. The essay also examines the main criticisms directed at Düzgün, suggesting that many do not fully engage his central conceptual claims. It then turns to Greece and Cyprus not to classify them definitively, but to explore the broader comparative questions opened by Düzgün’s framework. If Turkey appears as the strongest case of sustained Jacobin substitution, Greece and Cyprus are approached more tentatively as cases that complicate and probe the limits of that framework: Greece raises the possibility of a powerful Jacobin ideological legacy without a fully Jacobin state form, while Cyprus points to a more fragmented terrain shaped by colonial modernization, uneven capitalist tendencies, and episodic Jacobin currents. Taken together, these cases suggest that modernity did not unfold along a single capitalist path and that its historical trajectories remained more open, uneven, and contested than conventional narratives allow.

  1. Conceptual colour: race, economic knowledge, and the anthropology of financialization
    Kimberly Chong · pp. 769-788 · online 2026-03-25
    摘要

    Economic anthropologists now carry out fieldwork in settings for which the ethnographic method was never designed, amongst powerful financial actors who are notoriously difficult to access, and in contexts which transcend geographical boundaries. This has engendered a re‐orientation of anthropology, to consider not only the economic lives of people but also the social life of devices and concepts. Drawing on ethnographic readings of corporate documents as well as fieldwork with various fee‐earning intermediaries, this article looks at two concepts, ‘emerging markets’ and ‘human capital’, that are directed towards the making of investability in financial imaginaries and thus play a critical role in the production of financialization. Although often portrayed as neutral forms of knowledge, such concepts of economy, it is argued, are implicated in processes of racialization and that financialization is enacted through and produces racial forms. Responding to recent calls to attend to the legacy of empire and colonialism in financial capitalism, and inspired by the Black Radical Tradition, this article shows how colonial ideas of racial ordering are incorporated into these economic concepts. Thus, the invocation of these concepts, which play a vital role in creating legitimacy for intermediaries and their interventions, entrenches racializing dynamics of financialized capitalism.

    pp. 789-791 · online 2026-07-08

  5. Liquidity, the makings of India's emerging economic body
    Ikuno Naka · pp. 796-814 · online 2026-05-09
    摘要

    Under Narendra Modi's premiership, India's economy is rapidly being ‘dematerialized’ towards a digital economy: ‘faceless, paperless, cashless’ in his words. At first glance, it captures the quintessence of free laissez‐faire market reforms, but I argue there is more to Modi's project of liquidity. This paper re‐reads the re‐engineering of India's economy, arguing that this idea of liquidity is what holds together in conflicting tension the polarities for de‐regulated free laissez‐faire market reforms with a re‐sacralized and protectionist nationalism. Grounded in the stories of local builders working the ‘spatial fix’, the multi‐sited ethnography recovers how money is more than some medium of exchange or vessel for abstract value. It is itself constitutive of shared dispositions, meanings, understandings, a vernacular, all in service towards a conceived ideal commons. Reframing our understanding of modern money thus, the paper therefore argues that Modi's monetarist reforms are an assertion for a different kind of collective existence – a re‐sacralized socio‐spatial project wherein membership demands the reconfiguration of self and of relations, as these builders experience first‐hand. The economy is never just about economics; it is inherently political and indelibly personal.

  6. Nationals through tax: claiming citizenship as fiscal subjects in Latvia and Estonia
    Marija Norkunaite · pp. 815-832 · online 2026-04-14
    摘要

    This article follows the efforts of Russian‐speaking (non‐)citizens to join the national Latvian or Estonian collectives, primarily as fiscal subjects. The Russian‐speaking residents of Daugavpils in Latvia and Sillamäe in Estonia, who feel excluded to various degrees, use their taxpayer subjectivity to claim (full) membership in the political community as good, deserving, and loyal citizens. In the popular and scholarly imaginations, taxation and citizenship are often inextricably linked. This article looks at how Russian‐speaking (non‐)citizens employ taxes to navigate national and neoliberal ideals of citizenship in Latvia and Estonia, and why they focus on taxes in particular. It also asks how effective taxes are in accessing (full) political membership in Latvia and Estonia. In doing so, I build upon and further contribute to the growing body of anthropological research on tax, arguing that the actual relationship between citizenship and taxation is always contingent on the political context, and should be studied and ethnographically unpacked rather than presumed and taken for granted.

  7. The birth of an earth being: ‘Rights of nature’ in Brazilian Amazonia and elsewhere
    Aparecida Vilaça · pp. 833-851 · online 2026-03-18
    摘要

    In June 2023, the Laje River, located in the traditional territory of the Wari’ Indigenous people in Rondônia, Brazil, was declared a legal entity, an earth being , with rights, following the co‐ordinated action of an indigenous councillor and non‐indigenous activists. This made the Laje the first river in Brazilian Amazonia to attain such a legal status. In this article, I explore the different conceptions of earth being that characterize, on one hand, the indigenous perspective, and, on the other, the perspective of the non‐indigenous actors involved in the legislative process. Similar cases in South America and New Zealand are compared, seeking a broader understanding of the equivocations that arise in the encounter between societies that accept instability and the possibility of an alternation between ontologies as positive factors and those societies more oriented towards dogmatism and a unity of perspectives.

  8. The choice to submit: freedom, gender, and the figure of God in Pentecostal Nigeria
    Naomi Richman · pp. 852-868 · online 2026-03-12
    摘要

    Why do some women choose to submit to their husbands in marriage? In anthropology, the paradox of ‘chosen submission’ has famously been explored by Saba Mahmood. Her work amongst Egyptian women donning the veil in the Islamic da'wa movement spotlights the notion of ‘piety’ to explore how devotion to God can act as a powerful motivator of human behaviour. This article pushes this line of argument further by seeking to make God a more central figure in anthropological analysis, following recent efforts in the field to create both ‘ethnographies of God’, as well as anthropologies that are ‘theologically engaged’. Drawing on ethnographic research amongst Nigerian Pentecostal women, this article illustrates the ways that marital submission is understood by these Christians as a spiritual state that connects them to God – a theological relation , in other words – as well as a social relation between them and their husbands. By analysing their theologies of freedom and submission, this article shows how theological thinking more generally can shape how power is conceived and wielded by religious subjects.

  9. Singing to the saint: labour of healing at Jaunpur dargahs
    Smita Tewari Jassal · pp. 869-887 · online 2026-03-18
    摘要

    On visiting Sufi saint shrines in India, one encounters terminologies reminiscent of legal courts where protocols facilitate active engagement of pilgrims in their own healing. With practices that induce participation in the social world of dargahs, pilgrims not only seek the miracles of saints for material, spiritual, and bodily transformation, but pro‐actively ‘work’ to materialize blessings. Based on ethnographic fieldwork at three shrines in Jaunpur, Uttar Pradesh, I argue that the exhausting work of singing to the ‘listening’ saint, is illustrative of ‘devotional labour’. Through a sung bayan , or witness statement, I show how people deploy devotional labour to achieve the desired restoration of health. The muakkil ’s ability to confer with possessing spirits, based on his former experience of possession, facilitates interpretation and legal clarification like that provided by solicitors, hence the demand for his expertise by the afflicted. Finally, that possession can be traced to the malicious intent of close relatives raises questions about dargahs as spaces for healing, and the internal and external transformations sought by shrinegoers estranged from familial and social environments. Centred on the category of healers at shrines, termed muakkils, this article seeks to advance ongoing discussions in the anthropology of healing in the subcontinental context.

  10. Conceptualizing moral migration: how disillusionment and the transnational right motivate migration to Russia
    Lauren Woodard · pp. 888-906 · online 2026-04-20
    摘要

    Russia is consistently a top migration destination. While most migrate to Russia from other post‐Soviet countries, a small but highly visible group of the Russian‐speaking diaspora has returned from Europe and North America. Lauded in Russian media as ‘ideological migrants’, their narratives at first glance echo those of the state as they claim to flee liberalism and seek freedom. Drawing on ethnographic encounters with some of these migrants in Russia and the United States since 2014, this article examines how disillusionment and geopolitical imaginaries intersect with transnational circulations of the far right to motivate migration. Through two case studies of men returning from the West, this article conceptualizes their migration as a form of moral migration, an ethical alignment of where one lives with one's values. Ethnographic attention to moral migration has implications for understanding how individuals weigh factors beyond economic and political calculations to plan their migration and to address growing disillusionment worldwide with cultural and political liberalism.

  11. Amidst (waste) abundance: world‐making and struggles in hosting a municipal landfill in the Bolivian lowlands
    Vanesa Martín Galán · pp. 907-926 · online 2026-03-12
    摘要

    In recent decades, solid waste has proliferated worldwide, becoming a pressing global issue. This article explores the role of Indigenous people dwelling within and upon emerging waste scenarios, with a specific focus on involved forms of sociality and ontological contestation. Drawing on the case of a municipal landfill sited on a Guarani community in Bolivia, it seeks to understand the social relations with humans and other‐than‐humans that are generated and through which its world‐making takes place. It introduces questions of alterity, dependency, and autonomy into the anthropological field of discard studies, shedding light on how waste may act as a generative force for Indigenous life projects amid dependencies, inequalities, and ontological differences. The article shows how living with the landfill involved the community in a complicated relation with the municipality. Despite the environmental degradation it caused, the landfill became a paradigmatic place of abundance that supported community well‐being through the creation of wealth. Moreover, it allowed the community to re‐create desired forms of relatedness and exert some degree of control over powerful Others (municipal authorities). Unfolding within the context of consumer disposal, the community's engagement with the landfill is part of a subordinating integration of Indigenous people into the global economy.

  12. The company you keep: becoming one(self) in an Indonesian convent
    Meghan Rose Donnelly · pp. 927-945 · online 2026-03-23
    摘要

    This article investigates companionate processes of self‐making in a religious community of Catholic nuns in eastern Indonesia. I argue that the sociality of the convent establishes a unique context for understanding the effects of one's company on processes of self‐becoming. Through two years of participating in convent life, and set against majority‐Muslim Indonesia's religious and ethnic diversity, I offer an experience‐near account of the complex ways that nuns negotiate self in the midst of others, each on her own journey of personal transformation. Inspired by concepts of community, intersubjectivity, and empathy, I propose company as a mid‐level analytic for anthropological investigations into self and sociality. By attending closely to the dynamics of ever‐shifting social groupings, company promises a better understanding of the transformational relationship between self and community.

  13. Boredom, despondency, and the scourge that lays waste at noon: an anthropology of acedia
    Richard D.G. Irvine · pp. 946-964 · online 2026-03-24
    摘要

    Attentive to the ways that inertia can take hold of life, Catholic monks recognize despondency as a potential not only within the monastery, but in contemporary society more widely. Such experiences are regularly mapped onto an understanding of what early Christian monks termed ‘acedia’ (a Greek term that can be translated as ‘lack of care’). Taking as my ethnographic starting point the ways in which monks of an English Benedictine monastery understood and narrated the struggle with acedia, I note how this insight has been shared as a matter of public significance: most proximately during the boredom and constraint of the lockdown in response to COVID, but also more generally as something embedded, unrecognized, in modern life. What, then, does it mean to consider acedia as an analytical category? I argue that identifying acedia not only centres despondency as an active presence in life, but refuses to privatize it and strip it of its social dimensions. This fundamental sociality is both in the interaction with the demon of acedia, moving across the boundaries of a porous mind; and also in the means to resist acedia through the stability of shared time and space.

  14. Editor's preface: AI and linguistic anthropology
    Dimitrios Theodossopoulos · pp. 965-966 · online 2026-08-14
  15. Introduction: Towards a linguistic anthropology of AI
    Webb Keane, Constantine V. Nakassis · pp. 967-982 · online 2026-04-13
    摘要

    This essay introduces the themed cluster of articles, ‘Towards a linguistic anthropology of AI’. The advent of artificial intelligence (AI), especially in large language models capable of producing coherent discourse mimicking conversational interaction, is exerting unprecedented pressure on prevailing concepts of language, personhood, and the human. This provides a challenge and opportunity for reflection, particularly for linguistic anthropology, that sub‐field centred on language as medium/practice of meaning‐making in social life. Moving beyond AI as communicating machine to explore communicative processes that structure AI as ideological imaginary and technological infrastructure, the articles in this cluster draw on linguistic anthropology to examine different facets of AI as people take it up, such as the features that invite users to endow chatbots with personhood (Keane), attempts to use AI to unlock animal communication (Handman), and research seeking linguistic biomarkers predictive of psychopathology (Semel). To contextualize the articles, this introduction offers some basic semiotic distinctions developed within linguistic anthropology needed to parse AI, with a focus on the concept of semiotic ideology. We then situate AI within a set of ideological genealogies – from Enlightenment visions of universal languages to the representationalism of modern linguistics – before turning to themes of transduction and semiosis beyond the human.

  16. ‘Everything is a signal’: speaking circuits and noisy signs in the making of language‐oriented AI
    Beth M. Semel · pp. 983-1002 · online 2026-04-20
    摘要

    Contemporary artificial intelligence (AI) technologies are often presumed to be capable of revealing unmediated truths about the world, including the truths language might hold, echoing the long‐standing assertion that language's primary function is to directly translate reality. These interrelated representationalist ideologies unite in vocal biomarker AI, a subarea of machine learning research and development dedicated to optimizing how the voices of people experiencing mental distress are interpreted. Although the premise behind vocal biomarker AI – to listen to the brain through the voice – assigns transcendent semiotic capacities to both AI and language, another story unfolds in practice. Drawing from fieldwork with vocal biomarker AI researchers, I show that researchers take the social, pragmatic, and political dimensions of language to be foundational to its computational modelling. This is because to gather the voice data necessary to their research, researchers must recruit human research subjects and make them speak, contending with the gaps between the subjects’ and their own expectations regarding the meanings and values that language can embody. By underscoring the centrality of disjuncture, maintenance, and partiality – i.e., transduction – in vocal biomarker AI, I counter hegemonic claims about the people behind AI systems and the systems’ abilities to do things with words.

  17. Animal translations: AI and the intelligibility of non‐human worlds
    Courtney Handman · pp. 1003-1021 · online 2026-04-14
    摘要

    Amid the general sense of worry that large language models will soon drown out human voices, some researchers are optimistic that machine learning will allow humans to listen to and understand animal voices to an unprecedented extent. As part of a broader project aimed at interspecies communication, a loosely connected set of animal behaviourists, AI engineers, and public‐facing animal advocates are working to comprehend ‘animal languages’ and thus to improve the lives of domesticated and wild species. Work in this field not only assumes a novel set of speakers (which can include, for some, plant as well as animal species), but it also contributes to a redefinition of what language is. This article examines how the expansion of language to non‐human animals is helping to produce posthuman worlds in which linguistic expertise is partially transferred from humans to machines. These new ways of defining language are especially visible in the forms of translation that people in the world of interspecies communication engage in that link animals, humans, and artificial intelligence. Repeating earlier moments in translation history, a new kind of linguistic expertise makes a new kind of translation possible.

  18. From talking tools to metahumans: social interaction, semiotic skill, and the authority of AI chatbots
    Webb Keane · pp. 1022-1039 · online 2026-04-10
    摘要

    What does it take to turn a tool into a talking tool and that into an ultimate authority? Generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) in its diverse forms, such as large language models (LLMs), is celebrated as a useful tool. But LLM‐based conversational agents, or chatbots, the software applications through which ordinary users are likely to engage with LLMs on a regular basis, can seem, for many, to harbour uncanny insights or transcendental knowledge whose sources are inexplicable – resembling what Marshall Sahlins called ‘metahumans’. The temptation to treat chatbots as metahuman is just an extreme case of a more general process. The inferences that chatbot users draw about GenAI's human or superhuman properties arise in part from the apps' role as an interlocutor in social interaction. Their authority draws on the pragmatics and semiotics of speech. Much as a prophet embodies and legitimates the authority of divinity, so chatbots can endow the profit‐seeking strategies of their corporate owners with a supernatural aura. That such effects can seem intuitively real is, in part, because humans and metahumans address one another on semiotically unequal grounds.

  19. Linguistic anthropology of AI: an afterword
    Ilana Gershon · pp. 1040-1048 · online 2026-04-19
    摘要

    This reflection on the themed cluster of articles on linguistic anthropology and AI addresses what social anthropologists might wish to know about the linguistic anthropological analytical toolkit when turning to large language models (LLMs) as objects of analysis. In particular, this afterword discusses what agency might be understood to be when the scholarly attention is focused largely on how people use communicative repertoires to accomplish complex social tasks with others. Building on how linguistic anthropologists understand human semiosis, the article makes apparent how human communicative agency differs from the communicative agency expressed by LLMs.

  29. Torres Colón, Gabriel Alejandro. Styles for flourishing: histories of survival in the racial niche. 304 pp., bibliogr. New York: Columbia University Press, 2024. $35.00 (paper)
    Constancio R. Arnaldo · pp. 1067-1068 · online 2026-08-04
    摘要

    ‘A racial niche is an adaptation of the social scientific concept of the human niche that allows us to approach people's experiences as nestled at multiple, intersecting levels’ (p. 13). Gabriel Torres Colón provides an understanding of how marginalized people initiate strategies for survival in the face of inequities as ‘styles for flourishing’ (p. 7). In other words, styles for flourishing highlight agency, at times overt resistance, and a much more nuanced approach to racialized living. Rather than simply studying race and racial performance as always in opposition to racism, Torres Colón asserts that the ‘performance of styles, embodied racial experiences are more than the oppressive weight of racism. Embodied racial experiences are regenerated into everyday efforts to survive social marginalization, which in time become histories of survival’ (p. 11). Through the theoretical framework of a racial niche, he examines the everyday, taken-for-granted performances of race, from boxing gyms in the Midwest, to capturing the lives of Afro-Puerto Ricans in Puerto Rico, to the political participation of Muslims in Cueta, Spain. Torres Colón argues that these spaces are where racial niches flourish and are important sites through which to understand how marginalized and racialized people navigate their social and political worlds that move beyond simple Left-Right political discourse. By focusing on three ethnographic sites, Torres Colón provides a much-needed analysis of how racial niches are transnational and diasporic, augmenting cross-racial and cultural perspective of understanding race and racial negotiations. In doing so, Torres Colón asserts that racial experiences must be in conversation not only with governmental policies and practices, and the ideologies that govern how race is understood, but also in the liminal space of racial niches. Torres Colón begins by taking on the intellectual tradition of racial theorization, an idea grounded in W.E.B. Dubois's and Lee Baker's theories of race as a social problem. The author then tackles the complexity of 1990s racial discourse, highlighting the conflicting insights of how scholars theorized racial experience and the intellectual chasm that exists in their frameworks. Torres Colón intervenes in racial debates about the ontology of race, how racial discourse offered by various anthropologists like Virginia Dominguez, Anne Stoler, and Lawrence Hirschfeld reveals an impasse in how we make sense of race. He seeks to find common ground by pointing to a liminal racial space that has been largely unexplored; through a pragmatistic theory of racial experience, which he posits includes both aesthetic and biocultural experience – that is, taking seriously sensorial aspects of the human experience – at the crossroads of structural and historical forces. A strength of Styles for flourishing lies in its ability to expand understandings of racial experience beyond simply examining the deleterious effects of racism. Torres Colón accomplishes this by leveraging interdisciplinary approaches to race. For example, rather than tackle racial experience as a response to racism's effects (e.g., anxiety, depression, and low self-esteem), he looks to youth development literature to explore what happens when racial/ethnic identity becomes central to one's identity. Centring one's racial/ethnic centrality creates positive self-esteem outcomes for some youth of colour, and thus, represents a strategy for them to adopt styles for flourishing. It is here where the racial niche is applicable. One clear example of this is in the chapter ‘Going to the Body’. Torres Colón captures how young Mexican American boxers embody a racial style that is informed by broad socio-cultural phenomena that boxers bring into the boxing ring. They apply the racial niche to make sense of their racial styles by adopting a ‘Mexican boxing style’, a gesture to a kind of relentless and physical boxing approach that evokes a working-class toughness. In terms of geographic region, it would have been helpful to situate the Midwest as a site of racial negotiation; between whiteness and racial brownness (and to a lesser degree, white masculinity), to understand how young Latino men navigate the Midwest region that, in the popular culture imagination, is understood and racialized as white. I wonder what his interlocutors – like Samuel, Smiley, and Ray – might have said if Torres Colón asked them about their experiences living in the Midwest and how their boxing style was imagined and practised in relation to their white counterparts. Ultimately, Styles for flourishing offers us an alternative pathway to how we might combat racism in ways that exceed traditional liberal democratic practices. Racial niches are one such way where experimentational coalitions can exceed, and therefore have the potential to transform, liberal democracy not simply for marginalized people, but for societies writ large. As Torres Colón writes, ‘styles for flourishing – is the most authentically democratic way to do right by the history of survival in the racial niche’ (p. 240).

  35. Folch, Christine. The book of yerba mate: a stimulating history. 272 pp., bibliogr. Princeton: University Press, 2024. £25.00 (paper)

  36. Muir, Sarah. Routine crisis: an ethnography of disillusion. 200 pp., bibliogr. Chicago: University Press, 2021. $30.00 (paper)
    Dolores Señorans · pp. 1081-1082 · online 2026-07-16
    摘要

    Routine crisis examines the aftermath of Argentina's social and economic collapse in 2001-2 through an ethnographic focus on the middle classes of Buenos Aires. Attentive to the textures of everyday speech, the book addresses the lingering experience of this crisis through an analysis of narratives about the crisis and its consequences, approached not simply as representations but as material practices through which a particular mode of historical consciousness takes shape. By tracing how these narratives – or, as Muir terms them, ‘crisis talk’ – circulate, sediment, and acquire force, she demonstrates how crisis becomes routinized as a condition of everyday life rather than a temporal rupture. In doing so, the book advances an anthropological intervention into how crisis can become an enduring social condition rather than an exceptional event. One of the book's key theoretical contributions lies in its identification of a distinctive chronotope of ‘routine crisis’, one that unsettles linear and teleological assumptions about time and its experience. In this chronotope, crisis is not an inflection point that catalyses change, and the future is imagined less as an open horizon than as a space of historical exhaustion, diminishing possibilities, and even the potential disappearance of the middle class as a social form. The analysis unfolds through an engagement with ‘crisis talk’ as a shared interpretive framework that constituted the middle class as a public, despite significant internal differences. Muir captures with notable precision and ethnographic detail the pervasive disposition towards critique, reflexivity, and frequent self-pity that animates casual conversation among porteños. As one interlocutor puts it, ‘we must critique everything, ourselves included, in order to find a way to live’ (p. 82). She shows how critique functioned as a practical disposition grounded in a suspicious interpretive mode, drawing on genres akin to psychoanalysis and conspiracy theory. This emphasis is central to her argument that disillusion arises not only from political economy alone, but from the interpretive labour of suspicion and its associated forms of critique. Subsequent chapters extend this analysis to money, corruption, and solidarity initiatives – set against clientelist political practices – to trace critique in action as it is articulated through folk theories and modes of ethical reasoning. One of the book's most original interventions is its sustained engagement with what Muir terms ‘radical negativity’. Far from being politically inert or merely melancholic, this negativity is shown to be productive, structuring social belonging and moral evaluation in the years following the crash. In contrast to influential accounts of the Argentine crisis that foreground the popular uprisings of 2001 as moments of hope and radical possibility (e.g. Sitrin, Dinerstein, Graeber, Gago), Routine crisis attends to a less examined yet widely shared sensibility organized around disillusionment, critique, and a sense of the ‘end of the future’. This sensibility, Muir argues, came to shape middle-class experience and a broader idiom of national belonging during a period when change appeared uncertain and perpetually deferred. One might object that the book's focus on the urban middle classes of Buenos Aires offers only a partial perspective on these historical events. Yet Muir clearly delineates the scope of her inquiry and convincingly demonstrates its social significance in the Argentine context. In particular, her attention to the classed and racialized undertones of her interlocutors’ narratives effectively captures their attachment to a spatialized, deeply European vision of modernity. The period under analysis (2003-7) coincided with the emergence of new political horizons alongside the pervasive disillusionment documented by the book. As economic conditions slowly improved under the commodity consensus, Kirchnerism – a renewed iteration of Peronism that remains a central political force – began to inspire a sense that the future could be different, including among segments of the middle classes considered here. Although this development is briefly addressed, a more detailed consideration would have strengthened the analysis, given the centrality of the possibility of imagining alternative futures to the argument and the book's conclusions. That said, the chronotope Muir describes resurfaced after the period of economic instability following the 2018 devaluation, allowing the book to be reread in light of later events, including the conjuncture opened by Javier Milei's more recent rise to the presidency. While grounded in linguistic anthropology, Routine crisis will be of interest to anthropologists concerned with economic anthropology, crisis and temporality, money, and corruption, as well as to those engaged in broader debates about historical consciousness and agency (or the lack thereof), and the affective dimensions of political economy.

  37. Moreno Figueroa, Mónica G. & PeterWade (eds). Against racism: organizing for social change in Latin America. 292 pp., illus., bibliogr. Pittsburgh: University of Pittsburgh Press, 2022. $60.00 (hardback)
    Claudia Fonseca Alfaro · pp. 1083-1084 · online 2026-07-16
    摘要

    Conceptualizing and examining racism and anti-racial struggle in a Latin American context is like walking through a hall of mirrors. Racism might or might not exist (according to popular imagination), racial subjects oscillate, and what can work as an anti-racist strategy can also act as an oppressive force. Such is the landscape that Against racism captures. Edited by Mónica G. Moreno Figueroa and Peter Wade, the volume is an impressive collective effort by a group of established scholars and early-career researchers who seek to understand anti-racist struggle across four mestizo societies. Contributing to the racism/anti-racism turn in Latin America, the collective engages with a myriad of actors in Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, and Mexico to present their rich empirical findings in unexpected ways. Making the analytical choice to put the four countries in conversation through a ‘relational comparison’ (p. 202) approach and, opting to find commonalities between Black and Indigenous struggles through an intersectional method, the team structures the book around four common threads. The themes – ‘the publicly mobilized body’ (chap. 3), ‘the struggle for territory’ (chap, 4), ‘the search for upward mobility’ (chap. 5), and ‘the mobilization of legal redress’ (chap, 6) – are accompanied by two sections, chapters 2 and 7, that help frame the overarching analysis. What we find is that mestizaje acts as ‘the friend or the foe of anti-racism’ in Latin America. The ideology and practice of mestizaje, broadly understood as ‘racial’ and cultural mixture, has to varying degrees been an element of nation-building and underpins the distinct racial formations of the countries examined. Under the banner of inclusion, mestizaje actually acts as a foe by minimizing racism and gravitating towards the erasure of Indigenous and Black identities. In short, blanqueamiento, or whitening. However, the practice of mestizaje also offers possibilities for anti-racist work. Mestizos, for example, can become allies, and actions that at first seem to have little to do with racism can be anti-racist in practice. The authors suggest an ‘alternative grammar of anti-racism’ as a tool to capture ‘elements that, when verbalized or excavated, do not seem to talk about anti-racism or even perhaps racism’ (p. 6). Widening the lens of what we consider to be an anti-racist action, the authors argue, expands the horizon of what can be achieved. This is not to deny the risk of tokenism or to diminish the vital task of addressing structural racism, but to point out that discarding strategies that do not seem radical enough is unhelpful to anti-racist work. While an ‘alternative grammar of anti-racism’ is an important conceptual contribution to the discussion of racism in Latin America – and other contexts that might suffer from what the authors refer to as ‘the myth of racial innocence’ (p. 156) – I was left with some questions. What are the tensions when researchers label an action through words that research participants and activists do not use themselves? Thinking with Spivak, can the subaltern speak? Are the racialized doomed to always speak ‘race’ even when they are talking about something else because of their positionality as, for example, an ‘Indigenous woman’ or a ‘Black mother’? Who is writing or crafting this ‘alternative grammar’? The racialized or the researchers? Given that the authors highlight the importance of the coloniality of power in the contexts under study, I wonder if the collective could have done more to expand on their call for social transformation with a postcolonial or decolonial approach. Tariq Jazeel's work on ‘singularity’, articulated in ‘Singularity: a manifesto for incomparable geographies’ (Singapore Journal of Tropical Geography, 40: 1, 2019), warns us of the danger of unintentionally distorting the particularities of subaltern narratives in the process of translation to our conceptual toolboxes. What alternative concepts to ‘racism’ and ‘anti-racism’ could we find if we were to surrender to the words of the subaltern, as Jazeel suggests? The Mixe writer and activist Yásnaya Elena A. Gil writes in Beyond Mestizaje: contemporary debates on race in Mexico (2024) that one of the challenges of fighting racism is ‘the existence of a single hegemonic mirror dictating what is worth reflecting’ (p. 150). Instead, what is needed is a mirror ‘carved and polished’ (p. 151) by the racialized. Although I would have liked to hear the voices of the activists, NGOs, and other actors that engaged with the authors more clearly, the strength of Against racism lies in its ambitious analysis and the door it opens to a ‘radical imagination’ (p. 194) built on intersectional anti-racist strategies.

  38. Nolan, Rachel. Until I find you: disappeared children and coercive adoptions in Guatemala. 320 pp., bibliogr. Cambridge, Mass.: Harvard University Press, 2024. £29.95 (paper)
    Nicole S. Berry · pp. 1085-1086 · online 2026-07-16
  39. Rasmussen, Nika. Embodied citizenship in the making: Bolivian urban youth at the crossroads of social hierarchies. 243 pp., bibliogr. Uppsala: Acta Universitatis Upsaliensis, 2021
    Jonathan Alderman · pp. 1087-1088 · online 2026-07-16
    摘要

    Nika Rasmussen's book explores how changing regimes of citizenship have been experienced by young people in the private and public spaces of urban Bolivian life during the government of Evo Morales (2006-19). Rasmussen conveys how the proceso de cambio (the slogan Morales's government used to describe their programme for government which put decolonization at the forefront) was embodied by teenagers in their daily lives. The book attempts to make comparisons between the experiences of plurinational citizenship for young people in the cities of La Paz and neighbouring El Alto, but is overwhelmingly written through the lens of ethnographic data that Rasmussen collected through fieldwork research (participant observation and interviews) with teenagers (mostly aged 16+) in a youth centre in El Alto. Rasmussen states that she was interested in how political changes which have sought directly to challenge racism and discrimination might impinge on young people's relationship to their own bodies. In exploring the relationship between national political changes and their effect on individual Bolivians, she captures many of the contradictions in the polices and rhetoric of Morales's government. Rhetorically, Evo Morales and his government claimed to represent el pueblo (the people), and the refunding of the state through its 2009 constitution as a plurinational state acknowledged the heterogeneity of the Bolivian people. Through his brown skin and humble origins, Morales himself embodied the proceso de cambio. However, as Rasmussen discusses in the book, despite decolonization supposedly including depatriarcalization, national-level politicians, including Morales himself, reinforced a masculine vision of citizenship and political participation through the public displays of machismo. She finds the young men and women in the youth centre rejecting and attempting to change rigid gender norms and dynamics but being met with resistance at home and school. She argues that these changing attitudes are citizenship practices because they influence how young people feel they belong in society. The book demonstrates a clear nexus between class, race, and geography in Bolivia. Meaning-making and belonging, Rasmussen argues, are shaped in relation to place. The brown-skinned, working-class Alteños are constructed as further away from embodied whiteness (and the connotations of attractiveness, and higher wealth and class) than youngsters living in La Paz, the city connected to El Alto by cable car. Family names typical in El Alto are regarded as less prestigious than those of the families in La Paz, and Rasmussen finds that this feeds through to young people's self-esteem and their ideas (which they acquire from their parents), or who they should aspire to date and marry. While she describes the proceso de cambio coming to be embodied in the cholita, an urban-based woman with indigenous roots identifiable through her pollera (a layered, pleated skirt), young people still associated female beauty with the white woman around which the nation-building project had been constructed in twentieth-century Bolivia. This is highlighted when an Alteño boy is laughed at by his peers for describing his ideal partner as a cholita. Embodied citizenship successfully conveys the practical difficulties of real decolonization. Bolivians were promised change, but structures don't change overnight. The young people with whom Rasmussen conducted research are growing up at a time of flux in the meaning of Bolivian nationhood and citizenship, and find their own subjectivities being forged by struggles against the norms of their parents regarding how their children should behave to fit in with perceived expectations around gender and sexuality and their racial-class position in society. The book also highlights the difficulties individual young people face in forging a path for themselves within these social structures. For example, one boy studying for a prestigious career describes how he feels he is in the wrong body to achieve his career aims. In showing how the macro politics of society are reproduced in the micro spaces of the home, school, and in everyday social relations, the book achieves its aim of showing how nation-making processes are bodily reproduced. As an ethnographic monograph describing how belonging is experienced and negotiated in the intimate spaces of everyday life, it could be read alongside Andew Canessa's Intimate Indigeneities: race, sex, and history in the small spaces of Andean life (Duke University Press, 2012), which focuses on rural life in the plurinational state. It is a worthwhile contribution to studies of twenty-first-century Bolivia, and will be of interest to researchers studying belonging, nation-making, and racial geographies.

  40. Verswijver, Gustaaf.The way of warriors: annotated narratives of the Mebengokre (Kayapo) in Brazil. 679 pp., bibliogr. Almeria: Turuti Books, 2023. R$319.80 (paper)
    Vanessa R. Lea · pp. 1089-1090 · online 2026-07-27
    摘要

    The book attests to a monumental effort of research and compilation, facilitated by Verswijver's command of the Mẽbêngôkre language and almost half a century of research (1974-2019). It provides an authentic collection of what the author designates as eighty-three historical narratives and life stories, focusing mainly on the 1930s to the 1960s. It is best read in tandem with Verswijver's previous book, The club-fighters of the Amazon: warfare among the Kayapo Indians of Central Brazil​ (Turuti Books, 2018), where he makes a minute analysis of the strategies and objectives of Mẽbêngôkre (Kayapó) warfare. The narratives in the latest book are presented without any intervention from the author (as if the text speaks for itself), except for prolific though somewhat repetitive notes, and a glossary of terms used in the text, providing a wealth of ethnographic detail. For anyone interested in kinship, it is frustrating that English terms such as cousins and brothers-in-law are used throughout the book despite lacking exact counterparts in the Mẽbêngôkre language. Verswijver aims to explain how the present-day configuration of the Mẽbêngôkre came about after the gradual break-up of their ancestral village from the turn of the twentieth century onwards. The author gives voice to twenty-four narrators whose memories could otherwise have been obliterated. Each narrator recounts incidents in which he was involved personally, thereby providing a sui generis portrait of both internal skirmishes and external warfare expeditions from the perspective of a generation of Mẽbêngôkre warriors who were gradually dying out whilst their stories were being collected. The narratives detail how the Mẽbêngôkre were compelled to attack the non-Indigenous population that encroached upon their land to obtain firearms and ammunition (besides other booty and the odd captive), with which to protect their villages from both non-Indigenous and Indigenous enemies, including other Mẽbêngôkre subgroups who had already acquired such weapons. Bows and arrows, wooden clubs, and bone-tipped lances were no match for firearms, so a tragic arms race ensued which had repercussions on both external violence and on the lethality of internal fighting. Even today, old grudges and the desire for revenge continue to smoulder beneath the surface of everyday life. This book makes a gruelling read due to seemingly endless and often gratuitous violence, with some gruesome episodes recounted by various narrators, leaving the reader wondering if he has already encountered some specific gory incident told by another narrator or whether it is an analogous episode to a previous one. It is debatable whether the Mẽbêngôkre could have survived until the present day if levels of internal homicide were on a par with that described in the book for the twentieth century, besides the mortal attacks from Indigenous enemy peoples and from the non-Indigenous population. Implicitly the book raises an important question: does it characterize an ontology of violence or rather a reign of terror instigated by the increasing population density that followed the arrival of the Portuguese colonists from the sixteenth century onwards and the ensuing native population displacements? Recent linguistic research indicates that a proto-Northern Jê language may go back some two thousand years, thus the period dealt with in the book alludes to a tiny fraction of Mẽbêngôkre history. The sophistication of Mẽbêngôkre social and ceremonial organization, their elaborate adornments and body paint designs, their complex and prolific kinship categories, besides the profound knowledge of their environment, suggest the possibility of a former heyday or belle époque when they could have flourished, if not without strife, at least on an attenuated level in comparison to that described in this book. The price of the book established by Amazon ensures that it is essentially a product for the Northern hemisphere. It is unfortunate that funds were unavailable for a revision of the English, this not being the author's first language. The Mẽbêngôkre are increasingly interested in reading what is written about them, and the results of an AI translation are unimaginable due to the errors, a bane of self-publishing. Information is lacking concerning the whereabouts of the transcription of the narratives in Mẽbêngôkre and in Portuguese that are mentioned in the book. One of the narrators, the much-acclaimed leader Raoni, has just launched a book in Brazil of his memoires, in Portuguese, giving any interested scholar the opportunity to verify the congruity of style across these two publications. Verswijver does not exaggerate the violence but his book deals almost exclusively with it, whereas Raoni also recounts shamanic journeys and other episodes which alleviate the atmosphere.

    Meghna Roy · pp. 1093-1094 · online 2026-07-16

  44. Decoteau, Claire Laurier. The Western disease: contesting autism in the Somali diaspora. 272 pp., bibliogr. Chicago: University Press, 2021. $35.00 (paper)
