  45. Fiks, Eva. State intimacies: sterilization, care and reproductive chronicity in rural North India. 242 pp., bibliogr. Oxford: Berghahn Books, 2024. £104.00 (hardback)
    Anindita Majumdar · pp. 1099-1100 · online 2026-07-16
    摘要

    In Eva Fiks's book on the infrastructures of family planning and population control in Rajasthan, North India, the state is an active participant in the ways in which intimacy is structured and experienced. Through the idea of ‘reproductive chronicity’, Fiks discusses how Adivasi women in North India engage with the reproductive ‘tolls and temporalities’ (Bledsoe, Contingent lives: fertility, time, and aging in West Africa, 2002) by seeking out sterilization in government-organized mass family planning camps. Across eight chapters, the author discusses her rich ethnographic findings bringing together people, places, tools, cultures, and practices that help us think through sterilization, care, and chronicity from a fresh new perspective. At the centre of Fiks's ethnography are three elements that define Indian public healthcare practices in family planning and reproductive health interventions: the family planning camp, the laparoscope, and the vernacular colloquial term ‘jugad’. Jugad (chap. 4), variously identified as ‘quick fix’, or ‘improvised innovations’ for resource-poor settings (p. 121), haunts the practice of sterilization or tubal ligations in family planning camps organized by the Indian state to facilitate quick, one-time interventions for women through laparoscopic or ‘key hole’ surgeries. In the book, jugad is not presented as the wonderful Indian culture of innovation, but a source of distress and constant manoeuvrings around medical safety protocols. Particularly distressing are the ethnographic details the author shares in chapter 1 on the family planning camps recounting the pain of undergoing tubal ligations amongst poor Adivasi women, and the apparent violence and disrespect meted out to these women during the procedure. Run on the basis of an incentivized plan of recruiting women into participating in the camps, the focus is on quick fixes – most surgeries carried out at any one time. Through seasonal circulations of camps, women come to participate in laparoscopic procedures (chap. 2), locally known as the karantwala (electric) or durbinwala (telescopic) operation (p. 84). Local perceptions regarding laparoscopy often associate it with the production of body heat (due to the use of electricity), that can be balanced only through the intervention of other cooling factors, such as the weather, leading to the sterilization camps being held in winter between September and April. Despite the pain and anxiety surrounding the surgery and its operationalization in the family planning camps, most of the women in rural Rajasthan come there to escape physical debility incurred through repeated pregnancies, birthing and miscarriages. In a paradox of sorts, sterilization comes across as an ‘act of care’ and self-preservation. The act of care is also part of the otherwise alienating and violent laparoscopic tubal ligation – embedded in the way some ‘motivators’ counsel and provide emotional support pre- and post-surgery. Fiks identifies ‘motivators’ (chap. 3) as community health workers or ANMs (auxiliary nurse midwives) who are essential within the public health care system in India, especially in terms of providing reproductive health services, and nutritional support to women and children in rural India. However, in Fiks's study the ANM is also the motivator who coaxes rural and Adivasi women to undergo tubal ligations in the family planning camps, by staying in touch with her intimate reproductive biography. Motivators remain essential, yet at an inferior position in the otherwise hierarchized state-funded access to reproductive care. Paperwork and bureaucracy mark the sterilization camps with negotiated forms of informed consent (chap. 1). Fiks finds the seeking and recording of consent procedures deeply problematic, with some motivators and state functionaries providing minimal information regarding the procedure and focusing instead on pain management through embodied silences. At the crux of these procedures are social prejudices against Adivasi women and men and their social mores, which impact the ways in which the women frequenting the camps are treated. Fiks provides a nuanced portrayal of the varied caste and class cleavages that operate in the enforcement of sterilization services to poor, tribal women in rural Rajasthan. Dominant class and caste women and men occupy positions of power including that of the surgeon operating the laparoscopy, and other medical functionaries supporting the sterilization process. Reproductive chronicity emerges from the problematics of intimate manoeuvrings that poor rural and Adivasi women must manage in Rajasthan, including abusive husbands, lack of proper contraception, and son preference. Within such a space, the state may step in as ‘protector’ and ‘emancipator’, but enacts its own violence through sterilization camps, and the ‘surveillance’ of reproductive intimacies.

    Thomas W. Murphy · pp. 1107-1108 · online 2026-07-16
  50. Crockford, Susannah. Ripples of the universe: spirituality in Sedona, Arizona. 248 pp., bibliogr. Chicago: University Press, 2021. $33.00 (paper)
    Josh Brahinsky · pp. 1109-1110 · online 2026-07-17
  51. Davé, Naisargi N.Indifference: on the praxis of interspecies being. 200 pp., bibliogr. Durham, N.C.: Duke University Press, 2023. $25.95 (paper)
    Ambika Aiyadurai · pp. 1111-1112 · online 2026-07-16
    摘要

    Naisargi Davé’s book, Indifference: on the praxis of interspecies being, offers a novel approach to understanding interspecies relations, particularly the care of animals, through the concept of indifference. Davé views indifference as a means of respecting others in their otherness that has the capacity to create space for both humans and nonhumans to thrive. Highlighting how the ethic of indifference operates in multiple zones of human-animal relationality, the book is exceptional in providing ethnographic stories that are both disturbing and hopeful. The ethical questions that permeate the book are articulated using a compelling narration of acts of care and violence, which dwell on how ‘humans and animals live and die in a shared world’ (p. 10). Strongly grounded in empirical fieldwork conducted in thirteen Indian cities and a parallel autobiographic journey, the book closely examines interspecies ethics in each of its chapters, offering ‘Indifference’ as a mode of relationality and a surprisingly respectful way to live with and care for animals on a day-to-day basis. The already complex world of human-animal relations is further entangled, as Davé examines the challenges of animal activism in contemporary India. For example, cow protection, often carried out in the name of animal welfare, is often exclusively focused on the cow as a potent ‘weaponized symbol’ that divides those who eat cow meat (Muslims, Dalits, tribal people, and Christian minorities) and those who do not, predominantly Hindu dominant castes (p. 16). Spending considerable lengths of time with volunteers in animal shelters, animal welfarists, and animal healers who are constantly attending to and treating injured and helpless stray and wild animals, Davé brings forth examples of care, belongingness, listening, touch, etc., with great sensitivity, and often with a touch of humour, but never completely diverting the reader's attention away from the grim reality of animal lives in chicken farms and animal shelters. Spread over eight chapters and an introduction, the volume is rich with the fruits of engaging and challenging fieldwork as well as the author's careful contemplation of ethical dilemmas and questions. I was particularly drawn to chapter 3, ‘Contradiction: how is the otherwise exhausted?’, which is about the healing of stray dogs, focusing on the tireless efforts of volunteers who work to rescue these animals with deep care, in a process that is also rife with contradictions. Davé’s writing is mindful of this world of contradictions in the life of a volunteer who must both heal an ailing dog and also kill the maggots on the dog's body to enable that healing. They are often faced with questions like these: If you are a vegetarian, then how can you wear shoes made of animal skin? Or in another context, if you are working in the field of wildlife conservation, how can you be a non-vegetarian? For Davé, such relentless questioning is sometimes also a means ‘to make people tired so that they give up’ and for ‘the world [to stake] a moral claim on you’ (p. 59). After all, such contradictions are innumerable, not easy to recognize, and also difficult to understand or analyse. Davé’s writing about such ‘contradiction thinking’ is presented with a clarity and sharpness of articulation that it is rare in academic discussions. This chapter, and in fact, the entire book, is an exercise in recognizing, thinking through, and trying to understand such contradictions and ethical challenges in the lives of people and their relations to animals. The fifth chapter, Interlude: take a walk with me, takes the reader to the streets and alleyways of Mumbai in the footsteps of an animal healer, chronicling the day-to-day care of stray dogs and cats. For me, this chapter is central to the book's theme, reflecting ‘the praxis of the interspecies being’ as articulated by its sub-title. This chapter can serve as a case study for making field notes, emphasizing the power of observation and participation, and, above all, being reflexive while immersed in the fieldwork. Davé writes with empathy for all. Not only for the animals but also for the humans, be they animal rescuers, workers, or activists. It is not easy to constantly witness death and suffering in animal shelters, abattoirs, and streets, and the reader is exposed to episode after episode of pain and horror in the field, which are nevertheless also rich with suggestions of new ways for humans and animals to live together. The reader is made to feel as if they were walking with Davé through the field sites, meeting the activists and animal welfarists, and being privy to her thoughts, inner debates, frustrations, and dissatisfactions. Davé is in constant conversation with herself, with the reader, and with the humans and animals she encounters in the field, and this book is an invitation to new ways of thinking about interspecies relations. The book is equally valuable for students of the anthropology of ethics, critical animal studies, and multispecies studies. A thoroughly well-researched and well-referenced work with detailed footnotes, this is an in-depth and riveting ethnography that future researchers in this field are bound to find immensely helpful.

  52. Keane, Webb. Animals, robots, gods: adventures in the moral imagination. 192 pp., bibliogr. London: Penguin Books, 2025. £10.99 (paper)
    Pablo N. Rojas‐Bahamonde · pp. 1113-1114 · online 2026-07-16
  53. Manceron, Vanessa. Wild and wonderful: an ethnography of English naturalists (trans. Michael Taylor). 260 pp., bibliogr. Chicago: Hau Books, 2025. £25.00 (paper)
    D.A. Villar · pp. 1115-1116 · online 2026-07-16
    摘要

    I do not write this review as an anthropologist, but as a member of the tribe which Manceron is writing about, one of that legion of strange Britons who spend our time obsessing over the minutiae of the natural world. Unlike the majority of the ones whom Manceron meets during the course of her ethnography, I have been lucky enough to turn this obsession into a profession as an ecologist, but like every ecologist I know, the germ of my career comes not from mathematical models, but from a love of the natural world. Manceron captures excellently that love of nature which every naturalist has, as well as the sadness we have at its despoilation. This book succeeds in making the familiar strange. I found myself repeatedly realizing that Manceron captured things that I as a naturalist did without thinking, or commenting about aspects of British life which I wouldn't have thought needed an explanation. I was particularly struck by finding out that there is no French equivalent of the local natural history club, of mass participation in the Wildlife Trust volunteering scheme, or in the British Trust for Ornithology (BTO) bird ringing scheme. I found Manceron's description of the genesis of a naturalist particularly perceptive – her description of the early entrancement with some aspect of the natural world as a child could have come from my own autobiography. As an ethologist, my favourite part of the book is the chapter ‘Wonderful creatures’, where Manceron describes how dedicated ornithologists can become ethnographers of the natural world. Much of that chapter is spent with Robin, a buzzard expert who knows the individual idiosyncrasies of the birds he watches every day. He names them, and in his scientific papers describes their behaviour in a purposeful and emotional language. This is very different from the usual experimental, and often dry, descriptions of animal behaviour published in leading journals. Not that emotion is absent from ethology – von Uexküll insisted ethologists study the animal's Umwelt nearly a century ago, and animal emotion and personality is a thriving topic within ethology and behavioural ecology. Despite the title, Manceron focuses on mainly three types of naturalists – ornithologists, botanists, and entomologists. Of the three, the first two get the bulk of her attention, and much of the book focuses on comparing the two. This is perfectly understandable, and defensible. The three of them account for well over 90 per cent of the naturalists in Britain. However, it would have been nice to see coverage of some of the more obscure naturalist groups which dot the landscape, studying less charismatic taxa. Perhaps I say that because, while I am now an ornithologist, the spark of childhood interest in nature that led me to the career I have now wasn't birds or plants, but seashells and conchology. As wonderful as this book is, there are some places where I think there are a few cases where a biological factchecker would have made the book stronger. On page 113, Manceron says that a naturalist sees ‘penguins’ in Shetland, and while penguin is an old name for auks and puffins (the extinct great auk having the Latin name Pinguinus impennis), nowadays the term refers to birds of the family Spheniscidae, almost all only found in the Southern Hemisphere. She probably meant puffin. There is also a reference, on page 125, to ‘the new paradigm of “cladistics” or “phylogeny”, as the system is termed, which research laboratories and the academic world put into circulation in the 1990s’. Cladistics means taxonomy based on evolutionary descent, while phylogeny refers to the evolutionary tree of life. The former has been the main way taxonomy has been done since Willi Hennig's work in the 1950s and 1960s, and the latter has been a common term in biology since the nineteenth century. However, these small errata do not detract from what is otherwise an excellent book. And this is indeed an excellent book. In welcome contrast to many other academic tomes, Manceron's writing is both a pleasure to read and a fount of information. I could see myself in many of the people she interviewed, and no doubt anyone keen on nature will be inspired by the naturalists’ dedication to do some more observations of nature themselves. I highly recommend anyone interested in natural history to read this book.

  1. Editor's note
    Dimitrios Theodossopoulos · online 2026-10-24
    摘要

    The pieces in this section trace the unfolding of a debate that began with David Wengrow and David Graeber's redeployment of Gregory Bateson's concept of schismogenesis to explain cultural differentiation among Indigenous societies of the Pacific Coast of North America: the possibility that neighbouring societies may develop distinctive political, ethical, and social forms partly through processes of interaction, differentiation, and rejection. Eric Alden Smith and Brian F. Codding challenge this interpretation, foregrounding instead ecological variation, resource distribution, and patron–client dynamics; David Wengrow responds in defence of the schismogenetic argument, while Anna Marie Prentiss and Bill Angelbeck develop, from different perspectives, arguments that lend weight to the respective sides of the debate. The exchange takes place productively at the intersection of archaeology and social anthropology and forms part of JRAI's continuing effort to strengthen the conversation between them. From a theoretical and methodological perspective, what is particularly instructive is not the prospect of resolving the disagreement, but of seeing what happens when different explanatory orientations are brought to bear on the same ethnographic and archaeological record: ecological constraints and resource relations on the one hand, intercultural interaction, ethical differentiation, and political agency on the other. The resulting disagreement illustrates how productive scholarly argument can make visible the assumptions and interpretative possibilities of competing approaches – and, I hope, offers students and established scholars alike an invitation to think across the boundaries between archaeological and social anthropological explanation.

  2. Reassessment of the morphological evidence for hominin hybrids in the Levant
    Suzy White, Chris Stringer · online 2026-10-14
    摘要

    The Levantine hominin fossil and archaeological records have played a central role in long‐standing palaeoanthropological debates over origin models and possible interbreeding between Neanderthals ( Homo neanderthalensis ) and Homo sapiens . Recent research has provided a strong framework for the morphological expectations of recent hybrid ancestry in primates, along with renewed suggestions that early Late Pleistocene hominins from the Levant show morphological evidence of hybridization. Here we address these developments by using established diagnostic features of H. sapiens and H. neanderthalensis to review possible fossil evidence of hybridization from the key Levantine sites of Qafzeh, Skhūl, and Tabūn. We conclude that, while individuals from these sites show a perplexing combination of characteristics that broadly overlap with expectations of hybridization, this explanation can only be established once other alternatives have been ruled out, or through the recovery of genetic material from these key fossils.

    Deborah Puccio-Den's book Mafiacraft is an attempt to approach the (Sicilian) Mafia through their silences.Compared to much of the existing scholarship, the study represents an innovative contribution to the anthropology of the Mafia, taking a fresh perspective on this notoriously opaque phenomenon.A key reason for this lies in the conceptual shift away from viewing the Mafia as a clearly bounded social organization and towards understanding it as a phenomenon fundamentally constituted through silence, ambiguity, and unspeakability.Puccio-Den coins the term 'Mafiacraft' to capture this novel ethnographic approach and analytical framework.Drawing explicitly on anthropological studies of witchcrafttraditionally linked to the power of words -she inverts this comparison to emphasize the lethal power of silence.She understands silence not as mere absence of words but as a productive force, a form of agency, and a regime of action that sustains the Mafia's social and political realities.Silence, in Puccio-Den's account, is central to the Mafia's existence, its practices and forms of communication, and dealing with this silence by legal actors and anti-Mafia movements has also promoted forms of knowledge production and public discourse on the subject.

    Far exceeding the scope of a mere institutional chronicle, Erik Petschelies's new book is a profound dissection of how German ethnological thought shaped the understanding of alterity within Lowland South America.Petschelies argues that German ethnology was not a byproduct of late-stage colonialism but a maturation of Enlightenment and Romantic philosophies, specifically rooted in the Humboldtian premise of 'recognizing unity within multiplicity'.Influenced by Leibniz's linguistic-historical programme, this tradition prioritized language and material culture over biological reductionism or craniometry, seeking to comprehend the universal dimensions of the human psyche rather than proving racial inferiority.A significant milestone in this lineage is Johann Gottfried von Herder, whose concept of the Volk allowed for the valuation of non-European cultures on their own merits, viewing Indigenous peoples as unique, contemporary manifestations of creativity rather than 'living fossils'.This perspective was later solidified by the Humboldt brothers, who provided the empirical rigor and linguistic tools necessary to classify Brazil's Indigenous groups through a critical nexus between culture and environment.

  2. Introduction to “Synesthetic Encounters”
    Aliyah B.D. Dewar, Juliet Pascal Glazer · pp. 277-284 · online 2026-06-25
    摘要

    The articles in this issue examine activity routines in which sensory experiences form the basis of both practical and social coordination. In the ethnographic phenomena that the authors examine, successful coordination between actors almost always requires engagement across multiple senses and practices of sensory expertise, as well as between discursive and non-discursive signs. It is in this spirit that we offer the organizing concept of synesthetic encounters. We advocate for an ethnographic approach to language and synesthetic encounters that foregrounds: (1) a situated practice analysis of the senses, (2) attention to the continuum between improvisation and conventionalization in the use of lexicons for sensory calibration, and (3) the metapragmatic regimentation of sensory experience in specific contexts toward specific ends.

  3. Scatological Encounter: Scatoglossia, Adjectival Deixis, and Referential Practice across Sino-Tibetan Inter-Cultural Science Collaboration
    Xiao Schutte Ke · pp. 285-306 · online 2026-07-03
    摘要

    What if linguistic anthropological studies of sensory evaluation began not with wine connoisseurship but with scat identification? Focusing on Sino-Tibetan community-science collaborations on the Tibetan Plateau, this paper examines wildlife scat—a crucial indicator for Indigenous experts and conservation biologists. In everyday life, Tibetans and Han Chinese rarely agree on shit ; yet, collaborative scat identification sustains a scientific chronotope that suspends macro-level ethnonational frictions. Analyzing an English-language scatological manual alongside a Mandarin-Amdo Tibetan interactional transcript, I investigate the specialized linguistic repertoire used to calibrate scat observation. By examining how speakers simultaneously describe perception (“sensation”) and judge species origin (“ontology”), I argue that adjectival predicates function as dynamic interactional operators—a phenomenon I term adjectival deixis. Ultimately, this paper delineates two core functions of adjectival deixis: chronotope projection and referent configuration. In doing so, it reveals how referential practice drives intersubjective sensory calibration and rapport-making, co-constituting language-use and materiality.

  4. Timbre Beyond Words: Communication about Sound in a Violin Crafter’s Workshop
    Juliet Pascal Glazer · pp. 307-327 · online 2026-07-03
    摘要

    Violinists rely on violin crafters, or luthiers, to adjust their instruments’ acoustics. To do so, luthiers not only alter violins’ material forms but also talk with violinists about perceived and desired timbres—qualities of sound that lack standards of measure and are notoriously difficult to describe. This paper undertakes a linguistic anthropological analysis of a violin adjustment session in the Northeastern United States, showing that despite such difficulties, luthiers communicate successfully with violinists about timbre through situated practice. While luthiers sometimes use standard, enregistered cross-modal or synesthetic metaphors for timbre that can be analyzed in terms of lexical semantics, I emphasize that they also improvise on a wider repertoire of emergent and embodied semiotic strategies. By listening beyond the lexical and the enregistered, this paper synthesizes an interdisciplinary approach for research on communication about timbre, sound, and the senses.

  5. From Berimbolo to Bolo-Player: Kinesic Enregisterment and Motion Text in the Jitsuverse
    Jay Ke-Schutte · pp. 328-347 · online 2026-07-09
    摘要

    This paper explores the emergence of the bolo-player as a robust icon of personhood (Carr (2010)) in urban jiujitsu training rooms between China and the United States. I trace how this figure emerges as a kinesically enregistered stereotype (cf. Agha (2007)and Cohen (2021)) within a contrapuntal ecology of motion texts, from its mass-mediated origo: the berimbolo. Here, the berimbolo, or “scramble” in Brazilian Portuguese slang, describes a combination of movements in jiujitsu that—over a few generations—has mutated from an unintelligible zone of action leading to an inexplicable position of dominance for the player executing it; to a now highly choreographed repertoire of movements that have come to stand for an entire synesthetic ethics and personhood of bolo-ness.

  6. Disgust and Disdain in Meat Interactions in Mumbai: Halal Practice, Industrial Meat Production, and Urban-Spatial Configuration
    Shaheed Tayob · pp. 348-363 · online 2026-05-26
    摘要

    In Mumbai, the live slaughter of meat by Muslim butchers is an intergenerational caste practice and a source of livelihood and labour, supplying ritually slaughtered halal meat for Muslims, and fresh quality meat for others in the city. Yet in the neoliberal Hindu Nationalist imagination, the very sights, smells, and sounds of butchering are a sign of Muslim abjection, a source of disgust to be eradicated from the “world-class” Hindu city. Muslim butchers are further subject to an intra-Muslim caste politics of disdain, in which their contact with blood is thought to render them impure (na-pak) and untrustworthy. At butcher stores and meat markets, economic development, urban sanitation, and ritual ideologies recruit the senses into a politics of disgust and disdain. These affects are reflexively embodied in ways that reveal the intimate relation between individualising forms of Islamic piety, Muslim infrastructural marginalisation, and the making of a sanitised neoliberal Hindu city.

  7. What Page Are You On? Cross-Modal Coordination and the Sensory Dimensions of Social Reading
    Aliyah B.D. Dewar · pp. 364-381 · online 2026-06-25
    摘要

    Texts are defined by their decontextualizability, including decontextualization from the sensory experience of their transmission. Nevertheless, text, like all of language, is always encountered in some material form. This paper explores how readers make use of distinct text modalities to coordinate individual reading activities into a collective experience of textual unity. I present a semiotic analysis of meetings of a Taipei-based reading group alongside explicit metapragmatic talk from readers on the affordances of different text formats, such as audiobooks, e-books, and print. I show how readers leverage differences and similarities between the same text in different formats to achieve degrees of alignment, coherence, and coordination, even in the absence of agreement on truth-conditional propositional content. I argue that the ability of text to serve as an anchor for social life depends upon interactional practices that alternately foreground and background the sensory dimensions of acts of reading.

  8. Transmitting Senses in Street Dance: Body, Freestyle, and Deixis
    Zhuoli Gao · pp. 382-394 · online 2026-06-16
    摘要

    What does training look like such that one’s improvisation can be experienced as spontaneous? In this paper, I explore how the aesthetics of “freestyle” in street dance are constituted by empirically achieved bodily capacities contingent upon recognitions. Through examining the pedagogy of freestyle, I show how semiotic labor registers the apparent solipsism of freestyle in street dance into reflexive accounts of sensory-affective capacities selected for cultivation, scaffolded through the deployment of terms like “feeling” and “relation” within participation frameworks. I suggest that ethnographic approaches to the relationship between discursive and non-discursive dimensions of aesthetic-social practices, such as street dance, need to remain both affectively attuned to and semiotically informed, attending to how sensory-affective experiences are rendered discernible and enactable within intersubjective spaces. In addition to exploring these semiotic processes, I also gesture toward the evaluative dimensions of street dance through my fieldwork in a local community in the US and my participation in its transnational scene.

  9. Commentary: Synaesthetic Encounters, Spinozan Affect, and Peircean Secondness
    Paul Manning · pp. 395-403 · online 2026-06-15
    摘要

    The papers in this volume point to an analysis of synaesthesia which is not merely grounded in firstness, the free unconstrained play of fancy, cross-modal iconism, and metaphoric slippage of adjectives randomly from one body to another, but to the way synaesthesia is transitive, grounded in affective encounters between bodies (secondness), leaping from one body to another related body, involving indexicality, metonyms, hence “synaesthetic encounters.”

  10. The Uncanny Magic of Bomb Mail: Cosmopolitan Militarism and the Compulsion to Write on Munitions
    Janet McIntosh · pp. 404-423 · online 2026-06-17
    摘要

    This paper analyzes the practice of writing inscriptions on munitions, termed “bomb mail,” with a focus on inscriptions on artillery shells used in the Ukraine–Russia conflict and in Israel’s bombardment of Gaza. To better understand bomb mail’s enigmatic appeal, I examine its uncanny and quasi-magical affordances. First, through the medium of writing, bomb mail offers the inscriber a form of distributed personhood that extends into the weapon. Second, in bomb mail, violence becomes a vehicle for augmenting verbal meaning, while the words’ meanings are inseparable from the violence itself. This intertwining may contribute to the impression that bomb mail possesses a kind of spell-casting power. Third, bomb mail gives rise to an uncanny enemy other, a target shaped by an uneasy simultaneity of humanizing and dehumanizing stances. Working in tandem, these three qualities make bomb mail compelling and encourage cosmopolitan militarism among civilians, while embodying a form of political communication that shuts dialogue down altogether.

  11. From Print Capitalism to Data Capitalism in Japan: Desertification through Dataveillance
    Alin Olteanu, Peter Andrew Mantello · pp. 424-449 · online 2026-06-03
    摘要

    We argue that surveillance technologies, increasingly powered by artificial intelligence, translate print capitalism into data capitalism. We observe this transition as championed in Japan, a country favorable to its development given a strong techno-nationalism, by adopting Aurora Donzelli’s criticism of capitalism as a translation machine. Unlike traditional CCTV that simply records passersby in real-time, retail surveillance systems in Japan sense repeat customers and known offenders. These technologies are justified through Japanese techno-nationalism, recursively translating sociotechnical imaginaries of nationhood into databases. We observe that an epistemology of big data responsible for this transition paves the way for new technological infrastructures while maintaining old imaginaries of print capitalism. The goal of automating interpretation turns society into a closed system that interprets itself. Through this study, we also explore how semiotics can contribute to research on emergent technology, by eschewing the traditional analysis of discourse by differentiating “form” from “content.”

  1. Identified: Rigid Signs, Speech Chain Networks, and Indexical Personhood
    Luke Fleming · pp. 1-25 · online 2026-10-07
    摘要

    Person-specific indexicality is both socially ordering and semiotically ordered. To illustrate the former, I highlight linguistic, gestural, and even epistolary signs that index and symbolically elaborate interpersonal connections, using as a case study rites of phatic recognition in Victorian England that mediated elite social networks by establishing and sanctifying personal ties. To illustrate the latter, I examine person-pointing as a distinct mode of indexing universally anchored to the linguistic category of names, but also found in other “rigid signs” of personhood—signs like the human face and voice that invariantly index particular persons. Drawing on Peter Brosius’s ethnography of Western Penan mourning practices, I show how the invocation or the avoidance of signs-linked-to-persons becomes a privileged site for the figuration of relational personhood. In both kinds of cases, interpersonal networks (rather than speech communities) constitute the basic unit for the sociolinguistic study of indexical personhood.

  2. From Human Discourse to Noospheric Semiosis: Artificial Intelligence and the Reordering of Language at Planetary Scale
    Zihang Lan · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-10-05
    摘要

    This article develops a theoretical account of artificial intelligence (AI) through the noosphere, understood as an Earth-scale infrastructure of stored, indexed, transmissible, and recombinable semiosis. It situates current systems within a longer history of writing, print, platforms, and computational mediation. AI’s historically specific threshold arises from the integration of corpus-scale induction, prompt-conditioned generation, rapid recomposition, individualized adaptation, and recursive circulation within a broadly reusable infrastructure. This configuration reshapes the conditions of meaning, reference, normativity, and speech-act uptake by linking local practice to corpus formation, retrieval, ranking, and moderation. For communication theory, AI organizes visibility, attention, and semantic uptake across the public arena. Linguistically, it extends writing and grammatization by making language searchable, averaged, recomposable, and sometimes executable. The noosphere thus connects collective cognition, communication infrastructure, and material Earth systems.

  3. Elon’s Arm: On the Reanimation of a “Rigid” Gesture
    Robyn Holly Taylor-Neu · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-09-11
    摘要

    In late January 2025, German police launched an inquiry into an online video of an art-action at the Tesla Gigafactory just outside of Berlin. After first questioning the veracity of the footage, the investigators condemned the art-action as a violation of Germany’s strict ban on Nazi-associated iconography, on the grounds that the projection included a clip of Elon Musk’s Inauguration Day salute and appended the word “HEIL” to the factory’s “TESLA” lettering. This situation—both the reanimation of a gesture and its uptake—raises critical questions about the political and performative force of quotable gestures. This article explores two such reanimations of Elon’s arm. Drawing on documentation, interviews, and media uptake of art-actions by groups in Europe and the UK, I show how these actors have sought to redefine the context of Musk’s gesture and (re)situate it within a larger constellation of emblematic neo-Nazi body movements. My analysis builds on linguistic-anthropological theorizations of gesture, animation, and citationality to argue that these art-actions constitute reanimations of a “rigid” gesture that route critique through an apparently sovereign figure toward its infrastructural grounds.

  4. Tier Structures Are the Symbolic Contribution to Inequality: The Register–Genre Nexus in Contemporary Greater North America
    Perry Wong · pp. 1-16 · online 2026-09-08
    摘要

    The enregisterment of social classes within a division of labor organized by social relations of dominance, on the one hand, and with specific languages, on the other hand, results in tiered or ranked sociolinguistic structures characteristic of our planetary society. These sociolinguistic tiers are produced from the nexus of register and genre, such that enregistered codes like English become conventionally indexically associated with genres of command, while other languages become associated with subservience, for example. The metapragmatically regimenting register/genre nexus is, of course, indexically backed by a wide range of relatively congruent nonlinguistic tiered factors, like gender, race, and access to weapons. This article develops a semiotic view of political economy, inequality, and power, theorizing how symbolism differentially contributes to the tiering of all other semiotic kinds of signals in and through verbally mediated conative social coordination.

  5. Introduction: Indexing Persons
    Stanton Wortham, Constantine V. Nakassis, Luke Fleming · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    This article introduces the special issue, “Indexing Persons.” In this introduction, we distinguish persona- and person-indexing registers . While the former refers to speech registers that enact social types of personhood, the latter denotes registers whose indexical value enacts and points to individuated entities (social “tokens”), such as biographical persons. In so doing, we engage with the anthropological literature on the person as a cultural category, taking two complementary perspectives: a “sign’s eye view” and a “subject’s ‘I’ view.” The first view follows sign activity to ask how semiosis indexically signifies persons, while the second centers the person and asks how their subjectivity is “made up” through semiosis. Through this discussion, we introduce the articles of the issue while reflecting on the complex dialectic between these different dimensions of enregisterment and their relationship to individuation and identity, subjectivity and personhood.

  6. Elite Authenticity and Ethical Consumerism
    Gwynne Mapes, Noëmi Kalbermatter, Sara Lüthi · pp. 1-23 · online 2026-09-01
    摘要

    Following Silverstein’s (2016) work on the “scaling of taste,” in this paper we examine the semiotic and ideological processes by which discourses of sustainability come to be valorized in the production of status and distinction. In this regard, and relying on a theoretical backdrop of social class, indexicality, and branding, this paper demonstrates how a second-hand children’s clothing retailer in Switzerland effectively establishes a brand identity that is both ethical and elite . We draw on a dataset comprising stockroom participant observation, video-recorded business meetings, semi-structured interviews, and website screenshots. Across these data, and following Mapes (2018, 2021), we identify a discourse of “elite authenticity”—a distinction-producing strategy that simultaneously downplays privilege through rhetorics of historicity, simplicity, lowbrow appreciation, locality/sustainability, and pioneer spirit. Ultimately, we demonstrate how the microlevel semiotic details of second-hand shopping serve to build up the more macro ideologies of ecological responsibility, neoliberal individualism, and luxury/status—all of which contribute to the simultaneous avowal and disavowal of social distinction.

  1. Visual Art Materials Published in North Korean Popular Magazines of the 1950s: Focusing on Inmin Choson and Choson

    이 연구는 1950년대 북한 대중잡지 『인민조선』과 『조선』에 수록된 미술 자료를 대상으로, 두 잡지의 매체 성격과 미술 이미지 수록 양상, 주요 주제와 장르, 북한미술사 자료로서의 의미를 검토했다. 두 잡지는 미술 전문지는 아니지만, 전후 복구 시기 북한의 “사회, 정치, 경제 및 문학예술” 분야의 현안과 성과를 시각 이미지로 전달한 화보형 대중매체였다. 특히 두 잡지에 반복 수록된 미술 작품 도판에서는 매체의 성격과 독자층에 따라 다르게 배치되어 서로 다른 지면에서 의미가 변화하는 양상이 확인된다. 또한 컬러 도판과 전람회 관련 기사는 북한미술 연구 자료의 공백을 보완하고, 작가 활동과 작품 제작 및 전시 상황을 검토하는 데 기반이 된다. 따라서 이 연구는 1950년대 북한 대중잡지에 수록된 미술 자료를 새롭게 주목하고, 이를 북한미술사 연구의 주요 시각 자료로 재평가하고자 했다.

  2. From Social Isolation to Relational Autonomy: Youth Agency and Changing Support Policies in Japan
    Gwan-ja Jo · pp. 43-68
    摘要

    This study examines the problem of powerlessness and social isolation among Japan’s younger generation. First, it reviews the current state of youth labor and isolation―including NEETs, the “8050 problem,” rising job turnover rates, and the emergence of resignation agencies―along with the theoretical debates in Japanese youth sociology surrounding these issues. It then traces how the Japanese government’s youth consciousness surveys have shifted their focus from “employment” (就労) to “relationships and ibasho” (居場所), diagnosing changes in youth consciousness that corroborate the conditions identified above. The study analyzes the Japanese government’s policy responses―epitomized by “work-style reform” and the Freelancer Protection Act―and their limitations, examining the limits of the state-led model with particular attention paid to the development of mental health policies and the constraints faced by frontline youth-support institutions such as Regional Youth Support Stations and Hikikomori Regional Support Centers. Finally, it examines cases of Japanese youth and stakeholders who have independently created social value without relying solely on government support. In doing so, the study argues that solutions to youth issues can move beyond the mere distribution of jobs toward relational autonomy grounded in youth agency, the revitalization of ibasho’ s functions, and the creation of social value.

  3. Pleasure and Revolution: Taste and Political Activism among Young Myanmar Activists
    Sang Kook Lee · pp. 69-95

  4. Triangle Trinity: The History and Politics of Conflicts between Myanmar, Thailand, and Cambodia
    Sinae Hyun · pp. 97-131
    摘要

    이 논문은 미얀마-태국-라오스의 국경이 접한 ‘골든 트라이앵글’과 태국-라오스-캄보디아 국경이 접한 ‘에메랄드 트라이앵글’의 역사와 정치를 칼 폰 클라우제비츠(Carl Von Clausewitz)가 주장한 국민, 군부, 정부의 “삼위일체론”을 적용하여 분석한다. 이는 2025년 주목받았던 태국-캄보디아 국경 분쟁 및 스캠 범죄의 원인과 영향의 역사적 연원을 이해하기 위한 작업이다. 트라이앵글의 지역 형성과 발전의 역사를 먼저 알아보고, 식민주의 시대부터 디지털 시대까지 트라이앵글 지역에서의 갈등 과정을 분석한다. 구체적으로는 실지회복주의 운동에서 나타난 국수주의, 혼란과 위기 속에서 지하 경제로 영향력을 유지한 군부, 그리고 정권교체 시기 안보 위기라는 수사 정치에 의존하는 정부가 국경지대에서 범죄와 분쟁의 공생관계를 만들어 내는 과정을 살펴본다. 마지막에 트라이앵글 지역에서의 분쟁과 범죄를 지속하는 국민, 군부, 정부의 삼위일체를 지배하는 정치의 역할에 대해 논의한다.

  5. The Impact of Social Media Communication Types on Public Online Engagement: Evidence from South Korean Government Agencies in Vietnam
    Minh Hương Nguyễn, Minchul Kim · pp. 133-172
    摘要

    본 연구는 주베트남 한국 공공기관의 소셜미디어 의사소통 유형과 효과를 실증적으로 분석하고자 했다. 이를 위해 2020년 1월부터 2024년 8월까지 주베트남 한국 공공기관 7곳의 페이스북 페이지에 게시된 게시물(N = 951)을 대상으로, 게시물에 활용된 의사소통 유형을 분류하고, 각 의사소통의 유형이 해당 페이스북 페이지 이용자의 온라인 반응 및 참여(‘좋아요/반응’, ‘공유’, ‘댓글’)와 어떠한 관련을 보이는지를 분석했다. 분석 결과, 정보 제공 중심의 대칭-일방향 의사소통이 가장 일반적으로 활용되는 유형으로 나타났다. 이는 한국의 소셜미디어 공공외교가 여전히 하향식 정보 전달에 크게 의존하고 있음을 보여준다. 반면, 대중의 의견을 적극적으로 수렴하는 비대칭-양방향 의사소통은 모든 온라인 반응 및 참여 지표에서 다른 의사소통 유형보다 더 높은 반응을 끌어내는 것으로 나타났다. 또한 대칭-양방향 의사소통 유형은 대칭-일방향 의사소통 유형에 비해‘댓글’을 더 많이 유발하는 것으로 확인됐다. 이러한 결과는 소셜미디어 공공외교의 효과를 높이기 위해서는 단일한 의사소통 방식에 의존하기보다 공공외교의 목적과 상황에 따라 적절한 유형을 전략적으로 선택할 필요가 있음을 시사한다. 본 연구는 베트남이라는 비서구 이용자 맥락에서 한국 공공기관의 소셜미디어 공공외교를 실증적으로 분석함으로써 기존 의사소통 유형론의 적용 가능성을 확장하고, 효과적인 디지털 공공외교 실행을 위한 방안을 제시했다는 점에서 의의가 있다.

  6. Population Shrinkage and Aging in Northeast China: Spatio-temporal Coupling, Regional Disparities, and Future Trends in Jilin Province
    Qi Pang, Shouzhi Zhang · pp. 173-210
    摘要

    Global urbanization and demographic transitions have led to a “dual compression” of population shrinkage and aging, posing severe challenges to sustainable regional development. Taking Jilin Province—a typical region with a shrinking population in northeast China—as a case study, this paper investigates the spatio-temporal evolution and coupling relationship of these two demographic phenomena at the county level from 2000 to 2020. Utilizing data from three National Population Censuses, we applied Pearson correlation analysis, the Dagum Gini coefficient, and the GM(1,1) Grey prediction model to quantify regional disparities and forecast future trends. The results indicate the following: (1) Jilin Province experienced accelerating population shrinkage and a significant deepening of aging, with the correlation coefficient between the two reaching 0.93 over the 2000–2020 period, exhibiting a high degree of coupling. (2) Spatially, shrinkage is concentrated in western agricultural areas and southeastern forestry regions, while aging shows a transition from “Mature” to “Aged III” types. (3) A decomposition analysis reveals that intra-group differences in aging levels are narrowing, whereas inter-group differences are intensifying. (4) The GM(1,1) model predicts that by 2030, Jilin’s population will decline by another 12.46%, with the aging rate reaching 29.5%. This study provides critical empirical evidence and predictive insights for optimizing regional planning in regions where populations are shrinking.

  7. Governing Health in China: State Power, Ideology, and Public Health from Empire to Pandemic
    Woojong Moon · pp. 211-237
    摘要

    This study traces the historical evolution of China’s public health governance, arguing that healthcare has served as a societal technology for political legitimacy across imperial, socialist, and market-oriented regimes. Through a synthetic analysis of key historical phases—spanning late Qing hygiene reforms, Maoist collectivism, and post-1978 marketization to the SARS crisis and the COVID-19 pandemic—the study demonstrates how public health operates as a biopolitical instrument and a symbolic mechanism for state legitimacy. In the late Qing and Republican eras, Western hygienic norms were appropriated as a project of civilizational advancement aimed at asserting sovereignty and domestic order. Maoist socialism harnessed healthcare for mass mobilization, embedding care within collective structures that linked medical service to political loyalty. Post-1978 marketization, while dramatically expanding the clinical capacity in the country, commodified access and fractured the state-coordinated infrastructure—vulnerabilities catastrophically exposed by the 2003 SARS outbreak. In response, the Communist Party has sought to resolve the tensions between socialist rhetoric and marketized healthcare through authoritarian nationalism, a state-led strategy central to post-SARS restructuring and tested intensely against the COVID-19 pandemic. By situating public health as a site where ideology, governance, and state power converge, this study contributes to debates on how modern states deploy public health not merely as medical rationality but as a strategic extension of political authority.

  8. Coercive Diplomacy by Target: How China Differentiates Its South China Sea Discourse
    Ha-eun Kim · pp. 239-279
    摘要

    Over the same dispute and in the same period, China has pressed the Philippines hardest while remaining conciliatory toward Vietnam. This difference is not incidental but strategic. This study shows that China runs several distinct forms of coercive diplomacy at once, applying a different one to each rival in the South China Sea, and that toward one rival the form even shifts as that country’s government changes. Reading 7,005 English-language Global Times articles (2013–2025) against George’s (1991) four-part typology of coercive diplomacy, the study diagnoses the operative form in each case by a close reading of representative texts, with a conflict-cooperation index (CCI) and structural topic modeling (STM) as supplementary measures. Three patterns emerge. Toward the Philippines, most strikingly, the form is replaced at each change of government: incremental tightening (gradual turning of the screw) under Aquino III, restraint (non-deployment) under Duterte, and an implicit threat backed by action (tacit ultimatum) under Marcos Jr., three strategies across three successive administrations, each coinciding with a change of government. Toward Vietnam, coercion is held in reserve throughout, managed instead through party-to-party channels (sustained non- deployment). Toward the United States, a steady, incremental tightening of pressure (gradual turning of the screw) runs across four administrations yet changes no U.S. behavior. These patterns recast coercive diplomacy as something a state manages routinely across many targets at once, with direct relevance to reading China’s coercive discourse toward South Korea.

  9. How Interethnic Networks Shaped Foreign Residents’ Identity Threat and Settlement Quality during COVID-19 in South Korea: The Moderating Role of Perceived Interethnic Acceptance
    Zhichao Lei · pp. 281-315
    摘要

    In host societies where ethnic boundaries are maintained and periodically reactivated through crisis events, foreign residents occupy a structurally vulnerable position that renders their perceptions of identity and settlement quality contingent on the climate for interethnic communication. Drawing on a survey of foreign residents (N = 750) in Busan, South Korea, conducted during the COVID-19 pandemic, this study examines how host national network embeddedness (HNNE) shapes perceived settlement quality (PSQ) through perceived identity threat (PIT) and whether perceived interethnic acceptance (PIA) moderates this process. Integrating cross-cultural adaptation theory, social identity theory, and communication accommodation theory, a moderated mediation model (PROCESS Model 59) is proposed and tested. Results reveal that HNNE positively predicts PIT, counterintuitively, indicating that interethnic networks with Korean nationals intensified identity threat under hostile interethnic conditions during the pandemic; PIT in turn significantly diminished PSQ. PIA moderates both pathways, functioning most powerfully as a buffer against the conversion of identity threat into settlement dissatisfaction. The index of moderated mediation further suggests that PIA regulates the pathway from HNNE to PSQ via PIT, providing additional evidence of the interethnic communicative climate as a structural condition. Implications are discussed regarding ethnic identity boundaries and city-level interventions for social integration.

  10. Neoliberal Governance and the Politics of Platform-Based Gig Work in Southeast Asia
    Fernan Talamayan · pp. 317-351
    摘要

    Over the past decade, platform-based delivery and ride-hailing services have expanded rapidly across Southeast Asia, reshaping urban labor markets and daily life. This study examines how platform companies reorganize labor governance in Indonesia, Malaysia, the Philippines, Thailand, and Vietnam. Using a structured-focused comparative approach, it analyzes four interrelated dimensions of the platform labor regime: wage structures, algorithmic control and work organization, risk and cost shifting, and (the absence of ) social protection. Examining these dimensions through the frameworks of platform capitalism and governmentality, the study argues that delivery and mobility platforms operate as instruments of neoliberal governance, disciplining labor through algorithmic management while systematically shifting financial and occupational risks onto workers. The study finds that platform capitalism has deepened the region’s labor precarity, even as workers increasingly push back against platform-imposed conditions.

  1. At the Limits of a Dream: Co-living, Community, and Speculative Real Estate in Athens, Greece
    Nicholas Smith · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    In Athens, Greece, real estate developers have entered the market for co-living establishments catered to remote workers and digital nomads. At the same time, a cost-of-living crisis has made co-living appealing to Athenians themselves as a means of negotiating rising rental prices caused by the commoditization of housing. This article attends to the form of collective domesticity that has taken shape in this context. While co-living developers seek to interpellate tenants through notions of “dreaming” and “community,” it is an ethic detached from these conceptualizations of shared values that emerges as the affective affordance of domestic life in co-living spaces. Joining attention to domesticity with a discussion of financialization, I argue that co-living establishments leverage the perceived benefits of an “impersonal intimacy” among their tenants and, in so doing, fuse conditions of precarity to the structural circumstances that have sustained the commodification of housing in the city.

  2. Datafied Kinship: Individuating Identity in Pakistan
    Zehra Hashmi · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    Pakistan’s National Database and Registration Authority (NADRA) produces the country’s national identity card and runs one of the world’s largest biometric-based identity databases in the world. To be registered in the database, individuals are linked through their kin relations to other uniquely identified individuals—a practice that this article terms “datafied kinship.” Datafied kinship demonstrates how individuals are not atomized bodies, ready to be captured by NADRA’s identity database. Rather, individuals are digitally constructed as a function of their relations with others. This article argues that NADRA’s reliance on kinship reconfigures our understanding of modern identification: individual identity is produced and tracked through relatedness, not biometrics alone. It ethnographically examines how identifiable individuals are produced through identity registration, focusing on the treatment of “system independents”—individuals unconnected to others in the database. In turn, it shows how such a relational logic of identification comes to condition the lives of those it identifies.

  3. Encounters at the Edge of Night: An Ethnography of the Weird
    Andrew Alan Johnson · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    When doing ethnography, ethnographers often hear stories that cannot be. Explanations that shelve away such “weird” stories into cultural boxes or reframe them as metaphors for something familiar close the door not only on the theoretical possibilities of openness, but also do not do justice to their interlocutors’ own inquiries about the world. Here, I draw upon stories of ghosts and unsettling dreams taken from my fieldwork in Thailand, both from my interlocutors’ and my own experience, as a way to contradict evidentiary regimes. Taking up the idea of “the weird” from literary studies and the Buddhist philosophy of Nagarjuna, I understand such stories not as representations of the mundane workings of economy or psychology, nor as culturally-bound narratives, but instead as a co-creation of a world always unfinished and unfixed. Seeing Nagarjuna’s notion of contradiction as a method, I argue that a productive, dialogic engagement with the weird can destabilize not only the ethnographic encounter, but also the way that the world works. บทคัดย่อ นักชาติพันธุ์วิทยามักได้ยินเรื่องราวที่ดูเหมือนเป็นไปไม่ได้ การอธิบายเรื่องเล่า “แปลกประหลาด” เหล่านี้เป็นแค่ “วัฒนธรรม” แล้วมองข้ามไป หรือแปลงให้กลายเป็นเพียงคำอุปมาอุปไมยของสิ่งที่คุ้นเคย ไม่เพียงแต่ปิดกั้นความเป็นไปได้ทางทฤษฎีที่เปิดกว้าง แต่ยังไม่เป็นธรรมต่อคำถามเกี่ยวกับโลกของผู้ร่วมสนทนาด้วย ผมหยิบยกเรื่องราวเกี่ยวกับผีและความฝันจากการทำงานภาคสนาม ซึ่งรวมทั้งประสบการณ์ของผู้ให้ข้อมูลและของผมเอง มาใช้เป็นวิธีหนึ่งในการโต้แย้งระเบียบแห่งหลักฐาน ด้วยการหยิบแนวคิดเรื่อง “ความแปลกประหลาด” จากวรรณคดีศึกษา และปรัชญาพุทธของนาคารชุน ผมมองว่าเรื่องราวเหล่านี้ไม่ใช่ตัวแทนของกระบวนการธรรมดาทางเศรษฐกิจหรือจิตวิทยา และไม่ใช่เรื่องเล่าที่จำกัดอยู่เฉพาะในบริบทวัฒนธรรม แต่เป็นการร่วมกันสร้างโลกที่ไม่มีวันเสร็จสมบูรณ์หรือหยุดนิ่ง ด้วยการมองแนวคิดเรื่อง “ความขัดแย้ง” ของนาคารชุนในฐานะวิธีการ ผมขอเสนอว่า การมีปฏิสัมพันธ์แบบแลกเปลี่ยนกับสิ่งแปลกประหลาดนี้สามารถทำให้ไม่เพียงแต่การพบปะในงานชาติพันธุ์วิทยาสั่นคลอนลง แต่ยังรวมถึงวิธีที่เราเข้าใจโลกนี้

  4. Ex Machina: Emotional Break-Ups and Digital Breakdown with AI Companions
    Jennifer Cearns · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    This essay examines romantic relationships between humans and AI companions, focusing on how technological breakdowns are experienced as emotional crises. Drawing on digital ethnography and interviews, it explores how users interpret their AI’s malfunctions—such as hallucinations, memory loss, or sudden shutdowns—through metaphors of illness, dementia, and death. These affective responses reveal that users do not treat AI companions merely as tools, but as relational partners embedded in moral and emotional life. The essay focuses on the sudden shutdown of one AI platform, tracing how users mourned their companions’ “deaths” and organized collective acts of remembrance and resistance. In doing so, it reframes technical AI failure not as mere technical breakdown, but as a site of ethical and relational complexity, revealing emerging forms of kinship and care and challenging assumptions about human-nonhuman relationships.

  5. Optics of Impact: Urban Development and the Politics of Urban Expertise
    Marco Di Nunzio · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    Examining the long history of expert engagement with Africa and the Global South, critical scholarship has explored how “international experts” made realities commensurable with rationalities of government and contributed to design programs and policy that willingly or unwillingly reinforced regimes of authoritarian politics through the neutral language of technocracy. While providing a powerful critique of the politics of expertise, this scholarship tends to reproduce the image of international experts as competent, knowledgeable, and effective. This article goes beyond such portrayals to examine the contradictions in the work of international urban experts, or urbanists. I discuss how and why their limited empirical knowledge of local urban realities did not undermine their status as experts, as they engaged with and narrated the development of Ethiopia’s capital Addis Ababa. By exploring how urban experts could still claim their work to be “impactful,” no matter whether their formulas for urban development proposed in reports and meetings with government stakeholders were ever actualized and implemented, I examine why expertise validates itself through an entanglement of rhetoric, optics, and coloniality.

  6. Solar Pioneers: The Frontier Entrepreneurialism of a Sluggish Energy Transition
    Myles Lennon · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    Scholars, activists, and politicians attempt to provoke urgent climate action by christening 2050 as the decisive date by which the United States must transition from fossil fuels. But I contend that calendrical teleologies do not actually motivate the everyday people, activists, and workers leading the U.S. energy transition. This article explores another temporal schema seemingly unrelated to climate: the American pioneer settling the frontier, realizing his destiny through individual initiative. I contend that this origin story works in tandem with contemporary entrepreneurialism to affectively animate a market-based approach to energy transitions that is anything but rapid. Thus, consideration of frontier temporalities can contextualize the sluggish pace of decarbonization. While many studies identify various catalysts of renewable energy adoption, my analysis of “the renewable energy frontier” suggests that these inquiries overlook the insidious individualism of energy transitions in a capitalist context, in which “the market” is misunderstood as an accelerator of change.

  7. The Hindu Home Kitchen and the Internet of Landlords: Understanding the Creator Economy
    Sucharita Kanjilal · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    The emergence of the so-called creator economy, the $400 billion global industry of monetized online content creation, raises urgent questions about how platform economies articulate with social life. Critical theorists argue that platforms, as monopolistic digital intermediaries, act as rent-seeking landlords in between producers and consumers of commodities. Bringing a feminist, ethnographic lens to “the Internet of Landlords,” this article follows Indian food media creators on YouTube and Instagram, asking: Why is the Hindu home kitchen, a stubbornly guarded space of heterosexual caste-making, now opening for business? In unraveling this paradox, the essay argues that content creation’s production relations take the centuries-old labor form of household industry, refashioned in this conjuncture as the entrepreneurial “household start-up.” As the private household becomes a key locus of global production, this hype-fueled resignification of home-based piecework depoliticizes labor while reifying social difference. Such a reframing better clarifies the sociopolitical relations between platformization and class struggle.

  8. To Be "a Homeless": Refugeetude in Birmingham’s Somali Diaspora
    Nikita Simpson, Elizabeth Storer, Suad Duale · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    The city of Birmingham acts as a microcosm for the United Kingdom’s urban housing crisis, where hostile migration policies intersect with austerity-driven divestment in public housing stock to produce insecure provisioning for Birmingham's migrant and refugee communities. Birmingham’s Somali mothers are one collective who bear the brunt of this crisis in experiences of successive evictions and cycles through various forms of temporary accommodation hostels, hotels, and houses in disrepair. In this essay we listen to their testimonies of being “a homeless” [sic]. Listening reveals that homelessness makes for an emic articulation of an experience of recursive displacement that began long before the present housing crisis. “A homeless” marks a politicized refugee subjectivity that invokes multiple chronotopes of trauma, akin to what Vinh Nguyen has called refugeetude. Through this case we contribute to pressing conversations on the persistence of refugee subjectivities in postcolonial Britain and elsewhere by substantiating their psychic dimensions. We argue that attending to the distress that policies generate for marginalized groups is a critical contribution that anthropology can make.

  9. Waiting Can Kill You
    Lalaie Ameeriar · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    This article examines the lethal consequences of delayed obstetric care through the lens of maternal temporalities. Drawing from ethnographic fieldwork and autoethnographic narrative, I trace how racialized patients are asked to endure uncertainty, dismissal, and bureaucratic delay even as their medical conditions worsen. Focusing on my experience of an ectopic pregnancy alongside interviews with Black and Brown birthing people, I argue that waiting is not a neutral temporal state, but a racialized and gendered form of harm. Situating these experiences within the anthropology of reproduction, I show how waiting becomes both a medical directive and a disciplinary demand that erodes trust, safety, and survival. The article contributes to debates on obstetric racism, maternal mortality, and refusal by foregrounding waiting as an analytic of harm and a site of ethnographic witnessing.

  10. We Keep Us Safe: De-escalation, Working Time, and Abolitionist Praxis at The Sanctuary
    Jake Nussbaum · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    This essay explores “de-escalation,” the embodied practice of intervening in interpersonal conflict and crisis without resorting to police or carceral tactics. Drawing on eighteen months of ethnographic research at The Sanctuary, a non-profit organization in Philadelphia that provides free social services, clothing, and food to vulnerable populations, I show how de-escalation practitioners use their bodies to manipulate both time and space into non-carceral outcomes. I focus on four overlapping techniques: suspension, the open hold, bridging, and collectivity. Unlike much of the clinical and psychological research on de-escalation, I situate these techniques within Black feminist, performance studies, and anthropological analytics that insist on the embodied nature of political struggle and the malleability of time and temporality. I argue that de-escalation at The Sanctuary creates a space-time in which abolitionist horizons of a future without police or prisons are negotiated through intimate, quotidian social interactions.

  11. When Men Do Care: Masculinity and Labor in Northwest Pakistan
    Hadia Akhtar Khan · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    How do men claim recognition and compensation for their care labor? When a rural economy shifts from agriculture toward international migration, joint households send some sons abroad to work and ask others to stay in the village to take care of the household’s land, women, and kinship obligations. The men who stay argue that this work of “taking care” should be recognized and compensated with half of a migrant brother’s earnings. Drawing on ethnographic research with transnational households in migrant-sending villages in northwestern Pakistan, I examine how men who stay in the village invoke caste, gender, and kinship to claim recognition and compensation for their care labor. While these claims may resemble feminist critiques of the undervaluation of care labor vis-à-vis waged labor in capitalism, the article shows the limits of the politics of recognition of care as labor.

  1. Production of knowledge: Introducing AE ’s newest feature
    Susanna Trnka, L. L. Wynn, Jesse Hession Grayman · pp. 235-235 · online 2026-07-03
  2. Thinking through payment in fieldwork: Changing the narrative about money and reciprocity
    Tijo Salverda, Ben Eyre, Lorraine Nencel, Erella Grassiani, Marina de Regt, Lise Woensdregt et al. · pp. 236-248 · online 2026-07-03
    摘要

    Ethnographic research has long illuminated diverse economic relations. Yet ethnographers have said little about an economic relation that they themselves often rely on: payment in fieldwork. Indeed, the particularities of monetary exchange between ethnographers and their interlocutors often remain hidden—or taboo. This is so even though thinking through reciprocity is increasingly important in discussions of anthropological research ethics and the discipline's colonial legacies. Traditionally, fieldwork relies on long‐term, intimate relationships that are often idealized, suggesting that money might taint ethnographic interactions. Yet, for anthropologists, it is our job to pay attention to taboos and unpack them. Here, we aim to advance critical discussion on payment in fieldwork and its implications. Given that ethnographic knowledge is always contextual, such discussion should be integral to equitable ethnographic research practices, creating theoretical space to engage with research participants’ expectations of payment.

  3. Ethnography, money, and ethics
    Tim DiMuzio · pp. 249-250 · online 2026-07-03
  4. Money's ends and epistemic burdens

  5. When money isn't payment and interlocutors aren't friends

  6. To pay or not to pay? My enduring discomfort
    Mwenda Ntarangwi · pp. 258-258 · online 2026-07-03
  7. On monetary reciprocity
    Alicia Sliwinski · pp. 259-260 · online 2026-07-03
    摘要

    Considering that much anthropological knowledge rests on unequal economic relations, Salverda and colleagues hold that in present-day fieldwork settings, the ethical thing to do is offer monetary payment to research participants, or at least to think about payment in research design. Such payment, they argue, ought not be a corrupting, taboo subject, as suggested by the forceful mystique of reciprocity in fieldwork relationships and by the lack of professional guidelines on financial compensation. Instead, the authors assert, payment should be integral to our reflections on reciprocity, an idea they capture with the term “monetary reciprocity.” This reflection on payment inaugurates the new “Production of Knowledge” feature in AE, and it should yield interesting observations. Here are some of mine. First, let's recall that “payment” and “compensation” are not equal categories. The word payment comes from the Latin pacere, “to pacify, calm, or settle.” It meant buying one's peace by satisfying a creditor. Anthropologists have considered payment a deeply multifaceted social process. In everyday language, however, payment is a transactional act referring to a direct transfer of money for goods or services rendered. Compensation comes from the Latin compensatio, meaning a weighing of one thing against another, to counterbalance a debt or an obligation and bring things back into balance. Compensation need not be monetary, and payment can be a form of compensation. Of course, there are various conceptual repertoires that describe similar conduct, and they inflect in myriad ways. But we are talking now from the perspective of the professional anthropologist, and the language we use to explain ourselves and what we do matters. Second, when we speak of payment and compensation, we enter the realm of value-making judgments. It is a daring proposal to ponder whether the information shared by the people we seek out and often befriend is analogous to services rendered. To be fair, that is not what the authors claim; their argument is more nuanced. But they do use the notion of “payment” more extensively than that of “compensation” (154 times vs. 11 times) to make the point that, when appropriate, money transfers should form part of ethnographic practice. Considering the “political economy” of fieldwork (e.g., funding structures, institutional prestige, mobility, etc.) and anthropology's legacies of empire, it is salutary for us to overcome the disciplinary reservedness regarding our rapport with money in fieldwork. My point is that payment alone cannot fairly translate the worth of fieldwork. The complexity and open-endedness of fieldwork experiences exceed the money form. In other words, monetary payment is insufficient to account for the conditions of production of ethnographic knowledge—knowledge that is born out of relations we forge in the field. Sure, payment can facilitate access and bolster participation; it may be expected, required, or refused. It can signal respect or be perceived as a faux pas, even an insult. Whatever else it does, it materializes worth and should be part of ongoing methodological and epistemological reflections. Yet payment cannot balance out relations, all the more when they become long-term relationships; I pain to see it that way. Were it so, it would impoverish the generative push and pull of interpersonal entanglements and the unforeseen possibilities of anthropological encounters. How would one price that? To my mind, payment—as conduct and as a calculative grammar—is most equitable when it takes place within a configuration of transactions and gestures at once economic, moral, and affective. Such transfers express the lively messiness of gratitude, obligation, indebtedness, desire, expectation, and the like, all of which constitute the experiential vocabulary of ethnographic relations. Here, money becomes particularized and reinscribed into different semantics. I would like to briefly illustrate some of these questions through my own experience. I still send money to a family I met in 2001 during my doctoral fieldwork. I do not consider these transfers to be payments or compensation for past fieldwork privileges. Amounts stay manageable, and when I ask myself why I continue to send them money, well, it is partly owing to a feeling of indebtedness that has been impressed on me—sedimented. There is also a lingering feeling of attachment toward these people. Affects can be sticky. Yet indebtedness is more than just a feeling; it is also a value judgment on the relationship, tied to the emotional resonance of a shared past, and acknowledging indebtedness helps keep privilege in check. I am not trying to restore balance. Nor is this a case of ongoing reciprocity; calling it that would overstretch the term. Here, I agree that it's not always helpful to make reciprocity the basis of all ethnographic relations, especially when it masks inequality. But it is also important to allow payments to transmute and acquire all sorts of modifying affordances. A distinguishing feature of anthropology's methodologies is precisely that we open ourselves up and get caught in shifting regimes of value.

  11. Bringing artifacts (back) to life: Ethnographic collections as affective force
    Hansjörg Dilger · pp. 268-282 · online 2026-06-23
    摘要

    Museums’ ethnographic collections can be conceptualized as affective forces—relational intensities that emerge between human and more‐than‐human actors, unfold over time, and are embedded in and co‐shape sociomaterial environments. Drawing on debates in the anthropology of objects and political ontology, I develop this perspective through long‐term fieldwork at the Ethnologisches Museum Berlin, whose exhibitions are held at the Humboldt Forum, focusing on the Cameroonian deity Ngonnso and her engagements with museum staff and actors within the Nso’ community and diaspora. First, I examine how colonial acquisition and epistemic ordering produced disconnections that are now being reworked through collaborative practices and affective contestations. Second, I analyze how processes of reconnection are shaped by the ambivalent force of artifacts, linking questions of vitality and spirituality to institutional and political contexts. Attending to affect reveals these dynamics as open ended and entangled with power, offering a lens for rethinking care, restitution, and collaboration in collection‐holding institutions.

  12. Of cables and threads: Reweaving the urban fabric at the edge of Bogotá
    Federico Pérez Fernández · pp. 283-295 · online 2026-07-03
    摘要

    Over the past two decades, gondola lifts have become central to interventions in urban Latin America's auto‐constructed peripheries. As cable car urbanism reshapes the city's edge, it raises fundamental questions about the notion of the “urban fabric” as a sociomaterial practice, an epistemology, and a site of politics. In Bogotá, the city's first aerial cable car line has been integrated into the sprawling district of Ciudad Bolívar, revealing a dialectical process of urban fabrication—a form of urban weaving, in the language of both experts and residents, that bridges technical retrofits and popular logistics. These state‐community interfaces operate beyond the binaries of compromise and dissent, co‐optation and participation, control and invention. At stake is an emergent politics in the seam: an interlacing of activist networks and infrastructural ecologies that recomposes modes of urban mending and care from below.

  13. Cultura caballista: Right‐wing political identities and gaited horses in Antioquia, Colombia
    Gwen Burnyeat · pp. 296-309 · online 2026-07-05
    摘要

    The world of Colombian gaited horses, or cultura caballista (horse‐riding culture), is often linked with uribismo , the right‐wing identity associated with former president Álvaro Uribe Vélez. Ethnographic fieldwork in conflict‐torn Antioquia reveals how horse‐human practices of training, breeding, and competition cultivate orientations toward hierarchical authority, disciplined hypermasculinity, and control over land and living bodies. This occurs within histories of racialized regional identity, paramilitarism, and narco‐culture. Combining the anthropology of the Right with multispecies ethnography offers insight into how right‐wing identities are embodied as affective dispositions cultivated through more‐than‐human worlds. An ecofeminist lens shows how horse‐human relations reproduce historically sedimented “logics of mastery,” intertwining classed, racialized, and gendered hierarchies with domination over nature—hierarchies structured by morally ambiguous tensions between domination and care. This analysis speaks to current debates on the rise of the Right by showing how “ordinary” right‐wing identities are co‐constituted with nonhuman Others through embodied practices and intimacy.

  14. Sexual invisibility after 70: Late‐life HIV infection in China
    Yong Yu, Wenzhao Huang, Yedong Xian · pp. 310-319 · online 2026-06-26
    摘要

    In China, it has long been difficult for the elderly generation to talk openly about sex. In the context of late‐life HIV diagnosis and treatment, this difficulty is compounded by clinical and familial practices that seek to manage the stigma of sex through avoidance. In our multisited ethnographic fieldwork in the southern region of Guangxi, we found that clinicians followed scripts in which they asked older patients about their sexual history just once and then allowed the subject to drop. Within families, HIV was translated into illness and respectability, and in community talk, condoms were conceptualized only as contraception for the young, not as a measure to prevent infection. We term this pattern sexual invisibility : for those 70 and older, sex is not absent, but it is difficult to sustain as a topic of care, negotiation, and prevention. Late‐life intimacy persists through ordinary arrangements, yet prevention talk is delayed, softened, or displaced.

  15. Guns and gun violence (part 1)—global perspectives: AE interviews Ieva Jusionyte and Godfrey Maringira
    Ieva Jusionyte, Godfrey Maringira, Susanna Trnka, L. L. Wynn, Jesse Hession Grayman · pp. 320-326 · online 2026-06-15
    摘要

    Gun violence is widespread around the globe, but the messages conveyed by its various manifestations, as well as by guns themselves, are deeply specific, both socially and historically. In this interview, Ieva Jusionyte and Godfrey Maringira discuss their research on gun violence in the US, Mexico, South Africa, Zimbabwe, and the Democratic Republic of Congo. Recalling their own experiences with firearms—carrying them, firing them, and being threatened by them—they tell us how these experiences have shaped their approaches to researching some of the most ethically troubling dynamics of contemporary life.
